# PCP-W Pre-writes — Criminal Litigation and Family Law Practice

Paper `pcp-w`, PCP written (PCP Paper 1), Mon 7 Dec 2026, 2:30–4:30pm. Subject 0 Criminal
Litigation Practice (Q1–Q2, about 60 marks); Subject 1 Family Law Practice (Q3–Q6, about 40–50
marks). Drafting Templates are in `templates.md`.

**How to use in the exam.** Each Topic (`0.I`, `0.II` … `1.VIII`) opens with a **Cheatsheet**:
bold questions a set of facts makes you ask, broad to specific, with short answers underneath.
Each answer line ends with a link such as `0-13`, which jumps to the Pre-write `pcp-w-0-13`. If
you know a keyword (a section, a case, a phrase like "qualified plea", "Newton", "care and
control", "PPO"), Ctrl-F it: every Cheatsheet line, title and Keywords line is text. Sections are
written `s 228(4) CPC`, `s 95A(2) WC`; search `228(4)` if the full form fails.

**Pre-writes** are I-CRUPAC paragraphs to adapt and retype. The bold labels (**I**, **C**, **R**,
**P**, **A**, **C**) are for finding your place only — don't type them. Swap the square-bracket
placeholders for the facts. The **Rule** part is short enough to retype; the **Rule in full** box
under it sets out the rule limb by limb, for checking and for picking the limbs the facts engage.
The **Application** part is a pattern, not a worked answer. The *Source note* is for checking
only; aliases are defined in `coverage.md` (Source key). Course Materials win; where a senior's
note differs, the Source note says so in a `Conflict:` sentence.

**Law as at.** The slides apply the CPC as amended in 2025 (Kadar and Nabill codified), the new
Part 7 of the Women's Charter (family violence, from 2 Jan 2025), the Women's Charter divorce
provisions with s 95A (six facts, including mutual agreement) and the Family Justice (General)
Rules 2024 (from 15 Oct 2024: "Applicant/Respondent", "originating application for divorce"
instead of "Plaintiff/Defendant", "writ"). Past papers before 2025 use the old terms and section
numbers (e.g. PPO under old s 65); answer on the current law.

**Answer shape for a criminal question:** (1) where the case is (investigation, charged, PG or
trial, sentenced) and what the client wants; (2) the CPC section or rule, with its limbs; (3) the
cases on how it is applied; (4) apply to the facts, flagging what more you need from the client;
(5) conclude with the practical next step (what to file or write, by when) and any ethical limit
(PCR r 14) on what you may do.

**Answer shape for a family question:** (1) the remedy and the court's power (WC section); (2)
the test, limb by limb; (3) the cases; (4) apply to the facts, noting evidence the client needs;
(5) conclude, with the realistic outcome and the therapeutic-justice point (mediation,
co-parenting, the child's welfare) where it fits.

**Which Pre-write?** *Criminal:* client before charge 0-1, after charge 0-2, own statements or
seized documents 0-3, client confesses or changes story 0-4; bail 0-5 to 0-7; representations,
warnings, composition, plea offers 0-8 to 0-11; PG valid 0-12, qualified plea 0-13, SOF 0-14,
Newton 0-15, TIC 0-16, antecedents 0-17, mitigation 0-18; CCDC 0-19 to 0-21, Kadar 0-22, Nabill
0-23, breach 0-24; trial 0-25, adverse inference 0-26, no case 0-27, amend charge 0-28, defence
0-29, recall 0-30, impeach 0-31, statement admissibility 0-32, judgment 0-33; sentencing 0-34 to
0-37 (consecutive/concurrent 0-36); appeals 0-38 to 0-43. *Family:* TJ 1-1, mediation 1-2, offer
of amicable resolution 1-3; CPP 1-4, jurisdiction 1-5, procedure 1-6, no-divorce relief 1-7;
nullity 1-8 to 1-9; divorce 1-10 to 1-16 (adultery 1-12, behaviour 1-13); children 1-17 to 1-23
(custody 1-18, care and control 1-19, access 1-20, relocation 1-22); maintenance 1-24 to 1-27;
assets 1-28 to 1-37 (ANJ 1-33, disclosure 1-37); PPO 1-38 to 1-41.

---

## 0.I Taking instructions and preliminary advice

- **Client is being investigated but not yet charged — what do I advise?**
  - Take full instructions; explain the s 22 long statement and that admissions in it can found a charge and a conviction → [0-1](#pcp-w-0-1)
  - Client wants to lie to the police, delete CCTV, hide evidence, or use a "fake" story: offences under ss 182, 201, 204A PC; I must not concoct evidence (PCR r 9(2)(g)–(h)) → [0-1](#pcp-w-0-1)
  - Will be charged soon: arrange a bailor to attend court → [0-1](#pcp-w-0-1), [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)
- **Client has been charged — first steps?**
  - Examine the charge and its elements; explain s 22 and s 23 (cautioned) statements → [0-2](#pcp-w-0-2)
  - Possible defences: general exceptions (accused proves, s 107 EA), alibi (notice under s 278 CPC) → [0-2](#pcp-w-0-2)
  - Must advise whether to plead guilty or claim trial; the decision is the client's (r 14(5)) → [0-2](#pcp-w-0-2)
- **Client wants his phone, documents, in-car camera or own statement**
  - Seized items he owned or controlled: write to AGC under *Goldring*; no right to third-party CCTV → [0-3](#pcp-w-0-3)
  - Copy of his own statement: CFP or PSB under the CCDC (opt in if Magistrate's Court) → [0-3](#pcp-w-0-3), [0-20](#pcp-w-0-20)
- **Ethics: client confesses to me / says "I'm sorry" then "I'm innocent" / do I believe him?**
  - Confession: may continue to act but must not run anything inconsistent with it (r 14(4)) → [0-4](#pcp-w-0-4)
  - My belief in guilt is irrelevant; I must not let it affect the defence (r 14(3)(b)); take instructions carefully, record them → [0-4](#pcp-w-0-4)
  - No promises ("I'll get the charge reduced"); attendance notes; interview witnesses separately, client absent → [0-4](#pcp-w-0-4)

### pcp-w-0-1 — Advising a client before charge: statements, false information, destroying evidence
*Keywords: before charge, police investigation, police interview, long statement, s 22 CPC, s 182 PC, false information to public servant, s 200 PC, s 201 PC, conceal evidence, s 204A PC, obstruct justice, destroy evidence, delete CCTV, fake story, concoct evidence, PCR r 9(2)(g), PCR r 9(2)(h), bailor*

**I** The issue is how to advise [Client], who is being investigated by the [Police] for [offence] and has been asked to attend an interview, and whether [Client] may [give the account that X / have the CCTV deleted]. **C** [Client] must not [give a false account / destroy or hide evidence], which would be an offence, and I cannot assist; [Client] should give a truthful account or exercise the options I explain, mindful that admissions in the statement can be used against [him].
**R** A statement recorded during investigations under s 22 of the Criminal Procedure Code 2010 ("CPC") may be relied on to charge and later to convict if it contains admissions. Giving false information to a public servant is an offence under s 182 of the Penal Code 1871 ("PC"), and stating as true a declaration known to be false is an offence under s 200 PC. Concealing evidence or screening an offender is an offence under s 201 PC, and destroying evidence to obstruct justice an offence under s 204A PC. A legal practitioner must not concoct any evidence or contrive any fact (r 9(2)(g)–(h) Legal Profession (Professional Conduct) Rules 2015 ("PCR")).

> **Rule in full**
> - Advice before charge (CL1 s5–7):
>   - obtain a thorough understanding of the allegations and the client's position (admits or denies);
>   - if the client denies, take a detailed account and identify evidence to verify it; preserve evidence;
>   - find out the client's connections with the complainant, Prosecution witnesses and co-accused;
>   - explain the importance of s 22 (long) statements: admissions may found a charge and a conviction.
> - Do not advise the client to:
>   - give false information (s 182 PC; s 200 PC);
>   - destroy evidence (obstruction of justice, s 204A PC);
>   - conceal evidence or screen anyone from punishment (s 201 PC).
> - PCR r 9(2): a legal practitioner must not (g) concoct any evidence, or (h) contrive any fact, whether or not it would advance the client's case.
> - If the client will be charged in the coming days: arrange a bailor to attend court (Singapore citizen or PR, above 21, not a bankrupt, not involved in the case), with sufficient security and passport; otherwise the client stays in remand until bail is furnished.

**P** In the 2026 sample, deleting cinema CCTV attracted ss 204/204A PC, claiming a real penknife was a "cosplay prop" attracted s 182 PC and r 9(2)(g)–(h) PCR, and the answer also had to explain the s 22 long statement.

**A** First, identify what [Client] proposes: [deleting the footage through a friend / claiming the weapon was fake / saying he was elsewhere]. Each step is [an offence under s 204A or s 201 PC / a false statement under s 182 PC], which would expose [Client] to a further charge and would destroy his credibility at any trial. Second, state my own limit: I must not concoct evidence or contrive facts (r 9(2)(g)–(h) PCR), so I cannot help frame the [fake prop] account. Third, explain the interview: the IO will record a s 22 statement; whatever [Client] says can be used to charge and convict, so the account must be truthful, and a later account inconsistent with it will damage his case. Fourth, if a charge is likely, advise him to arrange a bailor who meets the requirements. **C** Therefore I would advise [Client] not to [delete / fabricate], to give a truthful account in the interview with an understanding of how the statement may be used, and to prepare a bailor.

*Source note: CL1 s5–7 (before charge; ss 182, 200, 201, 204A PC; r 9(2)(g)–(h) PCR; bailor requirements); SQ p64 Q1(a) and guide p123 (marks for ss 204/204A, s 182, r 9(2)(g)–(h), s 22 CPC).*

### pcp-w-0-2 — Advising a client after charge: statements, defences, alibi notice, plead guilty or claim trial
*Keywords: after charge, charged in court, mention, examine the charge, elements of the offence, cautioned statement, s 23 CPC, long statement, s 22 CPC, possible defences, general exceptions, s 107 EA, s 108 EA, alibi, notice of alibi, s 278 CPC, plead guilty or claim trial, PCR r 14(5), client decides, representations, mitigation details*

**I** The issue is what advice [Client], now charged with [offence under s X], needs before deciding how to plead. **C** I must examine the charge against [Client]'s instructions, explain the statements already recorded, identify any defence (including [alibi], for which notice is required), and then advise [Client] whether to plead guilty or claim trial, the decision being [his].
**R** Once charged, the case enters the court system and the accused must eventually plead guilty or claim trial. The two investigation statements are the long statement under s 22 CPC and the cautioned statement under s 23 CPC, recorded when the charge is presented; admissions in either may be used to convict. A legal practitioner must advise an accused person whether to plead guilty or claim trial (r 14(5)(a)(i) PCR). The burden of proving a general exception in the Penal Code lies on the accused (s 107 Evidence Act 1893 ("EA")), as does proof of facts especially within his knowledge (s 108 EA), and an alibi may only be relied on if notice is given under s 278 CPC.

> **Rule in full**
> - After charge (CL1 s8): follow the same instruction-taking as before charge; then
>   - examine the charges and take detailed instructions on the client's involvement;
>   - explain the s 22 "long" statement (during investigations) and the s 23 "cautioned" statement (the accused's written response when the charge is presented);
>   - admissions in either may found the charge and the conviction.
> - PCR r 14(5)(a): the practitioner must advise the accused (i) whether to plead guilty or claim trial, and (ii) whether to give evidence or remain silent.
> - Defences (Cyril-Exam §2.2, quoting the course notes): watch for alibi and the general exceptions; s 107 EA (burden of general exceptions on the accused); s 108 EA (facts especially within his knowledge); s 278 CPC (notice of alibi).
> - s 278 CPC (alibi):
>   - (1) without the court's permission, the accused may not offer evidence in support of an alibi unless he gives notice of particulars of the alibi;
>   - (2) nor call an alibi witness without permission unless the notice gives the witness's name and address (or information to find him), with reasonable steps and prompt updates if not known;
>   - (5) notice is given (a) to the court when first charged, or (b) in writing to the Public Prosecutor within 14 days of first being charged in court;
>   - (9) the court may not refuse permission if no advocate appears to have been instructed before trial and the accused was unaware of the section;
>   - (10) "evidence in support of an alibi" = evidence tending to show that, being elsewhere at the time, the accused was not, or was unlikely to have been, at the scene.
> - Also decide whether to make representations to AGC (0-8) and, if pleading guilty, collect background for mitigation (0-18).

**P** Before advising on plea the lawyer should decide whether to make representations to the AGC to reduce, consolidate or withdraw charges, and if the client will plead guilty, obtain his background for mitigation (Cyril-Exam §2.2).

**A** First, break the charge into its elements and set [Client]'s instructions against each: [he admits touching but says it was accidental — this goes to the mental element]. Second, check what [Client] said in his s 22 and s 23 statements; if they contain [an apology or admission], an inconsistent defence at trial will be tested against them (see 0-32 for challenging admissibility). Third, identify defences: [alibi — notice must be given under s 278 CPC; a general exception — he bears the burden under s 107 EA]. Fourth, assess the strength of the evidence and the realistic sentence on each course, and whether representations (0-8) or a plea offer (0-11) are worth seeking first. Fifth, advise frankly on plea, record the advice in writing, and follow [Client]'s decision. **C** Therefore I would advise [Client] that [on the evidence a plea of guilty after representations / claiming trial on the defence of X] is the better course, while making clear that the choice is [his].

*Source note: CL1 s8 (after charge; ss 22, 23 CPC), s14 (r 14(5)(a)(i)); CL5 s41 (r 14(5)(a)(ii)); Cyril-Exam §2.1.2, §2.2 (ss 107, 108 EA; s 278 CPC; representations; mitigation details); CPC-Text s 278(1), (2), (5), (9), (10). Conflict: Nic-Crim 2.1.1.2 cites the unrepresented-accused rule as s 278(6); the CPC text has it at s 278(9).*

### pcp-w-0-3 — Getting seized evidence and the accused's own statements (Goldring; CCDC)
*Keywords: Goldring, access to seized documents, seized phone, in-car camera, WhatsApp, company documents, CCTV, copy of statement, own statement, long statement copy, not given a copy, obtain statement, Case for the Prosecution, Prosecution's Supplementary Bundle, CCDC opt in, Magistrate's Court*

**I** The issue is whether and when [Client] can obtain [the documents seized from him / a copy of the statement he gave the police]. **C** [Client] is entitled to access [documents he owned or controlled before seizure] under *PP v Goldring Timothy Nicholas* [2014] 1 SLR 586, by writing to the AGC; his statements will be disclosed through the criminal case disclosure procedure, in the Case for the Prosecution or the Prosecution's Supplementary Bundle.
**R** An accused has a common law right of access to documents over which he had ownership, legal custody or a legal right of control immediately before they were lawfully seized (*Goldring*). Under the CCDC, the Case for the Prosecution must include any written statement of the accused the Prosecution intends to adduce (s 162(1)(e) CPC), and the Prosecution's Supplementary Bundle every other written statement of the accused relating to the charges proceeded with (s 166(1)(a) CPC).

> **Rule in full**
> - *Goldring*: right of access to documents the accused owned, had legal custody of or a legal right to control immediately before lawful seizure; write to the prosecuting agency (usually AGC).
>   - Examples: the client's own communications (WhatsApp, Telegram, email), in-car camera footage, company documents, CCTV from his own home.
>   - Not covered: external sources such as a shopping mall's CCTV or a co-accused's phone records.
> - Accused's statements under the CCDC:
>   - s 162(1)(e): CFP includes written statements of the accused the Prosecution intends to adduce; (f)–(g) list and transcripts of audiovisual statements; s 162(2) defence may view (not copy) the recording.
>   - s 166(1)(a), (aa): PSB includes every other written statement of the accused (and transcripts of other audiovisual statements) relating to the charges proceeded with.
> - The CCDC applies to Second Schedule offences tried in a District Court, and to High Court cases; otherwise only if both sides consent (s 159(3)) — see 0-19.

**P** Unused statements of the accused that favour him may also fall within the Prosecution's Kadar obligations (CL4 s21), so a statement withheld from the CFP and PSB should be sought on that basis too (see 0-22).

**A** First, identify the item: [his own phone messages / dashcam / statement]. If it was his and was seized, write to the AGC invoking *Goldring*; if it is third-party material (e.g. [mall CCTV]), *Goldring* does not apply and the route is Kadar disclosure (0-22) or the defence's own enquiries. Second, for his statement: if the case is [a Second Schedule offence in the District Court], the CCDC applies and the statement comes in the CFP (if relied on) or the PSB (if not), within the s 161 and s 166 timelines (0-20). If the case is [in the Magistrate's Court] the CCDC does not apply unless both parties consent, so [Client] should consider asking the Prosecution to opt in under s 159(3) (0-19). **C** Therefore [Client] can obtain [the item] by [writing to the AGC under Goldring / the CFP or PSB after the CCDC starts], and I would [write now / seek to opt in].

*Source note: CL1 s9 (Goldring); CL4 s10–15 (ss 162, 166 CPC), s8–9 (s 159); PYP23-CRI Q1.2 (client not given a copy of his statement in a Magistrate's Court case).*

### pcp-w-0-4 — Ethics in acting for an accused: confession, inconsistent instructions, personal belief
*Keywords: client confesses, confession to lawyer, I did it but tell the judge I am innocent, PCR r 14(4), personal opinion of guilt, do you believe him, PCR r 14(3)(b), inconsistent instructions, changing story, PCR r 5, honest, diligence, attendance notes, no promises, witness interviews, discharge from acting, mislead the court*

**I** The issue is whether I may continue to act for [Client], who has [confessed to me that he committed the offence / given an account that is inconsistent with his earlier statement], and what I may do at trial. **C** I may continue to act, but I must not adduce evidence or make submissions inconsistent with [his confession]; my personal view of his guilt must not affect how I conduct his defence.
**R** Where an accused confesses to his legal practitioner, the practitioner may continue to act but must not adduce any evidence or make any submission inconsistent with the confession (r 14(4) PCR). A practitioner representing an accused must not express a personal opinion on guilt or let that opinion affect his assessment of the facts or law, his conduct of the proceedings, or his duty to the accused or the court (r 14(3)(b) PCR). He must be honest with, and diligent for, the client (r 5 PCR) and must not concoct evidence (r 9(2)(g)–(h)).

> **Rule in full**
> - PCR r 14(4): where the accused confesses to the practitioner, the practitioner (a) may continue to represent the accused; but (b) must not adduce any evidence or make any submission which is inconsistent with the confession.
> - PCR r 14(3)(b): must not express a personal opinion on whether the accused is guilty, or allow that opinion to affect (i) the professional assessment of the facts or law, (ii) the conduct of the criminal proceedings, or (iii) the duty to the accused or the court.
> - PCR r 14(2): must pursue every reasonable defence and raise every favourable factor in accordance with law.
> - PCR r 5 (selected): (a) honest in all dealings; (b) inform the client of all information that may reasonably affect his interests; (c) reasonable diligence and competence; (e) keep the client informed; (i) follow lawful, proper and reasonable instructions; (j) use all legal means to advance the client's interests; (k) keep contemporaneous records of instructions and advice.
> - Practice points (CL1 s15–17): keep attendance notes; do not concoct or adduce false evidence; do not give advice you are unsure of or know to be untrue; make no promises beyond your control; interview witnesses separately, without the client present.

**P** A practitioner must not knowingly assist or permit a client to mislead the court (r 10(6)(a) PCR), and must not knowingly mislead the court himself (r 9(2)(a)); where he knows the client is about to give or has given false evidence, he must cease to act or, if he continues, conduct the case so as not to perpetuate the falsehood (r 10(4)). He may withdraw where that is necessary to avoid contravening the Rules (r 26(5)(f)).

**A** First, identify the instruction: [Client said "I admit it, but tell the judge I'm innocent"]. Under r 14(4)(b) I cannot tell the court he is innocent, call him to deny the offence, or put a positive defence inconsistent with the admission. Second, explain to [Client] what I can still do — [test the Prosecution's evidence; mitigate if he pleads guilty] — and what I cannot. If he insists on testifying that he is innocent, I must cease to act or, if I continue, not perpetuate the falsehood (r 10(4) PCR), and I may withdraw to avoid breaching the Rules (r 26(5)(f)). Third, where the issue is my belief ([Client]'s story changed from "I am sorry, I plead for leniency" to "it was accidental"), r 14(3)(b) means my opinion of guilt is not the test: I take full instructions, explore the inconsistency with him, record them in an attendance note, advise on how his earlier response will be used, and act on his instructions so long as I do not knowingly put forward false evidence. **C** Therefore I may continue to represent [Client], but only within r 14(4) [and r 9(2)], and I would record my advice and his instructions in writing.

*Source note: CL1 s10–17 (r 5, r 14(3)(b), r 14(4), r 14(5)(a)(i); practice points); CL3 s27 (r 14(2), (7), (8)); PYP25 Q1.5 (confession during trial); PYP23-CRI Q1.1 ("do you believe him enough to represent him?"); Cyril-Exam §1 (r 14 summary); PCR-Text rr 9(2)(a), 10(4), 10(6), 26(5)(f).*

## 0.II Bail

- **Will my client be released on bail? Who decides?**
  - Before charge: police (agency) bail; after charge: court bail → [0-5](#pcp-w-0-5)
  - Check the First Schedule CPC, 5th column: bailable or non-bailable → [0-5](#pcp-w-0-5)
- **Bailable offence**
  - Must be released (s 92(1)); but if punishable with imprisonment, court may refuse on prescribed grounds that he will not attend (s 92(3); r 5 CPR: not a citizen or PR, not domiciled, seriousness) → [0-5](#pcp-w-0-5)
- **Non-bailable offence (e.g. outrage of modesty, criminal intimidation)**
  - Discretionary ("may be released", s 93(1)); onus on the accused (*Selvamsylvester*); *Yang Yin* factors: guilt, gravity, punishment, absconding, character, repetition, witnesses, preparation, delay → [0-6](#pcp-w-0-6)
  - Foreign ties, half the year abroad, family overseas = flight risk; answer with conditions (passport, tagging, higher bail) → [0-6](#pcp-w-0-6), [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)
  - Offence carries 7 years or less: personal bond instead of bail with prosecution consent (s 93(1A)) → [0-6](#pcp-w-0-6)
  - No bail: death or life imprisonment, or prescribed grounds of non-attendance (s 95(1)); carve-outs for juveniles, sick, infirm (s 95(2)) → [0-6](#pcp-w-0-6)
- **Conditions, quantum, bailor and breach**
  - Conditions: attend, surrender passport, no offence, no witness interference, no leaving Singapore, electronic tagging (s 94) → [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)
  - Amount: enough that the bailor won't shirk (*Ewe Pang Kooi*; s 96); up to $15,000 pledge of property, above that cash or equivalent → [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)
  - Breach: arrest without warrant (s 103); absconding is an offence (s 103(5)); surety's duties and discharge → [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)
  - Can I stand bail for my client? No (r 14(3)(a)) → [0-7](#pcp-w-0-7)

### pcp-w-0-5 — Bail for a bailable offence (s 92 CPC)
*Keywords: bail, agency bail, police bail, court bail, bailable offence, First Schedule CPC fifth column, bail as of right, must be released, s 92(1) CPC, s 92(3) CPC, prescribed grounds, r 5 CPR, not a citizen, not permanent resident, not domiciled, remand*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], charged with [offence], which is a bailable offence, will be released on bail. **C** [Client] must be released on bail as of right under s 92(1) CPC, unless, the offence being punishable with imprisonment, the court believes on a prescribed ground that he will not attend court (s 92(3) CPC).
**R** Bail is the release of an accused from remand while the case is pending, usually on a surety placing money in court custody, to ensure he attends court. Whether an offence is bailable is shown in the fifth column of the First Schedule to the CPC. For a bailable offence the accused "must be released" on bail (s 92(1) CPC); but where the offence is punishable with imprisonment, the court may refuse bail if it believes, on the grounds prescribed in r 5 of the Criminal Procedure Rules 2018 ("CPR"), that he will not attend (s 92(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Agency bail: granted by the police, usually before charge. Court bail: granted by the court.
> - Bailable offences: bail as of right; generally no discretion to refuse ("must be released", s 92(1) CPC).
> - s 92(3): for offences punishable with imprisonment, the court can order non-release on police bail or refuse court bail if it believes on prescribed grounds that the person will not attend.
> - r 5 CPR prescribed grounds: a mix of facts (e.g. the accused is not a citizen or PR; is not domiciled in Singapore) and factors (e.g. nature and seriousness of the offence; likely sentence on conviction; strength of evidence; association with others; preparatory acts showing an intention not to surrender) (CL1 s22; Cyril-Exam §2.4.1).
> - Purpose of bail: to ensure the accused attends court and remains answerable (does not abscond).

**P** The quantum is not meant to deter flight but to ensure the bailor does not shirk his duties (*Ewe Pang Kooi v PP* [2015] 2 SLR 672) — see 0-7.

**A** First, check the First Schedule: [offence under s X PC] is bailable, so the starting point is release as of right. Second, if it is punishable with imprisonment, consider the r 5 CPR grounds the Prosecution may raise: [Client] is [a Singapore citizen with a home and job here], so there is no prescribed ground for believing he will not attend; [or: he is a foreigner who is not domiciled here, which the Prosecution may rely on, so propose conditions such as surrender of passport]. Third, prepare a bailor (0-7). **C** Therefore [Client] should be released on bail [as of right / on conditions], and I would have the bailor attend at the first mention.

*Source note: CL1 s19–22 (definition, agency and court bail, s 92(1), (3), r 5 CPR); Cyril-Exam §2.4.1 (r 5 grounds listed).*

### pcp-w-0-6 — Bail for a non-bailable offence: discretion, Yang Yin factors and exceptions (ss 93, 95 CPC)
*Keywords: non-bailable offence, discretionary bail, may be released, s 93 CPC, onus on accused, Selvamsylvester, Yang Yin, flight risk, absconding, foreign ties, lives abroad, Thailand, family overseas, gravity of offence, tamper with witnesses, s 95 CPC, death or life imprisonment, no bail, juvenile, sick, infirm, s 93(5), outrage of modesty, criminal intimidation, will I be locked up*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], charged with [offence], a non-bailable offence, will be released on bail. **C** Bail is at the court's discretion; on the facts it is likely that [Client] will be granted bail [on conditions such as surrender of his passport and a higher quantum], because [the offence is not of the gravest kind and his ties to Singapore are strong / the flight risk can be managed].
**R** For a non-bailable offence the accused "may be released" on bail at the discretion of the police or court (s 93(1) CPC), and the onus is on him to persuade the court (*S Selvamsylvester v PP* [2005] 4 SLR(R) 409). The court weighs the factors in *PP v Yang Yin* [2015] 2 SLR 78. Bail must not be granted where the offence is punishable with death or life imprisonment, or where the court believes on prescribed grounds that the accused will not attend (s 95(1) CPC), subject to the carve-outs in s 95(2).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 93(1): discretion ("may be released").
> - Must release (subject to s 95): where there are no reasonable grounds for believing the accused committed a non-bailable offence, or there are grounds for further inquiry into whether some bailable offence was committed (s 93(2), (3A)); but bail may be refused if that bailable offence is punishable with imprisonment and the court believes on prescribed grounds he will not attend (s 93(3B); r 5 CPR).
> - *Yang Yin* factors:
>   - whether there are reasonable grounds for believing the accused is guilty;
>   - the nature and gravity of the offence charged;
>   - the severity and degree of punishment that might follow;
>   - the danger of the accused absconding if released;
>   - the accused's character, means and standing;
>   - the danger of the offence being continued or repeated;
>   - the danger of witnesses being tampered with;
>   - whether bail is essential for an adequate opportunity to prepare the defence;
>   - the length of detention and the probability of further delay.
> - s 93(5): even after bail, the court may at any later stage cause the person to be arrested and committed to prison.
> - Personal bond instead of bail (s 93(1A)–(1D), in force 1 Aug 2024): the court may release the accused on his own personal bond if (a) the non-bailable offence is punishable with imprisonment for not more than 7 years and (b) the prosecution consents; the prosecution may withdraw consent, and the court must then revoke the order and consider bail (s 93(1B)); police (sergeant or above) may also release on personal bond for such offences (s 93(1C)); not where an enhanced penalty provision takes the maximum above 7 years (s 93(1D)).
> - s 95(1): bail shall not be granted where (a) the accused is charged with an offence punishable with death or imprisonment for life; or (b) the court believes on prescribed grounds he will not attend.
> - s 95(2): carve-outs allow release (e.g. a juvenile, or a sick or infirm person).

**P** In *Yang Yin* the court weighed these factors together; no single factor is decisive, and the risk of absconding is usually met by conditions (passport surrender, reporting, electronic tagging) and a higher quantum rather than refusal (CL1 s24, s26; Cyril-Exam CS1: tagging can assuage the court).

**A** First, confirm [offence] is non-bailable (First Schedule, col 5) and not punishable with death or life imprisonment, so s 95(1)(a) does not bar bail. Second, run the *Yang Yin* factors on the facts: gravity and punishment — [s 354(1) PC carries up to X years; serious but not of the gravest kind]; absconding — [Client spends half the year in Thailand, where his wife and child live, which points to flight risk; but he has [a job / property / parents] here and surrendered his passport]; character — [no antecedents, a teacher with a mortgage and elderly parents]; witnesses — [the complainant is a stranger, little risk of interference]; repetition — [none suggested]. Third, if the offence carries 7 years or less (e.g. s 354(1) PC, 3 years), ask the prosecution whether it will consent to release on a personal bond (s 93(1A)); otherwise propose conditions to answer the risk: surrender of passport, no leaving Singapore without permission, reporting, electronic tagging, a substantial sum from a suitable bailor (0-7). **C** Therefore [Client] is likely to be offered bail on conditions, though the court [may set a higher amount / impose tagging] because of his overseas ties.

*Source note: CL1 s23–25 (s 93(1)–(5); Selvamsylvester; Yang Yin factors; s 95(1)–(2)); SSO-CPC s 93(1A)–(1D) (Act 5 of 2024, wef 1 Aug 2024 — after the course slides' bail lecture was prepared, not mentioned in CL1); Cyril-Exam §2.4.2 and CS1 notes (tagging); PYP25 Q1.1 (half-year in Thailand); SQ p64 Q1(b), guide p123–124 (teacher, mortgage, parents; non-bailable, discretion, onus).*

### pcp-w-0-7 — Bail conditions, breach, quantum, the bailor and the surety's duties (ss 94, 96, 103 CPC)
*Keywords: bail conditions, s 94 CPC, surrender passport, travel documents, electronic tagging, no leaving Singapore, breach of bail, arrest without warrant, s 103 CPC, absconding offence, s 103(5), quantum of bail, Ewe Pang Kooi, s 96 CPC, $15,000, cash, fixed deposit, pledge property, bailor, surety, duties of surety, forfeiture, discharge surety, PCR r 14(3)(a), lawyer stand bail*

**I** The issue is [what conditions and amount of bail are likely / what happens if [Client] breaches bail or fails to attend / what [Bailor]'s duties are as surety]. **C** [Answer: conditions under s 94 CPC; quantum set so that the bailor will not shirk (*Ewe Pang Kooi*); breach exposes [Client] to arrest without warrant under s 103 and a fresh offence, and the bailor to losing the sum].
**R** Bail is granted on statutory conditions (attendance, surrender of travel documents, no offence, no interference with witnesses, no leaving Singapore without permission) and any other necessary condition, including electronic tagging (s 94 CPC). The amount must be sufficient to ensure the bailor does not shirk his duties, not to deter flight (*Ewe Pang Kooi v PP* [2015] 2 SLR 672; s 96 CPC). A released person who breaches a condition may be arrested without warrant (s 103 CPC), and absconding on bail is itself an offence (s 103(5)). A legal practitioner must not stand surety or bail for his client (r 14(3)(a) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Conditions (s 94(1), imposed unless the police or court specify otherwise): (a) surrender travel documents; (b) surrender to custody, be available for investigations or attend court as appointed; (c) commit no offence on bail or personal bond; (d) not interfere with witnesses or obstruct justice; (e) a surety must not be a co-accused. Not leaving Singapore without permission (s 99(2), (4)). Other necessary conditions, including electronic monitoring (s 94(2)–(3)); if the prosecution applies for electronic monitoring, the police or court must impose it (s 94(4)); on a personal bond under s 93(1A), the court must impose any condition the prosecution specifies (s 94(1A)).
> - Breach (s 103): the accused may be arrested without warrant; the court then decides whether to re-grant or deny bail; absconding while on bail is an offence (s 103(5)). Cyril adds arrest where the bailor tells police the accused is unlikely to attend and asks to be relieved as surety (s 103(3)(b)).
> - Quantum (*Ewe Pang Kooi*; s 96): primarily sufficient so that the bailor does not shirk; it is the bailor's money at stake.
>   - Bail up to $15,000: the surety may pledge fully paid personal property (e.g. a flat) instead of cash.
>   - Above $15,000: cash or cash equivalents (fixed deposits), frozen by the Bail Centre.
> - Bailor (CL1 s7): Singapore citizen or PR; above 21; not a bankrupt; not involved in the case (not a co-accused or victim); bring passport.
> - PCR r 14(3)(a): a practitioner must not provide any surety or bail for the accused.
> - Contact session (Cyril-Exam CS1): it is the bailor who furnishes the sum ("my client can't afford it" is beside the point); a bailor who wants to be discharged must inform the Prosecution and go through a court process; the bailor is told of his duties when signing the bail bond.

**P** A surety must ensure the released person attends as required, keep in daily communication with him and lodge a police report within 24 hours of losing contact, and ensure he stays in Singapore unless permitted to leave (s 104 CPC); a surety may apply to discharge the bond (s 105); if the released person fails without reasonable excuse to attend, the court must forfeit the bond and may call on each surety to explain why the bond should not be forfeited as against him (s 107A).

**A** First, list the likely conditions for [Client]: [surrender passport; no travel; report to IO; electronic tag given his overseas links]. Second, quantum: [given the gravity and the flight risk, the court may fix a higher sum, e.g. above $15,000, which must then be in cash or fixed deposit]; identify a bailor who qualifies ([his brother, a citizen over 21, not a bankrupt]). Third, explain the consequences of breach: [Client] may be arrested without warrant and remanded, and absconding is a separate offence; [Bailor] risks losing the sum and should keep in touch with [Client] and inform the police if he will not attend. Fourth, decline any suggestion that I stand bail (r 14(3)(a)). **C** Therefore [Client] should expect bail of about [$X] on conditions [A, B, C], furnished by [Bailor], who must understand that the money is at risk if [Client] breaches.

*Source note: CL1 s7 (bailor), s26–29 (ss 94, 99, 103 CPC; quantum; Ewe Pang Kooi; s 96; $15,000; r 14(3)(a)); SSO-CPC s 94(1)–(4) (current text); Cyril-Exam §2.4.3–2.4.5 and CS1 notes; CPC-Text ss 104, 105, 107A (surety's duties, discharge, forfeiture — not in CL1); PYP21-CRI Q2.4 (surety's duties).*

## 0.III Pre-trial negotiations

- **Can the client settle privately or avoid prosecution?**
  - Representations to the police (before charge) or AGC (after), and CCMS discussions → [0-8](#pcp-w-0-8)
  - Ask for withdrawal (evidential gaps or public interest), with or without a warning → [0-9](#pcp-w-0-9)
  - Offence in the Fourth Schedule CPC: compound with the victim, with PP's consent after investigations start (s 241) → [0-10](#pcp-w-0-10)
- **Are representations privileged? What must they say?**
  - Without prejudice without express agreement (*Glenn Knight*); still mark them WP; include the s 182 PC statement → [0-8](#pcp-w-0-8)
- **Effect of a warning or a composition later**
  - Stern warning: discharge amounting to acquittal; conditional warning (12/24 months): DNAA, charge can be revived → [0-9](#pcp-w-0-9)
  - Warning cannot aggravate a later sentence (*Teo Seng Tiong*; *GCO*) → [0-9](#pcp-w-0-9)
  - Compounded offence: discharge amounting to acquittal (s 241(4)–(5)) but presumptively relevant to later sentence (*Teo Seng Tiong*) → [0-10](#pcp-w-0-10)
- **Prosecution offers a plea deal: proceed on some, TIC the rest**
  - Ask to proceed on fewer charges (watch s 307: 3 sentences of imprisonment means 2 consecutive), reduce s 380 to s 379, agree the SOF and sentencing position → [0-11](#pcp-w-0-11)

### pcp-w-0-8 — Representations to the AGC and police; CCMS; without prejudice privilege
*Keywords: representations, letter of representation, AGC, Attorney-General's Chambers, police representations, CCMS, Criminal Case Management System, plea negotiations, without prejudice, privilege, Glenn Knight, Ng Chye Huay, s 182 PC statement, consensual case disposal, withdrawal, reduction, composition, amalgamation, TIC*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] should make representations to the [AGC / Police] to [have the charge(s) withdrawn / reduced / compounded], and whether what is said is protected. **C** [Client] should make written representations, marked without prejudice, seeking [withdrawal / a reduced charge], and they will be privileged under *PP v Knight Glenn Jeyasingam* [1999] 1 SLR(R) 1165.
**R** Pre-trial negotiations are without prejudice communications with the Prosecution seeking a change in its position, including withdrawal or reduction of charges, composition, amalgamation or taking charges into consideration (*Glenn Knight* at [24]–[25]). Representations and CCMS discussions with the AGC are without prejudice without the need for express agreement (*Glenn Knight* at [72]); representations to the police are privileged where conditions are met (*Ng Chye Huay v PP* [2005] SGHC 193). AGC requires every representation to state that the accused's attention has been drawn to s 182 PC (*Glenn Knight* at [49]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Modes: written representations to AGC; CCMS discussions with prosecutors (meetings, calls; oral representations; informal exchange of evidence); written representations to the police (usually after investigations start but before a charging decision).
> - Privilege (*Glenn Knight*):
>   - [72]: privilege over representations in plea negotiations maintains public confidence; no express agreement is needed; limited to representations made in plea negotiations;
>   - policy: consensual case disposal serves the Prosecution (costs, time, resources), the courts (sentencing policy) and the accused (withdrawal or lesser charges);
>   - [49]: the s 182 PC statement guards against false information in representations.
> - Practice: mark all representations "without prejudice"; do not mark letters you may want to use in court.
> - Purposes (CL2 s11): withdrawal (evidential gaps or mitigating, public-interest factors), composition, amending or reducing charges on a plea, revising PG offers, negotiating the Prosecution's sentencing position.
> - Always obtain documentary support (medical reports, testimonials) (Cyril-Exam §2.2).

**P** In *Glenn Knight* the High Court recognised that this arena "evolved to meet the interests" of all three participants, and that protecting representations is "necessary" to preserve consensual disposal and the sentencing discount for pleas (at [24]–[25], [72]).

**A** First, identify the aim: [withdrawal because the evidence on dishonesty is weak / reduction from s 380 to s 379 to avoid mandatory imprisonment / composition with the victim]. Second, gather the material: [Client]'s account, [medical report on depression, testimonials, proof of restitution]. Third, draft the representations marked "without prejudice", setting out the grounds and confirming that [Client] has been told of s 182 PC. Fourth, explain to [Client] that the representations cannot be used against him at trial (*Glenn Knight*), but that the Prosecution's decision is discretionary and no outcome can be promised (0-4). **C** Therefore I would write to the [AGC] seeking [outcome], marked without prejudice, and follow up at the CCMS.

*Source note: CL2 s3–11 (Glenn Knight [24]–[25], [49], [72]; Ng Chye Huay; CCMS; purposes); Cyril-Exam §2.2 (documentary evidence); PYP25 Q2.1 ("Can I settle this privately or avoid prosecution?").*

### pcp-w-0-9 — Withdrawal of charges and warnings (stern and conditional)
*Keywords: withdrawal of charge, warning, stern warning, conditional warning, 12-month warning, 24-month warning, discharge amounting to acquittal, DAA, discharge not amounting to acquittal, DNAA, Wham Kwok Han Jolovan, Han Li Ying Kirsten, Teo Seng Tiong, GCO v PP, warning not aggravating*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can seek withdrawal of the charge with a warning, and what effect a warning would have. **C** [Client] may ask the Prosecution to withdraw the charge, typically with a warning; a stern warning would lead to a discharge amounting to an acquittal, a conditional warning to a discharge not amounting to an acquittal, and neither may later be treated as an aggravating factor.
**R** Withdrawal may be sought on evidential grounds (absence of an element) or public-interest grounds, unconditionally or with a warning. A warning is no more than the authority's opinion that an offence was committed and is not a binding finding of guilt (*Wham Kwok Han Jolovan v A-G* [2016] 1 SLR 1370 at [34]); by itself it is not an assurance against prosecution unless accompanied by a discharge amounting to an acquittal (*Han Li Ying Kirsten v A-G* [2023] SGHC 137 at [28]–[40]). A warning cannot aggravate a later sentence (*Teo Seng Tiong v PP* [2021] 2 SLR 642 at [88]), nor can breach of a conditional warning (*GCO v PP* [2019] 3 SLR 1402 at [69]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Types of warning (CL2 s12):
>   - stern warning: withdrawal results in a discharge amounting to an acquittal;
>   - conditional warning (typically 12 or 24 months): if the offender re-offends within the period the charge can be resurrected; withdrawal results in a discharge not amounting to an acquittal.
> - *Wham Kwok Han* [34]: a warning is an expression of opinion, not a legally binding pronouncement of guilt or finding of fact.
> - *Han Li Ying* [28]–[40]: a warning is not an assurance of non-prosecution unless accompanied by a discharge amounting to an acquittal.
> - *Teo Seng Tiong* [88]: a warning cannot be an aggravating factor and is excluded from sentencing.
> - *GCO* [69]: breach of a conditional warning may lead to a fresh charge but is not a discrete aggravating factor.

**P** In *Teo Seng Tiong* the Court of Appeal set out which past conduct counts at sentencing: TIC charges and criminal records do, compounded offences presumptively do, and warnings do not (CL3 s24; see 0-17).

**A** First, assess whether withdrawal is realistic: [first offender, low value, restitution made, mental-health context] supports a request on public-interest grounds; [doubt about the dishonesty element] supports an evidential request. Second, make representations (0-8) seeking withdrawal with a [stern] warning. Third, explain the consequences: a stern warning ends the matter with a discharge amounting to an acquittal; a conditional warning leaves the charge capable of revival if [Client] re-offends within [12/24] months, but it will not count as an antecedent or aggravating factor in any later case. **C** Therefore I would seek withdrawal with a stern warning, explaining to [Client] the difference between the two types.

*Source note: CL2 s11–14 (warnings; Wham Kwok Han [34]; Han Li Ying [28]–[40]; Teo Seng Tiong [88]; GCO [69]); CL3 s24.*

### pcp-w-0-10 — Composition of offences (s 241 CPC, Fourth Schedule)
*Keywords: composition, compound, compoundable offence, Fourth Schedule CPC, s 241 CPC, settle with victim, compensation, consent of victim, consent of Public Prosecutor, discharge amounting to acquittal, s 241(4), s 241(5), Teo Seng Tiong, compounded offence at sentencing, settle privately*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can compound the offence of [offence] with [victim] instead of being prosecuted. **C** [Offence] [is / is not] listed in the Fourth Schedule to the CPC; [if it is,] [Client] may compound it with [victim]'s consent [and, investigations having begun, the Public Prosecutor's consent] under s 241 CPC, which leads to a discharge amounting to an acquittal.
**R** Offences listed in the Fourth Schedule to the CPC may be compounded, usually on payment of compensation, with the consent of the person entitled to compound (typically the victim) and, once investigations have commenced, of the Public Prosecutor (s 241(1) CPC). Once compounded, no further proceedings may be taken and the court must order a discharge amounting to an acquittal (s 241(4)–(5)). A compounded offence is nevertheless presumptive proof of an admission of guilt that may be a relevant sentencing factor later (*Teo Seng Tiong v PP* [2021] 2 SLR 642 at [101]–[102]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Composition: settling an offence between the parties without formal court proceedings; usually compensation; requires the consent of the other party (typically the victim); after investigations start, also the PP's consent (s 241(1)).
> - s 241(4)–(5): no further proceedings; court must order a discharge amounting to an acquittal on any charges.
> - *Teo Seng Tiong* [101]–[102]:
>   - compounded offences can be "a relevant factor which may affect the sentence" and are "presumptive proof of admission of guilt unless shown otherwise";
>   - the Prosecution must highlight them; the offender may show he compounded for other reasons; the court may hold a hearing to decide what weight to give.

**P** The Fourth Schedule lists each compoundable offence and who may compound it. Among Penal Code offences it includes voluntarily causing hurt (s 323), outrage of modesty (s 354(1), by the person assaulted), mischief (s 426, by the person who suffers loss), criminal trespass (s 447, by the person in possession of the property) and criminal intimidation (s 506, by the person intimidated, except threats to cause death or grievous hurt etc.); theft (ss 379, 380) is not listed (CPC-Text, Fourth Schedule Part 1). So in PYP25 Q2.1 (macaw thefts at a bird park) the trespass charges could be compounded with the park, but not the thefts.

**A** First, check the Fourth Schedule for [offence] and who may compound it ([s 447 — the bird park as occupier; s 379/380 — not compoundable]). If it is compoundable, approach [victim] (through the IO or directly, carefully) about compensation of [sum], and seek the PP's consent since investigations have begun. Second, if compounded, the court must grant a discharge amounting to an acquittal (s 241(5)). Third, warn [Client] that if he re-offends, the compounded offence may be raised at sentencing as presumptive evidence of guilt, though he may explain he compounded for other reasons (*Teo Seng Tiong*). If the offence is not compoundable, the alternatives are representations for withdrawal with a warning (0-9) or a favourable plea offer (0-11). **C** Therefore [Client] [can / cannot] settle this privately by composition, and I would [approach the victim and the PP / make representations instead].

*Source note: CL2 s11, s15–16 (s 241(1), (4)–(5) CPC; Fourth Schedule; Teo Seng Tiong [101]–[102]; the slide cites "[2021] 2 SLR 64", a typo for 642); CPC-Text Fourth Schedule Part 1 items 20, 30, 34, 36, 42 (check SSO for any later amendment); PYP25 Q2.1.*

### pcp-w-0-11 — Plea offers: proceeded and TIC charges, reduced charges, negotiating the SOF and sentence
*Keywords: plea offer, plea bargain, PG offer, proceeded charges, TIC charges, taken into consideration, reduce charge, amend charge, s 380 to s 379, theft in dwelling, mandatory imprisonment, number of charges, s 307 CPC strategy, negotiate statement of facts, sentencing position, CCMS*

**I** The issue is how to respond to the Prosecution's offer to proceed on [X] of [Y] charges with the remainder taken into consideration, and what [Client] can negotiate. **C** I would ask the Prosecution to [proceed on fewer charges / reduce the proceeded charges to s 379 PC], to agree the Statement of Facts and to state its sentencing position, and then advise [Client] on whether to accept.
**R** In most cases the Prosecution will offer a position on a plea of guilt, varying the number of charges proceeded with or reducing or amending charges. Proceeded charges are those on which the accused is convicted and sentenced; TIC charges are not, but with the accused's consent are taken into consideration in sentencing (s 148 CPC). Where an accused is sentenced to imprisonment for at least three distinct offences, at least two terms must run consecutively (s 307(1) CPC), so proceeding on two charges rather than three can lower the overall sentence.

> **Rule in full**
> - Plea offers (CL2 s17–18):
>   - vary the number of charges proceeded with; reduce or amend specific charges;
>   - proceeded charges: conviction and sentence;
>   - TIC charges: no conviction; taken into consideration with the accused's consent (s 148).
> - Illustration (CL2 s18): 5 charges of s 380 PC; Prosecution offers to proceed on 3 and TIC 2; defence asks to proceed on 2 of 5 (lower overall sentence, s 307 CPC); or to reduce proceeded charges to s 379 PC, which has no mandatory imprisonment.
> - Contact session points (Cyril-Exam CS1–2): ask for the PG offer (which charges, what type); negotiate the SOF — facts the client disputes that do not go to the elements, and facts material to sentence; the elements themselves are not negotiable; ask the Prosecution's sentencing position before the PG hearing and try to persuade it lower; put the client's instructions to plead guilty in writing; give no definitive promise of outcome.

**P** The effect of TIC charges on sentence is governed by *Lim Hsien Hwei v PP* [2014] 3 SLR 15 (see 0-16), and the s 307 rule by *Mohamed Shouffee bin Adam v PP* [2014] 2 SLR 998 (see 0-36).

**A** First, map the offer: [proceed on Charges 1–4, TIC 5–6]. With four imprisonment terms, s 307(1) requires at least two to run consecutively; [if the Prosecution then asks for all four consecutive, see 0-36]. Second, counter-propose: [proceed on two charges, TIC the rest], which still engages s 307 only if three or more are sentenced to imprisonment; or [reduce to s 379 PC to avoid mandatory imprisonment]. Third, ask for the draft SOF, go through it with [Client], and resolve disputes about non-elemental facts (0-14). Fourth, obtain the Prosecution's sentencing position and explain to [Client] the likely outcome and what TIC means (0-16). Fifth, confirm [Client]'s decision in writing. **C** Therefore I would seek [a reduced number of proceeded charges / reduced charges] and advise [Client] to accept the offer only once the SOF and sentencing position are known.

*Source note: CL2 s17–18 (plea offers; s 148; s 307; s 380/s 379); Cyril-Exam contact session notes 1 and 2 (SOF negotiation; sentencing position; s 307 when negotiating); PYP25 Q2.2, PYP23-CRI Q2 (offers with TIC).*

## 0.IV Pleading guilty and the sentencing hearing

- **What happens at the PG hearing?**
  - Charge read; court checks the accused understands and intends to admit without qualification; SOF read and admitted; conviction; TICs; record; address on sentence; mitigation; reply → [0-12](#pcp-w-0-12)
  - Valid plea: understands nature (each element) and consequences (punishment; no appeal against conviction, s 375) (*Balasubramanian*) → [0-12](#pcp-w-0-12)
  - Client may not be fit to plead → [0-12](#pcp-w-0-12)
- **Client wants to plead guilty but say "I forgot to pay" / "I didn't mean to" / "I thought it was my phone"**
  - Qualifies the plea: court must reject it (s 228(4); *Md Rafiqul*); case goes to trial → [0-13](#pcp-w-0-13)
  - Said after conviction, in mitigation: same — plea rejected; retraction needs valid and sufficient grounds → [0-13](#pcp-w-0-13)
- **Client disagrees with parts of the SOF**
  - Element disputed → trial; fact material to sentence → Newton hearing; immaterial fact → plea still valid (*Chota*) → [0-14](#pcp-w-0-14)
- **Client relies on a private psychiatric report (depression, OCD, kleptomania, morbid jealousy); IMH disagrees**
  - Not a qualification if it does not deny an element; contested fact material to sentence → Newton hearing (s 228(5)); defence proves it on balance of probabilities (*Catherine Chan*) → [0-15](#pcp-w-0-15)
  - How a mental condition is used in mitigation (rehabilitation, reduced deterrence) → [0-18](#pcp-w-0-18), [0-34](#pcp-w-0-34)
- **What does "taken into consideration" mean?**
  - Consent of both sides; no conviction; usually increases sentence on proceeded charges, but less than conviction would (s 148; *Lim Hsien Hwei*) → [0-16](#pcp-w-0-16)
- **Criminal record, compounded offences, warnings — what is relevant?**
  - Record and TICs yes; compounded presumptively; warnings no; I need not disclose antecedents but must not contradict them (r 14(8)) → [0-17](#pcp-w-0-17)
- **Mitigation plea: what can I say? How to avoid caning or jail?**
  - Every favourable factor (r 14(2)); nothing scandalous (r 14(7)); remorse, first offender, testimonials, mental condition; never qualify the plea → [0-18](#pcp-w-0-18)
  - Structure of a written plea → [0-44](#pcp-w-0-44)

### pcp-w-0-12 — The PG hearing and a valid plea of guilt (ss 227, 375 CPC); fitness to plead
*Keywords: plead guilty, PG mention, plea of guilt, s 227 CPC, nature and consequences, Balasubramanian, understands the plea, without qualification, advocate's confirmation, s 375 CPC, no appeal against conviction, statement of facts, conviction, fitness to plead, unfit to plead*

**I** The issue is what [Client] must understand and do for his plea of guilty to [charge] to be validly recorded. **C** Before the court records [Client]'s plea, I must confirm that he understands the nature and consequences of the plea and intends to admit the offence without qualification (s 227(2)(b) CPC); he must therefore understand every element of the charge, the prescribed punishment, and that he cannot then appeal against conviction (s 375 CPC).
**R** If the accused pleads guilty after the charge has been read and explained, the plea is recorded and he may be convicted on it (s 227(1) CPC). Where he is represented, the court must record the advocate's confirmation that he understands the nature and consequences of the plea and intends to admit the offence without qualification (s 227(2)(b)). "Nature" means he knows exactly what he is charged with; "consequences" means he knows the punishment prescribed (*Balasubramanian Palaniappa Vaiyapuri v PP* [2002] 1 SLR(R) 138 at [25]). An accused convicted on his plea of guilty may not appeal against conviction (s 375 CPC).

> **Rule in full**
> - PG mention (CL3 s3–4): proceeded charges read (others stood down); court confirms intention to plead guilty and understanding (s 227(1)–(2)); SOF read; accused admits it without qualification; conviction; consent to TIC (s 148); criminal record (s 228(2)(a)); address on sentence (s 228(1)–(2)); mitigation and reply (s 228(3)); s 228(4) rejection; s 228(5) Newton hearing.
> - s 227(1): plea after the charge is read and explained, as framed or amended, must be recorded and the accused may be convicted on it.
> - s 227(2): before recording the plea the court must
>   - (a) if unrepresented, be satisfied the accused (i) understands the nature and consequences of the plea and the punishment prescribed, and (ii) intends to admit the offence without qualification;
>   - (b) if represented, record the advocate's confirmation that the accused (i) understands the nature and consequences of the plea, and (ii) intends to admit the offence without qualification.
> - *Balasubramanian* [25]: nature = what exactly he is charged with (in practice, each element); consequences = the punishment prescribed.
> - s 375: no appeal against conviction on a plea of guilty.
> - Fitness to plead (CL-LP item 3; text from CPC-Text): where the court has reason to suspect that the accused, by unsoundness of mind or any physical or mental condition, is incapable of making his defence, it must first investigate (s 247(1)), may receive a medical certificate or oral medical evidence (s 247(3)), and if not satisfied he is capable must postpone and remand him for observation in a psychiatric institution for up to one month (s 247(4)); on the designated medical practitioner's certificate the court must, unless satisfied to the contrary, find him capable or incapable accordingly (s 248(1)–(2)); if capable, it proceeds (s 248(2B)); if incapable, the proceedings are stayed once the court makes an order under s 249(2) or reports the case to the Minister (s 248(2A)), and pending that he may be remanded or released on conditions (s 248(5)).

**P** The SOF exists partly so the court can be sure the accused understands his plea, by setting out the facts and ensuring every element is made out (*Biplob Hossain Younus Akan v PP* [2011] 3 SLR 2 at [6]; *Mok Swee Kok v PP* [1994] 3 SLR(R) 134 at [14]) — see 0-14.

**A** First, go through the charge element by element with [Client] and confirm he admits each one; if he denies any (e.g. [dishonesty]), he cannot plead guilty (0-13). Second, explain the punishment prescribed for [offence] ([up to X years / fine / caning]) and the likely sentence. Third, explain that once convicted on his plea he cannot appeal against the conviction, only the sentence. Fourth, go through the SOF with him (0-14). Fifth, record his instructions to plead guilty in writing. At the hearing I will confirm to the court, under s 227(2)(b), that he understands and intends to admit without qualification. [If there is doubt about his mental state, obtain a psychiatric report before he pleads.] **C** Therefore [Client]'s plea will be valid if he admits every element, understands the punishment and the bar on appealing conviction, and does not qualify it.

*Source note: CL3 s3–8 (PG procedure; s 227; Balasubramanian [25]; s 375); CL3 s14 (Mok Swee Kok; Biplob); CL-LP p3–4 item 3 (fitness to plead listed); SSO-CPC ss 247–248 (current text; s 248 amended by Act 5 of 2024 wef 14 Feb 2025).*

### pcp-w-0-13 — Qualified plea of guilt and retraction (s 228(4) CPC; Md Rafiqul)
*Keywords: qualified plea, qualify the plea, forgot to pay, didn't mean to, accident, thought it was my phone, groggy, mitigation denies element, mens rea, actus reus, dishonesty, s 228(4) CPC, must reject the plea, Md Rafiqul, retract plea, retraction, valid and sufficient grounds, plea rejected, trial*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can plead guilty to [charge] while saying in mitigation that [he forgot to pay / did not intend to touch her / thought he was holding his phone], and the consequences if he does. **C** He cannot: that statement denies [the fault element of dishonesty / intention], so it qualifies his plea, and the court must reject the plea of guilty under s 228(4) CPC; unless he withdraws the qualification, the case must go to trial.
**R** Where the court is satisfied that any matter raised in the plea in mitigation materially affects any legal condition required by law to constitute the offence charged, it must reject the plea of guilty (s 228(4) CPC). If mitigation indicates a lack of mens rea or actus reus, the accused has not admitted the offence without qualification and the plea must be rejected (*Md Rafiqul Islam Abdul Aziz v PP* [2017] 3 SLR 619 at [27]). An accused may retract a plea only on valid and sufficient grounds showing that it is proper and in the interests of justice (*Md Rafiqul*).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 228(4): where the court is satisfied that any matter raised in the plea in mitigation materially affects any legal condition required by law to constitute the offence charged, the court must reject the plea of guilty.
> - *Md Rafiqul* [27]: if mitigation qualifies the plea by indicating lack of mens rea or actus reus, the accused is not taken to have admitted the offence without qualification and the plea is rejected.
> - Example (CL3 s11): theft in dwelling (s 380 PC); dishonesty is the fault element (ss 24, 378 PC); "I forgot to pay" denies it and qualifies the plea.
> - How it arises in practice: (1) the written mitigation expressly undermines an element; (2) the accused orally challenges the SOF after it is read. If the qualification is maintained, the matter goes to trial.
> - Retraction (Cyril-Exam §4.5, citing *Md Rafiqul*): permitted if the accused shows valid and sufficient grounds that it is proper and in the interests of justice — e.g. mistake or misunderstanding, or the plea was not a voluntary and deliberate choice; the court must be careful at plea and in mitigation to ensure an unequivocal admission.
> - Contrast: a mitigating fact that does not deny an element (e.g. depression lowered self-control) is not a qualification; if disputed, it goes to a Newton hearing (0-15).

**P** In *Md Rafiqul* the Court of Appeal confirmed that s 228(4) codifies the earlier case law: a plea qualified by a denial of mens rea or actus reus cannot stand (at [27]).

**A** First, identify the element the statement touches: [s 379/s 380 PC — dishonesty; "I forgot to pay" denies it] / [s 354 PC — intention or knowledge; "it was accidental" denies it] / [s 324 PC — voluntarily causing hurt with a dangerous weapon; "I thought it was my phone" denies knowledge of the weapon]. Second, apply s 228(4): if [Client] says this in mitigation, the court must reject the plea, and if he maintains it the case is fixed for trial. [If raised after conviction, as in Brian's case, the conviction cannot stand on that plea; the plea is rejected and the matter tried, unless he withdraws the statement.] Third, advise the choice: either he genuinely admits every element and drops the statement, or he claims trial; I must not help him plead guilty while denying an element (r 14(4); r 9(2)). Fourth, if he wishes to raise [depression], frame it as a factor going to sentence, not to guilt (0-15). **C** Therefore [Client] must choose between an unqualified plea, without the statement that he [forgot to pay], and claiming trial; if he pleads guilty and then says it, the court must reject the plea under s 228(4).

*Source note: CL3 s4, s10–11 (s 228(4); Md Rafiqul [27]; theft example), s17 (dispute table row 1); Cyril-Exam §4.5 (retraction) and CS2 notes (do not qualify in mitigation); PYP21-CRI Q2.2, PYP22-CRI Q2.2, PYP23-CRI Q2.1, SQ Q1(c) (all tested).*

### pcp-w-0-14 — The Statement of Facts and disputes about it
*Keywords: statement of facts, SOF, admit without qualification, Mok Swee Kok, Biplob, Andrew Koh, Chota bin Abdul Razak, Aniza bte Essa, dispute SOF, disagree with facts, aggravating facts, mitigating facts, element dispute trial, material fact Newton, immaterial fact, negotiate SOF, unsubstantiated assertions*

**I** The issue is what [Client] can do about [the facts in the SOF he disagrees with] when pleading guilty. **C** Because the disputed fact [goes to an element / is material to sentence / is immaterial], [the case must go to trial / the dispute should be negotiated or resolved by a Newton hearing / the plea can still be accepted].
**R** There is no statutory requirement for an SOF; the practice assists the court in sentencing and in ensuring the accused understands his plea (*Mok Swee Kok v PP* [1994] 3 SLR(R) 134 at [14]; *Biplob Hossain Younus Akan v PP* [2011] 3 SLR 2 at [6]). It must contain all elements of the charge and all facts material to sentence, including aggravating and mitigating facts relied on (*PP v Andrew Koh Weiwen* [2016] SGHC 103 at [16]). The accused need not admit every fact, so long as what he admits contains all ingredients of the offence and what he disputes is immaterial (*Chota bin Abdul Razak v PP* [1991] 1 SLR(R) 501 at [16]).

> **Rule in full**
> - SOF must contain: all the elements of the charge (*Mok Swee Kok* [14]); all relevant facts material to sentence, including aggravating and mitigating facts parties intend to rely on (*Andrew Koh* [16]).
> - Practice: the court directs the Prosecution to furnish the SOF; defence counsel goes through it with the client and explains he must consent to it when pleading; disagreements are raised with the Prosecution and ironed out before the hearing.
> - Disputes that cannot be resolved (CL3 s17):
>   - element of the charge ("I always intended to pay") → trial (s 227);
>   - fact material to sentence ("I was depressed and it lowered my self-control"), not accepted by the Prosecution → Newton hearing (*Ng Chun Hian v PP* [2014] 2 SLR 783 at [24]; *Aniza*);
>   - irrelevant or immaterial fact ("the shirt was turquoise, not blue") → court should accept the plea (*Chota* [16]).
> - *PP v Aniza bte Essa* [2009] 3 SLR(R) 327 at [61]: where the Prosecution objects to unsubstantiated assertions in mitigation, the defence must withdraw them, prove them to the Prosecution's satisfaction, or call evidence (Newton); if unopposed, the court may accept them and give such weight as it thinks fit.

**P** In contact sessions the examiners stressed that the elements are non-negotiable, and that disagreements with the SOF must be based on what the client says happened, not on what helps sentence (Cyril-Exam CS2).

**A** First, list each fact [Client] disputes and classify it: [that he "planned" the theft — material to sentence (premeditation is aggravating)]; [that he took the item dishonestly — an element]; [the colour of his shirt — immaterial]. Second, raise the material disputes with the Prosecution and seek amendment of the SOF before the hearing, supported by [evidence]. Third, if the Prosecution will not amend a material fact, [Client] may plead guilty to an SOF with that fact disputed and ask for a Newton hearing (0-15), bearing the burden if it is extraneous to the Prosecution's case. Fourth, any disputed element means he cannot plead guilty (0-13). **C** Therefore [Client] can plead guilty if the dispute is confined to [non-elemental facts], which I would first try to negotiate out of the SOF.

*Source note: CL3 s14–17 (Mok Swee Kok [14]; Biplob [6]; Andrew Koh [16]; Chota [16]; Aniza [61]; Ng Chun Hian [24]; dispute table); Cyril-Exam CS1–2 notes.*

### pcp-w-0-15 — Newton hearing: disputed facts material to sentence; competing psychiatric reports
*Keywords: Newton hearing, s 228(5) CPC, disputed fact, material to sentence, Ng Chun Hian, Catherine Chan, burden of proof, balance of probabilities, beyond reasonable doubt, psychiatric report, IMH report, private psychiatrist, depression, major depressive disorder, OCD, kleptomania, morbid jealousy, contributory link, mitigation, absurd or obviously untenable*

**I** The issue is how the court will deal with [Client]'s reliance on [Dr X's report that he suffered from depression / OCD] in mitigation, when the Prosecution relies on [the IMH report finding no psychiatric condition]. **C** Because this is a dispute about a fact material to sentence that does not deny an element, the court may convene a Newton hearing under s 228(5) CPC, at which [Client], asserting a fact extraneous to the Prosecution's case, must prove it on a balance of probabilities.
**R** After hearing the mitigation, the court may, of its own motion or on application, hear evidence to determine the truth of matters that may materially affect sentence, and attach such weight as it considers appropriate (s 228(5) CPC). A Newton hearing is held when a fact material to sentence is contested; it is the exception, and the court may decline if the defence case is absurd or obviously untenable (*Ng Chun Hian v PP* [2014] 2 SLR 783 at [24]). The party asserting the fact bears the burden; the Prosecution proves beyond reasonable doubt, the defence on a balance of probabilities (*PP v Chan Yoke Ling Catherine* [2004] SGDC 108 at [47]–[52]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 228(5): after the plea in mitigation the court may (a) at its discretion or on the application of the prosecution or the accused hear any evidence to determine the truth or otherwise of the matters raised which may materially affect the sentence; and (b) attach such weight to the matter as it considers appropriate after hearing the evidence.
> - When (*Ng Chun Hian* [24]): a contested fact material to sentencing; exception rather than norm; may decline if the accused's case is "absurd or obviously untenable".
> - Not available where the plea is qualified (denial of a physical or mental element): a Newton hearing presupposes a valid plea.
> - Procedure: like a trial; witnesses give evidence (here, the two psychiatrists).
> - Burden (*Catherine Chan* [47]–[52]):
>   - on the Prosecution if the fact is "very fundamental" to its case and it would have had to prove it at trial — beyond reasonable doubt;
>   - on the Defence if the fact is extraneous (the Prosecution would not have proved it at trial), is within the accused's knowledge, or the law places the burden on the accused — balance of probabilities.

**P** In the 2026 sample (OCD report disputed by IMH), the answer guide required: the plea is not qualified if the accused accepts he meant to threaten; the OCD is a contested fact material to sentence; Newton hearing; defence bears the burden on a balance of probabilities (*Catherine Chan*).

**A** First, check the plea is not qualified: [Client] accepts [he knew what he was doing / intended the act] and relies on [depression] only as lowering his self-control or explaining his conduct — that is mitigation, not a denial of an element (0-13). [If he says "I did not mean to threaten", that qualifies the plea and must be dropped.] Second, the Prosecution disputes the condition (IMH: none). The fact is extraneous to the Prosecution's case and within [Client]'s knowledge, so [Client] bears the burden on a balance of probabilities. Third, prepare for a Newton hearing: call [Dr X] to explain the diagnosis, its timing ([after her husband's death in July 2023]) and its contributory link to the offence; challenge IMH's report in cross-examination. Fourth, explain the stakes: if proved, the condition may shift the emphasis to rehabilitation and reduce deterrence (0-34), possibly avoiding imprisonment; if not, the mitigation fails and the court sentences on the SOF. **C** Therefore [Client] can rely on [Dr X]'s report, but if the Prosecution maintains its objection there will likely be a Newton hearing at which she must prove the condition on a balance of probabilities.

*Source note: CL3 s4, s16–17, s30–32 (s 228(5); Ng Chun Hian [24]; Catherine Chan [47]–[52]; Aniza); CL6 s7, s9 (mental disorders and deterrence/rehabilitation); SQ guide p124–125 Q1(c); PYP21-CRI Q2.2, PYP22-CRI Q2.1, PYP23-CRI Q2.2, PYP25 Q2.3.*

### pcp-w-0-16 — Charges taken into consideration (s 148 CPC; Lim Hsien Hwei)
*Keywords: TIC, taken into consideration, outstanding offences, s 148 CPC, consent of prosecution and accused, admits the offence, no conviction, uplift, enhance sentence, Mok Ping Wuen Maurice, Lim Hsien Hwei, double jeopardy, disqualification not enhanced, proceeded charges*

**I** The issue is what it means for [Client]'s remaining [two] charges to be "taken into consideration", and how that affects her sentence. **C** With her consent, the court may take the outstanding charges into account when sentencing her on the proceeded charge(s) under s 148 CPC; she will not be convicted of them, and while they usually increase the sentence, the increase is less than if she were convicted of them.
**R** If the accused is found guilty of an offence, the court may, with the consent of the Prosecution and the accused, take into consideration any other outstanding offences that the accused admits to having committed (s 148(1) CPC). TIC charges usually enhance the sentence, but whether to consider them is for the court (*PP v Mok Ping Wuen Maurice* [1998] 2 SLR(R) 439 at [19]); the accused can expect a more favourable overall sentence than if he contested and was convicted of them (*Lim Hsien Hwei v PP* [2014] 3 SLR 15 at [22]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 148(1): on finding the accused guilty of an offence in proceedings by or on behalf of the PP, the court in determining and passing sentence may, with the consent of the prosecution and the accused, take into consideration any other outstanding offences the accused admits to have committed.
> - Procedure: after conviction on the proceeded charges (plea and SOF), the Prosecution applies for the remaining charges to be TIC; the accused's consent is sought and recorded.
> - Effect:
>   - typically enhances the sentence, at the court's discretion (*Mok Ping Wuen* [19]);
>   - more favourable than contesting and being convicted (*Lim Hsien Hwei* [22]);
>   - method (*Lim Hsien Hwei* [25]–[27]): compare (a) the base sentence for the proceeded charges with (b) the sentence had he been convicted of both proceeded and TIC charges; apply to (a) an uplift reflecting a suitably discounted measure of (b);
>   - a TIC offence cannot enhance a type of punishment not prescribed for it (e.g. disqualification) (*Lim Hsien Hwei* [26]–[27]).
> - s 148(4): the TIC must be entered in the court's record; s 148(5): after sentence the accused may not, unless his conviction for the original offence is set aside, be charged or tried for any offence taken into consideration.

**P** In *Lim Hsien Hwei* the High Court held that TIC charges should not be treated as if they were proceeded charges, and the uplift should reflect a discounted measure of the sentence they would otherwise attract (at [25]–[27]).

**A** First, explain the offer: the Prosecution proceeds on [Charges 1–4] and applies to TIC [Charges 5–6]; [Client] must admit the TIC offences and consent. Second, explain the effect: she is convicted and sentenced only on the proceeded charges; the TIC charges may lead the court to increase that sentence (*Mok Ping Wuen*), but by less than separate convictions would (*Lim Hsien Hwei*), and they cannot enhance a type of punishment they do not carry. Third, note that TIC charges also do not add to the s 307 count of imprisonment terms (only proceeded charges are sentenced). Fourth, advise that accepting TIC is usually in her interest if she does in fact admit the offences. **C** Therefore, by consenting to the TIC, [Client] avoids convictions on those charges and is likely to receive a lower overall sentence than if convicted of all of them, though higher than for the proceeded charges alone.

*Source note: CL3 s4, s19–20 (s 148(1); Mok Ping Wuen [19]; Lim Hsien Hwei [22], [25]–[27]); CPC-Text s 148(4)–(5); CL2 s17; PYP21-CRI Q2.1, PYP23-CRI Q2.3, PYP25 Q2.2.*

### pcp-w-0-17 — Antecedents and past conduct relevant to sentence (s 228(2) CPC; Teo Seng Tiong)
*Keywords: antecedents, criminal record, previous conviction, s 228(2) CPC, s 2 CPC criminal record, Teo Seng Tiong, compounded offence, warning, uncharged conduct, Bong Sim Swan Suzanna, PCR r 14(8), PCR r 15(4), disclose antecedents, first offender, victim impact statement*

**I** The issue is which parts of [Client]'s past conduct — [his 2000 theft conviction / a compounded offence / a police warning] — the court may take into account in sentencing. **C** The court will consider his criminal record (s 228(2)(a) CPC) and [presumptively] any compounded offence (*Teo Seng Tiong v PP* [2021] 2 SLR 642), but not a warning; I need not disclose antecedents myself, but must not say anything inconsistent with them (r 14(8) PCR).
**R** The Prosecution's address on sentence may include the accused's criminal records, any victim impact statement and any relevant factors affecting sentence (s 228(2) CPC); "criminal record" means the record of any conviction and certain orders (s 2 CPC). Past conduct is relevant as set out in *Teo Seng Tiong* at [79]–[108]: TIC charges and criminal records count, compounded offences presumptively count, and warnings do not. Defence counsel need not disclose antecedents but must not adduce evidence or make submissions inconsistent with them (r 14(8) PCR); the Prosecution must inform the court of known antecedents (r 15(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 228(2): the address on sentence may include (a) criminal records; (b) any victim impact statement; (c) any relevant factors which may affect sentence.
> - s 2 "criminal record": record of (a) any conviction in any court (or subordinate military court); (b) an order under s 34(2) Misuse of Drugs Act; (c) a supervision order under s 16 Intoxicating Substances Act; (d) an order under s 30(1) Criminal Law (Temporary Provisions) Act; (e) prescribed orders.
> - Past conduct (*Teo Seng Tiong* [79]–[108]):
>   - TIC charges: yes (s 148(1));
>   - criminal record: yes (s 228(2)(a));
>   - compounded offences: yes, presumptively (s 228(2)(c));
>   - warnings: no;
>   - uncharged past conduct that could be an offence: only with sufficient nexus to the offence before the court (*PP v Bong Sim Swan Suzanna* [2020] 2 SLR 1001 at [92]) — not tested.
> - PCR r 14(8): defence need not disclose any previous conviction or antecedent but must not adduce evidence or make submissions inconsistent with it.
