> - PCR r 15(4): the prosecutor must, after conviction and before sentence, inform the court of (a) known previous convictions or antecedents; (b) relevant sentencing factors and precedents; (c) if the accused is unrepresented, known mitigating circumstances.

**P** Lack of antecedents is a neutral factor, not a mitigating one (CL6 s14, citing *Chew Soo Chun v PP* [2016] 2 SLR 78 at [30]) — see 0-35.

**A** First, list [Client]'s past conduct: [a 2000 theft conviction] is a criminal record and will be tendered under s 228(2)(a); [a 2019 compounded offence] presumptively counts, but he may show he compounded for other reasons; [a 2021 stern warning] is irrelevant and should not be referred to. Second, in mitigation, I need not raise the record, but I must not describe him as a "first offender" or of clean record (r 14(8)); I may argue the conviction is old and dissimilar, so of limited weight. **C** Therefore the court may weigh [the conviction and the compounded offence], not the warning, and my mitigation must be consistent with his record.

*Source note: CL3 s22–28 (s 228(2); s 2 "criminal record"; Teo Seng Tiong [79]–[108] table; Bong Sim Swan Suzanna [92]; r 14(8); r 15(4)); CL6 s14 (Chew Soo Chun [30]); PYP23-CRI Q2 (2000 antecedent).*

### pcp-w-0-18 — Address on sentence and the plea in mitigation (s 228(1)–(3) CPC; PCR r 14(2), (7))
*Keywords: plea in mitigation, mitigation plea, address on sentence, s 228(1) CPC, s 228(3) CPC, right of reply, PCR r 14(2), every favourable factor, PCR r 14(7), scandalous allegations, remorse, plead guilty early, first offender, testimonials, character, youth, mental condition, rehabilitation, avoid caning, avoid imprisonment, Chew Soo Chun, Lim Ghim Peow, Praveen*

**I** The issue is what I can put forward in mitigation for [Client] to [avoid imprisonment / caning / reduce the sentence]. **C** I must raise every favourable factor (r 14(2) PCR) — [genuine remorse shown by an early plea, his lack of antecedents and good character shown by testimonials, his mental condition and prospects of rehabilitation] — without qualifying the plea or making scandalous allegations (r 14(7)), and ask for [sentence].
**R** On conviction the Prosecution may address the court on sentence, the court must then hear the plea in mitigation, and the Prosecution has a right of reply (s 228(1)–(3) CPC). Counsel must pursue every reasonable defence and raise every favourable factor (r 14(2) PCR), and must not make any allegation in mitigation that is scandalous or calculated to vilify, insult or annoy (r 14(7)). Mitigating factors include genuine remorse and youth, while lack of antecedents is neutral (*Chew Soo Chun v PP* [2016] 2 SLR 78 at [30]); a mental condition contributing to the offence may make rehabilitation the dominant consideration (*Lim Ghim Peow v PP* [2014] 4 SLR 1287).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 228(1): on conviction the prosecution may, where it thinks fit, address the court on sentence; (2) the address may include criminal records, a victim impact statement and relevant factors; (3) the court must then hear any plea in mitigation and the prosecution has a right of reply; (4) mitigation that materially affects an element → plea rejected (0-13); (5) Newton hearing (0-15).
> - PCR r 14(2): pursue every reasonable defence and raise every favourable factor in accordance with law.
> - PCR r 14(7): in a plea in mitigation, must not make any allegation which is scandalous or intended or calculated to vilify, insult or annoy any person.
> - Factors (CL6 s14): aggravating (premeditation, group offending, intoxication, antecedents); mitigating (genuine remorse, youth); neutral (lack of antecedents) — used to distinguish precedents and adjust benchmarks.
> - Mental conditions (CL6 s7, s9): general and specific deterrence may carry less weight where the offender cannot make proper choices; rehabilitation may take precedence (*Lim Ghim Peow*); rehabilitation is presumptive for young offenders (*Praveen s/o Krishnan v PP* [2018] 3 SLR 1300).
> - Background to collect (Cyril-Exam §2.2–2.3): personal particulars, education, employment, family, antecedents, medical history and reports, testimonials, awards.
> - Mitigation must stay consistent with the SOF and with any antecedents (r 14(8)).

**P** A sentencing court calibrates the sentence from the precedent or benchmark by adjusting for aggravating and mitigating factors — e.g. remorse shown by a guilty plea may justify going below an indicative starting point (CL6 s14, illustration 3).

**A** First, identify the likely sentence and what the Prosecution seeks ([5 days' imprisonment / caning under s 354(2) PC]). Second, marshal the favourable factors: [early plea saving the victim from testifying — genuine remorse]; [no antecedents — neutral but supports character]; [testimonials from employers and colleagues]; [family responsibilities]; [depression contributing to the offence — supports rehabilitation, proved if disputed (0-15)]; [youth]. Third, compare precedents and distinguish those with aggravating features absent here. Fourth, propose a specific sentence ([a fine / short detention order / the lowest imprisonment term]) and, for caning, argue [caning is discretionary for this offence — s 354(2) PC provides imprisonment up to 5 years, or fine, or caning, or any combination — and the facts are at the lowest end]; note that women, men over 50 at the time of caning, and men sentenced to death cannot be caned, though the court may add up to 12 months' imprisonment in lieu (s 325 CPC). Fifth, check nothing in the mitigation denies an element or contradicts the SOF or record. **C** Therefore I would submit a written plea in mitigation (structure at 0-44) asking for [sentence], relying on [factors].

*Source note: CL3 s4, s26–27 (s 228(1)–(4); r 14(2), (7), (8)); CL6 s7, s9, s14 (Chew Soo Chun [30]; Lim Ghim Peow; Praveen); Cyril-Exam §2.2–2.3, §7.2; CPC-Text s 325; PC-Text s 354; PYP23-CRI Q1.3 (avoiding caning on a s 354(2) charge).*

## 0.V Trial I: disclosure

- **Does the CCDC apply to my client's case?**
  - Second Schedule offence tried in a District Court, or any High Court case: yes (ss 159, 212) → [0-19](#pcp-w-0-19)
  - Magistrate's Court: only if both sides consent to opt in (s 159(3)); weigh the gains (statements, exhibits) against filing a CFD → [0-19](#pcp-w-0-19)
  - Can the defence opt out? No — old s 159(2) deleted from 14 Feb 2025 → [0-19](#pcp-w-0-19)
- **What is filed and when?**
  - CFP within 2 weeks of the 1st CCDC (s 162); CFD within 2 weeks of the 2nd (s 165); PSB and defence bundle within 2 weeks of the CFD (s 166) → [0-20](#pcp-w-0-20)
  - Client's statements: in the CFP if relied on; every other statement in the PSB → [0-20](#pcp-w-0-20)
  - Audiovisual statement: defence may view, not copy; transcript provided → [0-20](#pcp-w-0-20)
- **Can the client leave allegations out of the CFD to "surprise" the Prosecution?**
  - Inferences for omissions or inconsistency (s 169); CFD used to cross-examine (s 258A) → [0-21](#pcp-w-0-21)
- **Prosecution holds CCTV or a statement it won't use — can we get it?**
  - Kadar: unused material that undermines the Prosecution or helps the Defence, prima facie credible and relevant → [0-22](#pcp-w-0-22)
  - Statement of a material witness the Prosecution won't call (bystander, alibi witness): *Nabill* — disclose whether favourable, neutral or adverse → [0-23](#pcp-w-0-23)
- **Prosecution failed to disclose — what happens?**
  - Adjournment, adverse inference, retrial, or acquittal only if the conviction is unsafe (*Lim Hong Liang*) → [0-24](#pcp-w-0-24)

### pcp-w-0-19 — When the CCDC applies; opting in (ss 159, 212 CPC)
*Keywords: CCDC, criminal case disclosure conference, when does CCDC apply, s 159 CPC, Second Schedule CPC, District Court, s 8 CPC, 10 years, Magistrate's Court, opt in, consent, s 159(3), High Court, s 212 CPC, opt out, Case for the Prosecution, advantages of CCDC*

**I** The issue is whether the criminal case disclosure procedure applies to [Client]'s case in the [Magistrate's / District] Court, and whether [he] should opt in. **C** [The CCDC does not apply automatically because the case is in the Magistrate's Court], but it may apply if both the Prosecution and Defence consent under s 159(3) CPC; given that [Client] cannot recall his long statement, opting in is advisable because it obliges the Prosecution to disclose his statements and its case.
**R** The CCDC applies to offences specified in the Second Schedule to the CPC that are to be tried in a District Court (s 159 CPC); District Courts try offences with a maximum imprisonment term not exceeding 10 years or fine-only offences (s 8 CPC). It applies to every case tried in the High Court (s 212 CPC). A case that does not qualify can still proceed under the CCDC if both Prosecution and Defence consent (s 159(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 159: CCDC applies to (a) offences specified in the Second Schedule, (b) to be tried in a District Court.
> - s 8: District Courts may try offences whose maximum imprisonment does not exceed 10 years, or fine-only offences.
> - s 212: CCDC applies to cases tried in the High Court.
> - s 159(3): a case outside s 159 may be dealt with under the CCDC if both Prosecution and Defence consent (opt in).
> - No opting out: the old s 159(2), which let the defence tell the court on or before the first CCDC that the accused did not want the procedure, was deleted with effect from 14 Feb 2025 (Act 5 of 2024). Where s 159(1) applies, the CCDC now applies. Conflict: Cyril-Exam §3.1 and CS1 (2025 sitting) still describe opting out; the current CPC does not allow it.
> - What opting in gives the defence (CL4 s7, s10–15): the CFP (summary of facts, witnesses, exhibits, the accused's statements relied on) and the PSB (every other statement of the accused, documentary exhibits, criminal record) — at the price of filing a CFD (s 165), which can be used against him (s 258A) (0-21).

**P** Cyril's contact-session notes record the examiners' view that the CCDC is the "cornerstone" of criminal practice once a client claims trial and "probably going to be part of [the] exam" (Cyril-Exam CS1).

**A** First, locate the case: [s 354(1) PC in the Magistrate's Court] — the CCDC does not apply automatically. Second, weigh opting in: [Client] cannot recall what he said in his long statement and has no copy; under the CCDC the Prosecution must disclose it in the CFP or the PSB (s 162(1)(e); s 166(1)(a)), along with its witnesses and exhibits, so the defence can prepare properly. The cost is that [Client] must file a CFD setting out his defence, which can be used to cross-examine him if his evidence departs from it (s 258A), so his instructions must be firm. Third, write to the Prosecution proposing to opt in under s 159(3); if it refuses, rely on Kadar disclosure (0-22). **C** Therefore I would advise [Client] to opt in to the CCDC under s 159(3), subject to the Prosecution's consent.

*Source note: CL4 s7–9 (ss 159, 8, 212, 159(3)), s10–15, s18; SSO-CPC s 159(1)–(3) (s 159(2) deleted wef 14 Feb 2025); Cyril-Exam §3.1 and CS1 notes; PYP25 Q1.2 ("Cite the relevant CPC provisions"; Magistrate's Court; can't recall long statement).*

### pcp-w-0-20 — CCDC documents and timelines: CFP, CFD, PSB and defence bundle (ss 161–167 CPC)
*Keywords: Case for the Prosecution, CFP, s 162 CPC, Case for the Defence, CFD, s 165 CPC, Prosecution's Supplementary Bundle, PSB, s 166 CPC, Defence Bundle of Exhibits, defence supplementary bundle, timelines, 2 weeks, s 161, s 163, s 167, accused's statements, audiovisual recording, view not copy, transcript, list of witnesses, list of exhibits, criminal records*

**I** The issue is what each side must file under the CCDC, by when, and how [Client] will obtain [his statements]. **C** The Prosecution must serve its Case for the Prosecution within two weeks of the first CCDC, including any statement of [Client] it relies on; [Client] must serve his Case for the Defence within two weeks of the second CCDC; and within two weeks after that the Prosecution must serve its Supplementary Bundle with every other statement of [Client].
**R** The CFP must contain the charge, a summary of facts, lists of witnesses and exhibits, and the accused's written statements (and a list and transcripts of audiovisual statements) the Prosecution intends to adduce (s 162(1) CPC). The CFD must contain a summary of the defence and supporting facts, lists of witnesses and exhibits, and any objection to the CFP with its factual and legal basis (s 165(1)). The PSB must contain every other statement of the accused relating to the charges, the documentary exhibits and his criminal record (s 166(1)); the defence serves its exhibits (s 166(4)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 162(1) CFP: (a) charge(s) to be proceeded with; (b) summary of facts in support; (c) list of witnesses; (d) list of exhibits; (e) any written statement of the accused recorded by a law enforcement officer that the Prosecution intends to adduce; (f) list of audiovisual statements of the accused it intends to adduce; (g) their transcripts. s 162(2): if requested, the defence may view the audiovisual recording at a police station (view, not copy).
> - s 165(1) CFD: (a) summary of the defence and facts in support; (b) list of defence witnesses; (c) list of defence exhibits; (d) if objecting to any issue of fact or law in the CFP, (i) the nature of the objection, (ii) the issue of fact on which evidence will be produced, (iii) the points of law in support.
> - s 166(1) PSB: (a) every other written statement of the accused relating to the charges proceeded with; (aa) transcripts of other audiovisual statements; (b) each documentary exhibit in the s 162(1)(d) list; (c) criminal records (on payment). s 166(2): on request, the defence may view (not copy) the audiovisual recordings of those statements. s 166(2A) (wef 26 May 2025): a documentary exhibit showing an individual's genital or anal region or a woman's breasts is not copied to the defence, which may view it at a police station or prescribed place. s 166(3): if no CFD is served, the prosecution need not serve the PSB items and may still use them at trial. s 166(4): defence serves its bundle of exhibits listed in the CFD; s 166(5): the two obligations are independent.
> - Timelines:
>   - 1st CCDC: CFP not later than 2 weeks from the 1st CCDC (s 161(2)); 2nd CCDC not earlier than 7 days after the CFP (s 161(4));
>   - 2nd CCDC: CFD not later than 2 weeks from the 2nd CCDC (s 163(1)); PSB within 2 weeks of service of the CFD (s 166(1)); defence bundle within 2 weeks of the CFD (s 166(4));
>   - 3rd CCDC: any time after the CFD is filed and PSB served (s 163(2)); trial dates fixed (s 167).
> - Accused need not personally attend the CCDC; documents may be served on counsel (Cyril-Exam §3.7, citing s 167).

**P** Unused statements favourable to the defence can be adduced by the defence at trial once received in the PSB (CL4 s15); a statement withheld from both may be sought under Kadar (CL4 s21).

**A** First, explain to [Client] the sequence: at the first CCDC the Prosecution is given two weeks to serve the CFP; if it relies on [his long statement containing an admission], that statement will be in it; if not, it comes later in the PSB. Second, his CFD is due two weeks after the second CCDC; it must state his defence ([accident / alibi]) and the facts supporting it, and any objection to the CFP (e.g. [that his statement was involuntary — 0-32]). Because it can be used to cross-examine him (s 258A), he must approve it in writing. Third, the PSB follows within two weeks of the CFD with any other statements he gave; review them for anything favourable. **C** Therefore [Client] will receive [his statements] in the [CFP / PSB] and must file a full and accurate CFD within two weeks of the second CCDC.

*Source note: CL4 s10–18 (ss 161–167; contents; timelines table; audiovisual statements); SSO-CPC s 166(1)–(3) (s 166(2A) added wef 26 May 2025); Cyril-Exam §3.2–3.8; PYP23-CRI Q1.2, PYP25 Q1.2, Q1.4.*

### pcp-w-0-21 — Non-compliance and inconsistency in the CCDC: inferences, DNAA and use of the CFD (ss 169, 258A CPC)
*Keywords: s 169 CPC, adverse inference, failure to serve, inconsistent with CFD, case put at trial inconsistent, discharge not amounting to acquittal, DNAA, s 258A CPC, CFD admissible, cross-examine on CFD, impeach credit, take the Prosecution by surprise, omit allegations from CFD, ambush, new defence at trial*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can leave [his allegations of threats and oppression] out of his Case for the Defence so as to take the Prosecution by surprise at trial. **C** He should not: the court may draw an adverse inference where the case put forward at trial differs from or was omitted from the CFD (s 169(1) CPC), and the CFD itself can be used to cross-examine and impeach him (s 258A CPC).
**R** The court may draw such inference as it thinks fit where there is non-disclosure, where the case put at trial is inconsistent with the CFP or CFD, or where an exhibit is not served (s 169(1) CPC). Failure by the Prosecution to serve the CFP or required items may result in a discharge not amounting to an acquittal (s 169(2)). A filed CFD is admissible at trial and may be used to cross-examine the accused or impeach his credit (s 258A CPC).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 169(1) (current text): the court may draw such inference as it thinks fit if
>   - (a) the prosecution fails to serve the CFP, or the defence fails to serve the CFD after the CFP is served; (aa) either fails to file its Case in court;
>   - (b) the CFP or CFD lacks any item required by s 162(1) or s 165(1);
>   - (ba) the prosecution fails to serve any statement, transcript, exhibit or record required by s 166; (bb) the defence fails to serve its listed documentary exhibits in its possession;
>   - (c) a party's case at trial differs from or is inconsistent with its filed Case.
> - s 163(3) (wef 14 Feb 2025): if the defence does not file or serve the CFD, the accused may be cross-examined on why not, and the court may draw an inference under s 169(1)(a) or (aa).
> - s 169(2): the court may order a discharge not amounting to an acquittal if the Prosecution (a) fails to serve the CFP in time (s 161), (aa) fails to file it in court, (b) serves a CFP missing required items (s 162), or (c) fails to serve the s 166 items in time — rare in practice.
> - s 258A(1): the CFD is (a) admissible at the trial and (b) if the accused testifies, may be used in cross-examination and to impeach his credit. s 258A(2)–(3): in a joint trial the court may consider one accused's CFD against a co-accused, but may refuse where its prejudicial effect on that co-accused outweighs its probative value. Conflict: Cyril-Exam §3.8 states the prejudice proviso generally; the CPC text confines it to co-accused (s 258A(3)).
> - Practice (CL4 s18): take thorough instructions; obtain the client's written clearance of the CFD before filing.

**P** In the 2021 and 2022 papers (Bill; Alex) the "take the Prosecution by surprise" part was paired with the voluntariness challenge (0-32); the CFD must state the objection to the statement's admissibility with its factual basis (s 165(1)(d)).

**A** First, explain that [the allegations — no caution, 8 hours handcuffed, the IO's threat] are an objection to the Prosecution's case, which s 165(1)(d) requires the CFD to state with the facts and law relied on. Second, if they are omitted and raised for the first time at trial, the court may infer under s 169(1) that they were recently invented, and the Prosecution may cross-examine [Client] on the omission under s 258A, damaging his credit and the ancillary hearing (0-32). Third, the "surprise" gains little, since the Prosecution would in any event have to prove voluntariness beyond reasonable doubt once the issue is raised. **C** Therefore I would advise [Client] to set out the allegations fully in his CFD.

*Source note: CL4 s12 (s 165(1)(d)), s17–18 (s 169(1)–(2); s 258A); SSO-CPC ss 163(3), 169(1)–(2) (amended wef 14 Feb 2025), 258A(1)–(3); Cyril-Exam §3.5, §3.8; PYP21-CRI Q1.2, PYP22-CRI Q1.2.*

### pcp-w-0-22 — Kadar disclosure of unused material (Kadar I and II; ss 221A, 221D CPC)
*Keywords: Kadar, Muhammad bin Kadar, unused material, disclose, undermine the Prosecution's case, strengthen the Defence, prima facie credible and relevant, line of inquiry, CCTV footage, witness statement, inconsistent statement, Kadar II, no duty to search, continuing duty, timing, s 221A CPC, s 221D CPC, codified 2025, Prosecution refuses to hand over*

**I** The issue is whether the Prosecution must disclose [the CCTV footage / the statement of Kathy] that it does not intend to use. **C** Yes: if [the footage] tends to undermine the Prosecution's case or strengthen [Client]'s and is likely admissible and prima facie credible and relevant to guilt, it must be disclosed under *Muhammad bin Kadar v PP* [2011] 3 SLR 1205, now codified in ss 221A and 221D CPC, [within two weeks of the start of the CCDC / before trial].
**R** The Prosecution must disclose unused material that tends to undermine its case or strengthen the defence, where the material is (a) likely to be admissible and might reasonably be regarded as prima facie credible and relevant to guilt or innocence, or (b) exceptionally, likely to be inadmissible but would provide a real chance of pursuing a line of inquiry leading to such material (*Kadar I* at [113], [117]). Neutral or adverse material need not be disclosed (*Kadar I* at [113]), and there is no duty to search for material outside the Prosecution's knowledge (*Kadar II* [2011] 4 SLR 791 at [14]). The obligations were codified in 2025 (ss 221A, 221D CPC).

> **Rule in full**
> - *Kadar I* [113]: disclose unused material that tends to undermine the Prosecution's case or strengthen the Defence's case, where it
>   - (a) is likely to be admissible and might reasonably be regarded as prima facie credible and relevant to guilt or innocence; or
>   - (b) is likely to be inadmissible but would provide a real (not fanciful) chance of pursuing a line of inquiry leading to material that is likely admissible and prima facie credible and relevant — an "exceptional category" ([117]).
> - Not required: unused material that is neutral or adverse to the accused ([113]).
> - *Kadar II* [14]: the duty covers material within the Prosecution's knowledge; no duty to search.
> - Examples: CCTV footage; witness statements, including a prosecution witness's statement inconsistent with his testimony (*Nabill* [54]); the accused's own statements if not already in the CFP and PSB.
> - Timing (*Kadar I* [113]): where the CCDC applies, within 2 weeks from the start of the CCDC (i.e. with the CFP); otherwise, at the latest before trial; continuing until the case is disposed of, including appeals. Late disclosure may lead to an adjournment ([121]).
> - Rationale ([200]): the Prosecution's duty to the court and public that only the guilty are convicted.
> - Codified 2025: s 221A (esp. paras (c) and (d)) and s 221D CPC.

**P** In *Kadar I* the Court of Appeal held that the Prosecution's duty arises because it owes "to the court and to the wider public" a duty to ensure only the guilty are convicted and all relevant material is before the court (at [200]).

**A** First, identify the material: [CCTV the Prosecution holds but will not use, which Client says shows he did not touch Ms Bebe] / [Kelly's statement describing a ponytailed, tattooed man, unlike Alex]. Second, apply the test: it [tends to undermine the identification / strengthen the defence], is likely admissible as [real evidence / a witness's statement] and is prima facie credible and relevant to guilt, so it falls within *Kadar I* [113](a). Third, timing: [CCDC applies — it should come with the CFP; CCDC does not apply — before trial]; the duty continues. Fourth, write to the Prosecution requesting disclosure under *Kadar* and ss 221A and 221D CPC; if it refuses, raise it with the court and warn that non-disclosure may lead to an adjournment, adverse inference or a conviction being set aside (0-24). [If the material is neutral or adverse, Kadar does not require disclosure.] **C** Therefore the Prosecution must disclose [the footage] to [Client], and I would request it in writing now.

*Source note: CL4 s5, s19–28 (Kadar I [113], [117], [121], [200]; Kadar II [14]; Nabill [54]; ss 221A, 221D); PYP21-CRI Q1.1, PYP22-CRI Q1.1, PYP25 Q1.3.*

### pcp-w-0-23 — Disclosure of material witnesses' statements (Nabill; Roshdi; ss 221A(2), 221B CPC)
*Keywords: Nabill, Muhammad Nabill bin Mohd Fuad, Roshdi bin Abdullah Altway, material witness, additional disclosure obligations, statement of witness not called, alibi witness, father cannot confirm alibi, confirm or contradict defence, favourable neutral or adverse, redaction, no duty to record statements, s 221A(2), s 221B, when in doubt disclose*

**I** The issue is whether the Prosecution must disclose the statement of [Michael, Alex's father], whom it will not call. **C** Yes: [Michael] is a material witness, since his evidence can be expected to confirm or contradict [Alex]'s alibi, so his statement must be disclosed under *Muhammad Nabill bin Mohd Fuad v PP* [2020] 1 SLR 984, now codified in ss 221A(2) and 221B CPC, whether it is favourable, neutral or adverse.
**R** The Prosecution must disclose statements given to the police by a material witness — one who can be expected to confirm or contradict the accused's defence in material respects — whom it does not call (*Nabill* at [4]). Unlike Kadar, this applies whether the statement is favourable, neutral or adverse, and requires no prior assessment of credibility (*Nabill* at [41]). The obligation is triggered when the accused raises a defence and the Prosecution holds such a statement (*Roshdi bin Abdullah Altway v PP* [2022] 1 SLR 535 at [155]); there is no duty to record statements from all material witnesses (*Roshdi* at [67], [166]).

> **Rule in full**
> - *Nabill* [4]: "material witness" = one who can be expected to confirm or, conversely, contradict an accused's defence in material respects.
> - Scope: statements of witnesses other than the accused, recorded by the Prosecution, who will not be called; position for material witnesses who are called left open ([50]).
> - Differences from Kadar (*Nabill* [41]): (a) disclose whether favourable, neutral or adverse (redaction of irrelevant parts may be sought); (b) no prior assessment of prima facie credibility and relevance.
> - Basis ([44]–[45]): the Prosecution may misjudge the defence; the accused needs the information to decide whether to call the witness.
> - Trigger (*Roshdi* [155]): (a) the accused raises a defence; (b) the Prosecution has a statement of a witness who may materially confirm or contradict it. Process for identifying material witnesses at [156]–[161].
> - No duty to record statements from a new material witness (*Roshdi* [67], [166]), but the Prosecution risks failing its evidential burden ([167]); consequences of breach depend on the facts ([168]–[169]).
> - Timing (*Nabill* [50]–[53]): with the CFP if the CCDC applies, otherwise before trial and before the accused testifies; continuing duty.
> - Codified 2025: s 221A(2), s 221B(b) read with s 221B(2), s 221D CPC. Upshot: "when in doubt, disclose" (*Nabill* [48]).

**P** In *Nabill* the Court of Appeal held it would be "an intolerable outcome" if the court were deprived of potentially exculpatory evidence because the Prosecution misjudged its significance in good faith (at [44]).

**A** First, identify the defence raised: [alibi — at home since 6pm]. Second, identify witnesses whose statements bear on it: [Michael, who says he sleeps soundly and cannot confirm the alibi]. Though his statement may be neutral or even unhelpful, he is a material witness because his evidence can be expected to confirm or contradict the alibi; so *Nabill* requires disclosure regardless of whether it helps. Third, contrast [Kelly, a bystander whose description differs from Alex]: her statement is favourable unused material and falls under *Kadar* (0-22) — and, if her evidence bears on identity as part of the defence, under *Nabill* too. Fourth, timing: with the CFP or before trial; request in writing. Fifth, use the disclosure to decide whether to call [Michael]. **C** Therefore the Prosecution must disclose [Michael]'s statement under *Nabill*, and I would request it before trial.

*Source note: CL4 s29–37 (Nabill [4], [41], [44]–[45], [48], [50]–[53]; Roshdi [67], [155]–[161], [166]–[169]; ss 221A(2), 221B, 221D); CL5 s10; PYP21-CRI Q1.1, PYP22-CRI Q1.1.*

### pcp-w-0-24 — Consequences of a disclosure breach (Lim Hong Liang)
*Keywords: Kadar breach, non-disclosure, consequences, Lim Hong Liang, poison pill, material irregularity, failure of justice, conviction unsafe, acquittal, retrial, s 390(1)(b)(i), adjournment, adverse inference against Prosecution, prejudice, late disclosure*

**I** The issue is what consequence follows from the Prosecution's failure to disclose [material]. **C** The breach does not automatically overturn the conviction; the court will balance the effect of the breach and the prejudice caused, and may [adjourn / draw an adverse inference / order a retrial], acquitting only if the non-disclosed evidence strongly points to acquittal so that the conviction is unsafe (*Lim Hong Liang v PP* [2021] 5 SLR 626).
**R** A Kadar breach is not a "poison pill"; a conviction is overturned only if the non-disclosure causes a material irregularity occasioning a failure of justice (*Lim Hong Liang* at [21]; *Kadar I* at [120]). The court balances the effect on the evidence, the prejudice to the accused, whether it can be remedied, the causes of the breach, and the broader objectives of justice (*Lim Hong Liang* at [22]). Late disclosure may warrant an adjournment (*Kadar I* at [121]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Factors (*Lim Hong Liang* [22]):
>   - the effect of the breach on the evidence against the accused;
>   - how the breach prejudiced the accused (e.g. a lost line of cross-examination of a witness who cannot be recalled may be irremediable — [32]);
>   - whether steps can be or have been taken to remedy the prejudice;
>   - the causes of the breach, including the Prosecution's conduct (non-disclosure alone, without doubt on the integrity of the process, may not warrant acquittal — [36]);
>   - the broader objectives of the administration of justice, including fairness to victims.
> - Outcomes:
>   - conviction unsafe where the non-disclosed evidence is clearly admissible and strongly points to acquittal, with a causal link to the wrongful conviction ([28], [30]) → acquittal;
>   - otherwise possibly retrial (s 390(1)(b)(i) CPC) ([23]);
>   - other options ([24]): e.g. an adverse inference against the Prosecution; adjournment for late disclosure (*Kadar I* [121]).
> - Upshot (*Lim Hong Liang* [36]): acquittals for a Kadar breach should not be given too readily, especially for serious crimes.

**P** In *Lim Hong Liang* the court stressed that rendering acquittals too readily for a Kadar breach "will ill serve the needs of society, particularly the victims" (at [36]).

**A** First, identify the stage: [before trial — seek disclosure and, if late, an adjournment]; [during trial — seek an adverse inference or recall of a witness]; [on appeal — argue the conviction is unsafe or seek a retrial]. Second, weigh the factors: [the CCTV would have shown X, directly contradicting the complainant (strong effect); the complainant has left Singapore (irremediable prejudice); the Prosecution knew of it]. Third, conclude on the remedy. **C** Therefore [Client] should [seek an adjournment and disclosure now / argue on appeal that the conviction is unsafe and should be set aside or, failing that, a retrial ordered].

*Source note: CL4 s25–27 (Kadar I [120]–[121]; Lim Hong Liang [21]–[24], [28], [30], [32], [36]; s 390(1)(b)(i)), s37.*

## 0.VI Trial II: the trial process

- **How does the trial run?**
  - Charge read, plea; Prosecution opens, calls witnesses (EIC, cross, re-exam); put your case (Browne v Dunn) → [0-25](#pcp-w-0-25)
  - Prosecution doesn't call a witness — inference? (s 116(g) EA); silence and CCDC inferences → [0-26](#pcp-w-0-26)
- **Close of Prosecution's case: can we get the charges dismissed?**
  - No case to answer: is there some evidence, not inherently incredible, on each element (*Haw Tua Taw*)? Low threshold; don't submit for the sake of it → [0-27](#pcp-w-0-27)
  - Court may alter the charge before calling on the defence → [0-28](#pcp-w-0-28)
  - Can we appeal the ruling admitting the confession now? No interlocutory appeal (*Knight Glenn*) → [0-38](#pcp-w-0-38)
- **Defence case: should the client testify?**
  - Accused first, on oath, cross-examined (s 291(1)); I must advise whether to testify or stay silent (r 14(5)(a)(ii)) → [0-29](#pcp-w-0-29)
- **Recall a witness / rebuttal** → [0-30](#pcp-w-0-30)
- **Witness says something different from his police statement / "cannot remember"**
  - Impeach: s 147 EA cross-examination on the statement, then s 157(c); serious discrepancy needed → [0-31](#pcp-w-0-31)
- **Client's statement contains a confession, but he says he was threatened / not cautioned / held for hours**
  - TIPO: inadmissible if not voluntary (s 258); ancillary hearing (s 279); Prosecution proves voluntariness BRD → [0-32](#pcp-w-0-32)
- **Closing and judgment; counsel can't attend** → [0-33](#pcp-w-0-33)

### pcp-w-0-25 — Order of trial and the case for the Prosecution (s 230 CPC; Browne v Dunn)
*Keywords: trial procedure, s 230 CPC, claim trial, charge read, opening statement, examination-in-chief, cross-examination, re-examination, s 140(3) EA, Browne v Dunn, put the case, offer witnesses to defence, exhibits, s 263 CPC report*

**I** The issue is how the trial of [Client] will proceed, and what defence counsel must do during the Prosecution's case. **C** The trial follows s 230 CPC; during the Prosecution's case I must cross-examine each Prosecution witness and put [Client]'s case to any witness whose evidence he contradicts (rule in *Browne v Dunn*).
**R** At trial the charge is read and explained and the plea taken; if the accused claims trial the court hears the case (s 230(1)(a)–(c) CPC). The Prosecution may open and then calls its witnesses, each examined in chief, cross-examined and re-examined (s 230(1)(d)–(e)); re-examination is confined to matters arising in cross-examination (s 140(3) EA). A party who intends to contradict a witness must put the substance of the contradiction to the witness in cross-examination (*Browne v Dunn* (1893) 6 R 67).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 230(1) sequence: (a)–(c) charge read and explained; plea; if he refuses to plead, does not plead or claims trial, court proceeds; (d) Prosecution's opening statement (nature of offence, evidence proposed); (e) Prosecution witnesses examined; (f)–(m) close of Prosecution's case and no-case submission (0-27); (n)–(s) defence (0-29); (t) rebuttal (0-30); (u)–(v) closing; (w)–(x) judgment (0-33).
> - Each witness: examination-in-chief, cross-examination, re-examination (only to clarify matters raised in cross, s 140(3) EA).
> - *Browne v Dunn*: to contradict a witness, put the substance of the contradictory evidence to him so he can comment.
> - After its case the Prosecution may offer its remaining witnesses to the defence; note *Nabill* disclosure of their statements (0-23).
> - Cyril-Exam §5.1: exhibits are admitted through an appropriate witness; a report by a qualified person may be used as evidence without calling him unless the court or a party requires him (s 263(1) CPC, confirmed in CPC-Text).

**P** Failure to put the defence case to a Prosecution witness invites the submission that the defence account is an afterthought, which weakens [Client]'s evidence when he testifies (CL5 s9, s18).

**A** First, before trial identify the Prosecution witnesses whose evidence [Client] disputes ([the complainant on whether the touching was deliberate; the IO on the recording of the statement]). Second, in cross-examination put [Client]'s version to each: [that the contact was accidental when he bent down]; [that the IO threatened him]. Third, note what the Prosecution does not call, and whether *Nabill* or s 116(g) EA helps (0-23, 0-26). **C** Therefore the defence must cross-examine fully and put [Client]'s case to each relevant Prosecution witness.

*Source note: CL5 s3–10 (s 230(1)(a)–(e); Browne v Dunn; s 140(3) EA; offering witnesses); Cyril-Exam §5.1; CPC-Text s 263(1).*

### pcp-w-0-26 — Adverse inferences at trial: witnesses not called, CCDC, the accused's silence
*Keywords: adverse inference, s 116 illustration (g) EA, failure to call witness, evidence not produced, Satli bin Masot, Beh Chew Boo, s 169 CPC, s 258A CPC, accused remains silent, refuses to give evidence, s 291 CPC, s 261 CPC, failure to mention fact in statement, inference from silence*

**I** The issue is whether the court may draw an adverse inference from [the Prosecution's failure to call X / Client's failure to give evidence / Client's omission of his defence from his statement or CFD]. **C** [The court may presume that evidence a party could produce but withholds would be unfavourable to it (s 116 illus (g) EA), but only where the witness is material and the case is not otherwise proved; inferences may also be drawn from CCDC non-compliance (s 169 CPC), from the accused's failure to mention, when charged or told he may be prosecuted, a fact he later relies on (s 261 CPC), and from his refusal to give evidence when called on (s 291(3) CPC).]
**R** The court may presume that evidence which could be and is not produced would, if produced, be unfavourable to the person who withholds it (s 116 illus (g) EA). No inference arises where the witness was not material or the case was sufficiently proved by other independent evidence (*Satli bin Masot v PP*), but it is likely where the witness was readily available and central to the party's case (*Beh Chew Boo v PP*). The court may also draw inferences from failures and inconsistencies under the CCDC (s 169 CPC) and use the CFD to cross-examine (s 258A).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 116 illus (g) EA: evidence which could be and is not produced would, if produced, be unfavourable to the person who withholds it (Cyril-Exam §5.1.1).
>   - Not every failure to call a witness triggers it: not where the witness is not material or indispensable and the case is proved by other independent evidence (*Satli bin Masot*);
>   - likely where the witness was readily available and a central figure in the party's case theory (*Beh Chew Boo*).
> - CCDC: s 169(1) inferences; s 258A use of the CFD (0-21).
> - Accused's silence (CL-LP item 6 lists "adverse inferences"; the slides give no text, so the CPC text is quoted):
>   - s 261(1): where the accused, on being charged or informed that he may be prosecuted, failed to mention a fact he later relies on in his defence, which he could reasonably have been expected to mention, the court may, in deciding (a) committal, (b) whether there is a case to answer, and (c) guilt, draw such inferences as appear proper, and the failure may corroborate other evidence against him;
>   - s 291(3): if the accused, after being called on to give evidence (or after saying he will), refuses to be sworn, or without good cause refuses to answer a question, the court may draw such inferences as appear proper in deciding guilt;
>   - s 291(4): the accused is not compelled to testify and is not in contempt for refusing; s 291(6): (3) does not apply if his physical or mental condition makes it undesirable to call him.

**P** The s 23 cautioned statement is where an accused should mention his defence: an alibi or explanation raised for the first time at trial invites an inference under s 261 (CL1 s8; CPC-Text s 261). The *Satli* and *Beh Chew Boo* contrast is from Cyril's notes, which give no citations.

**A** First, identify whose omission it is: [the Prosecution did not call the cashier who witnessed the incident] — argue that she was available and central, so the court should presume her evidence would have been unfavourable (s 116(g); *Beh Chew Boo*); the Prosecution will reply that the case is proved by [CCTV], so no inference (*Satli*). Second, for the defence: warn [Client] that if he declines to testify when called on (s 291(3)), departs from his CFD (s 169), or relies on a fact he did not mention when charged (s 261), the court may draw an inference against him, which bears on the advice whether to testify (0-29). **C** Therefore the court [may / is unlikely to] draw an adverse inference against [party] on these facts.

*Source note: CL-LP p4 item 6 ("Adverse inferences"); CPC-Text ss 261, 291(3)–(6); CL4 s17–18 (ss 169, 258A); CL5 s41 (r 14(5)(a)(ii)); Cyril-Exam §5.1.1 (s 116(g) EA; Satli bin Masot; Beh Chew Boo — secondary, no citations).*

### pcp-w-0-27 — Submission of no case to answer (s 230(1)(f)–(k) CPC; Haw Tua Taw)
*Keywords: no case to answer, submission of no case, close of Prosecution's case, dismiss the charges, prima facie case, Haw Tua Taw, some evidence not inherently incredible, each element, Ong Lee Koon, Wong Wee Keong, discharge amounting to acquittal, s 230(1)(j), s 230(1)(k), call on accused to give defence, standard allocution*

**I** The issue is whether, at the close of the Prosecution's case, [Client] can ask the court to dismiss the charges, and whether he is likely to succeed. **C** [Client] may submit that there is no case to answer (s 230(1)(f) CPC), but the threshold is low; since there is [some evidence, not inherently incredible, on each element — including the admitted confession], the court is likely to call on him to give his defence.
**R** At the close of the Prosecution's case the defence may submit that there is no case to answer, and the Prosecution may reply (s 230(1)(f) CPC). The question is one of law: whether there is some evidence, not inherently incredible, which if accepted as accurate would establish each essential element of the offence (*Haw Tua Taw v PP* [1981–1982] SLR(R) 133 at [17]). If there is no such case the court must order a discharge amounting to an acquittal (s 230(1)(k)); if there is, it must call on the accused to give his defence (s 230(1)(j)). A submission should not be made for its own sake (*Ong Lee Koon v PP* [1995] 1 SLR(R) 682 at [19]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 230(1)(f): defence may invite the court to dismiss on the ground of no case to answer; Prosecution may reply.
> - Test (*Haw Tua Taw* [17]): whether there is some evidence (not inherently incredible) which, if accepted as accurate, would establish each essential element; a prima facie case; low threshold.
> - Guiding propositions (*Wong Wee Keong*, per Cyril-Exam §5.2): accept evidence of primary fact as true unless inherently incredible or wholly discredited; draw only reasonable inferences; consider the totality.
> - s 230(1)(k): no prima facie case → discharge amounting to an acquittal.
> - s 230(1)(j): prima facie case → call on the accused to give his defence (standard allocution, CL5 s15).
> - s 230(1)(g): the court may alter or frame a new charge before calling on the defence (0-28).
> - *Ong Lee Koon* [19]: do not make the submission just for the sake of it.

**P** The standard allocution under s 230(1)(m) tells the accused the court finds "some evidence, not inherently incredible, that satisfies each and every element" and calls on him to give evidence (CL5 s15).

**A** First, list the elements of [s 392 robbery by night] and the evidence on each: [DNA and fingerprints on the balaclava and dagger; the admitted confession]. Each element is supported by evidence that is not inherently incredible, so a prima facie case exists. Second, the submission is therefore unlikely to succeed, and making it without basis is discouraged (*Ong Lee Koon*). Third, the other courses: when called on, [Client] may plead guilty (s 230(1)(n)(i)) or give his defence, testify or remain silent (0-29), mindful of possible inferences (0-26). Fourth, the ruling admitting his confession cannot be appealed now; it may be challenged in an appeal after conviction (0-38). **C** Therefore [Client] is unlikely to obtain a dismissal at this stage and should prepare to [testify / consider a plea].

*Source note: CL5 s12–16 (s 230(1)(f)–(m); Haw Tua Taw [17]; Ong Lee Koon [19]; allocution); Cyril-Exam §5.2 (Wong Wee Keong — secondary); CL7 s4 (no interlocutory appeal); PYP21-CRI Q1.3.*

### pcp-w-0-28 — Altering the charge or framing a new charge at trial (s 230(1)(g); ss 128–131 CPC)
*Keywords: amend charge, alter charge, frame new charge, s 230(1)(g) CPC, s 128 CPC, s 129 CPC, s 130, s 131 CPC, recall witnesses after amendment, Tan Khee Wan Iris, not ready to proceed, adjourn, new trial, prejudice*

**I** The issue is whether the court may alter the charge against [Client] at the close of the Prosecution's case, and how [Client] is protected. **C** The court may alter the charge or frame a new one before calling on the defence (s 230(1)(g) CPC), following ss 128–131 CPC: the altered charge must be explained and a plea taken, [Client] may say he is not ready, and both sides may recall witnesses.
**R** Before calling on the accused to give his defence, the court may alter the charge or frame a new charge, following the procedure in ss 128–131 CPC (s 230(1)(g)). If the charge is altered, the parties must be allowed to recall witnesses with reference to the altered charge unless the application is frivolous or vexatious (s 131 CPC).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 230(1)(g): power to alter the charge or frame a new charge before calling on the defence; follow ss 128–131.
> - Cyril-Exam §5.2.1 (secondary):
>   - exercise only where the evidence obviously points to another offence (*Tan Khee Wan Iris*); the trial court need not search for offences;
>   - altered charge explained to the accused (s 128(2)) and plea taken (s 129(1));
>   - if the accused says he is not ready, the court considers his reasons (s 129(2)); proceeds if no prejudice (s 129(3)); otherwise directs a new trial or adjourns (s 129(4)).
> - s 129 (CPC-Text): (1) on alteration the court must immediately call on the accused to plead and say whether he is ready; (2) if not ready, consider his reasons; (3) proceed if unlikely to prejudice the defence or the prosecution; (4) otherwise direct a new trial or adjourn.
> - s 131: on application, the parties must be allowed to recall or re-summon and examine witnesses on the altered charge only, unless the application is frivolous or vexatious or meant to cause delay or frustrate justice.

**P** On appeal the power to alter a charge is exercised with great caution and not to the accused's prejudice (*GDC v PP* [2020] 5 SLR 1130 at [29]) — see 0-41.

**A** First, identify the alteration: [from s 324 to s 323 PC (no dangerous weapon proved)]. Second, ensure it is explained and [Client]'s plea taken; if he needs time to meet it, say so under s 129(2) and seek an adjournment if proceeding would prejudice him. Third, apply to recall [the complainant] to cross-examine on the altered charge (s 131). **C** Therefore the court may alter the charge, but [Client] is entitled to [time / recall of witnesses] to meet it.

*Source note: CL5 s16, s21 (s 230(1)(g); ss 128–131; s 131); Cyril-Exam §5.2.1 (Tan Khee Wan Iris; s 129 detail — secondary); CL7 s21–22 (GDC [29]).*

### pcp-w-0-29 — The case for the Defence: whether the accused should testify (ss 230(1)(n)–(p), 291(1) CPC; PCR r 14(5)(a)(ii))
*Keywords: case for the defence, called on to give defence, give evidence or remain silent, testify, oath, affirmation, cross-examined, s 291(1) CPC, accused gives evidence first, s 230(1)(o), s 230(1)(p), plead guilty at this stage, PCR r 14(5)(a)(ii), defence witnesses*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], called on to give his defence, should testify, and how the defence case proceeds. **C** I must advise [Client] whether to give evidence or remain silent (r 14(5)(a)(ii) PCR); if he testifies he must do so on oath, first, and be cross-examined (ss 230(1)(o), 291(1) CPC). Given [the confession / the need to explain his defence], I would advise him to [testify].
**R** When called on, the accused may plead guilty or give his defence (s 230(1)(n) CPC). If he gives evidence he must do so on oath or affirmation and is liable to cross-examination (s 291(1)), and his evidence is heard before any other defence witness (s 230(1)(o)). Each defence witness is examined in chief, cross-examined (by any co-accused, then the Prosecution) and re-examined (s 230(1)(p)). The practitioner must advise the accused whether to give evidence or remain silent (r 14(5)(a)(ii) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 230(1)(n): when called on, the accused may (i) plead guilty, or (ii) give his defence.
> - s 230(1)(o): the accused may open his case; if he gives evidence, he is heard before other defence witnesses (CL5 s18).
> - s 291(1): an accused giving evidence does so on oath or affirmation and is liable to cross-examination.
> - s 230(1)(p) (Cyril-Exam §5.3): accused gives evidence, cross-examined by any co-accused then the Prosecution, then re-examined; same for defence witnesses.
> - Browne v Dunn applies to the Prosecution putting its case to the accused.
> - PCR r 14(5)(a)(ii): must advise on whether to give evidence or remain silent; the accused decides.

**P** If, once called on, the accused refuses to be sworn or without good cause refuses to answer, the court may draw such inferences as appear proper (s 291(3) CPC), though he cannot be compelled to testify (s 291(4)) (0-26).

**A** First, assess what the Prosecution's case shows: [an admitted confession and forensic evidence]; without [Client]'s evidence there is nothing to contradict it. Second, weigh the risks: cross-examination on [his CFD and earlier statements], against the risk of an inference from silence and of the Prosecution's case going unanswered. Third, advise in writing and follow his decision; arrange defence witnesses ([alibi witness], noting the s 278 notice, 0-2) to follow his evidence. **C** Therefore I would advise [Client] to [give evidence], recording the advice and his decision.

*Source note: CL5 s18, s41 (ss 230(1)(n)–(o), 291(1); r 14(5)(a)(ii)); Cyril-Exam §5.3 (s 230(1)(n)–(p)); PYP21-CRI Q1.3.*

### pcp-w-0-30 — Recalling witnesses and rebuttal evidence (ss 283, 131, 230(1)(t) CPC)
*Keywords: recall witness, summon witness, s 283(1) CPC, s 283(2) essential to just decision, s 131 CPC altered charge, rebuttal witness, s 230(1)(t) CPC, Prosecution reply evidence*

**I** The issue is whether [a witness] can be recalled, or the Prosecution call rebuttal evidence, after [the defence case has closed]. **C** The court may summon or recall any witness and must do so if his evidence is essential to a just decision (s 283 CPC); the Prosecution may call or recall witnesses in rebuttal at the close of the defence case (s 230(1)(t)), and the defence may cross-examine them.
**R** At any stage the court may summon or recall a witness (s 283(1) CPC), and must do so if his evidence appears essential to a just decision (s 283(2)). Where the charge is altered, the parties must be allowed to recall witnesses on the altered charge unless the application is frivolous or vexatious (s 131). At the close of the defence case the Prosecution may call or recall any person in rebuttal, who may be cross-examined by the defence (s 230(1)(t)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 283(1): court's discretion to summon or recall a witness at the end of the trial.
> - s 283(2): must recall or summon if the evidence is essential to a just decision.
> - s 131: on an altered or new charge, parties must be allowed to recall witnesses unless frivolous or vexatious.
> - s 230(1)(t): Prosecution may call or recall any person for rebuttal at the close of the defence case; liable to cross-examination by the defence.

**P** The rebuttal right is the Prosecution's; the defence's protection is the right to cross-examine the rebuttal witness (CL5 s23).

**A** First, identify the reason: [new evidence emerged during the defence case — e.g. Client's alibi]; the Prosecution may call rebuttal evidence under s 230(1)(t), and I will cross-examine. [If the defence needs a Prosecution witness back because of a later disclosure, apply under s 283 on the basis that his evidence is essential to a just decision.] **C** Therefore [the witness] [may / must] be recalled under [s 283 / s 131 / s 230(1)(t)].

*Source note: CL5 s20–23 (ss 283(1)–(2), 131, 230(1)(t)).*

### pcp-w-0-31 — Impeaching a witness by prior inconsistent statement (ss 147, 157(c) EA)
*Keywords: impeachment, impeach credit, prior inconsistent statement, s 147 EA, s 157(c) EA, Muthusamy, Heah Lian Kin, cannot remember, Kwang Boon Keong, serious or material discrepancy, Loganatha Venkatesan, effect of impeachment, explain discrepancies, accused's statement used in cross-examination*

**I** The issue is how [the witness]'s credit can be impeached because his evidence in court contradicts [his earlier statement], and what follows. **C** Counsel may cross-examine him on the earlier statement under s 147 EA and then apply to impeach his credit under s 157(c) EA, provided the discrepancy is serious or material; even if his credit is impeached, the court still evaluates which parts of his evidence to accept (*Loganatha Venkatesan v PP* [2000] 2 SLR(R) 904 at [56]).
**R** A witness whose testimony contradicts his previous statement may be cross-examined on it (s 147(1) EA), following the *Muthusamy v PP* [1948] MLJ 57 procedure, and his credit then impeached (s 157(c) EA). Only serious or material discrepancies qualify (*Kwang Boon Keong Peter v PP* [1998] 2 SLR(R) 211 at [21]), but a claim not to remember facts given in detail earlier is an inconsistency (*PP v Heah Lian Kin* [2002] 2 SLR(R) 745 at [44], [51]). Impeachment does not automatically mean total rejection of the evidence (*Loganatha* at [56]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Step 1: invoke s 147(1) EA to cross-examine on the previous statement (*Muthusamy*):
>   1. establish the material parts of the evidence inconsistent with the statement;
>   2. highlight the discrepancies (cover prejudicial parts);
>   3. apply to cross-examine and tender a copy for the court to decide whether material discrepancies exist;
>   4. the court decides whether there are serious or material discrepancies (no irreconcilable conflict → stop);
>   5. if so, the process continues;
>   6. the witness is asked whether he made the statement voluntarily; if he denies making it, prove it by calling its maker;
>   7. once admitted, put the discrepancies and allow an explanation;
>   8. a satisfactory explanation saves his credit.
> - Step 2: apply to impeach credit under s 157(c) EA.
> - *Heah Lian Kin* [44], [51]: "cannot remember" versus a detailed statement is inconsistent.
> - *Kwang Boon Keong* [21]: only serious or material discrepancies trigger s 147.
> - *Loganatha* [56]: impeachment does not lead automatically to total rejection; the court scrutinises the whole evidence.

**P** The same procedure applies when an accused who testifies is confronted with his own earlier statement; the Prosecution may also rely on his CFD (s 258A, 0-21) and, if admitted, his statement (0-32).

**A** First, identify the inconsistency: [the complainant now says the touching lasted a minute; her statement said a second] / [Client says at trial he did not touch her; his long statement admits touching "on a drunken impulse"]. Second, decide whether it is serious or material — it goes to [the central fact], so yes. Third, run the *Muthusamy* steps and apply under s 157(c). Fourth, explain the effect: the court will weigh the whole of the evidence, and may still accept parts of the witness's testimony (*Loganatha*). [For Client: be ready to explain the inconsistency, e.g. that he signed without reading, which also supports a voluntariness or accuracy challenge.] **C** Therefore [the witness]'s credit can be impeached on the inconsistency, which will reduce the weight of his evidence on [point].

*Source note: CL5 s25–32 (ss 147(1), 157(c) EA; Muthusamy steps; Heah Lian Kin [44], [51]; Kwang Boon Keong [21]; Loganatha [56]); PYP25 Q1.4 (admission in long statement; margin note "impeach").*

### pcp-w-0-32 — Challenging the admissibility of the accused's statement: TIPO and the ancillary hearing (ss 258, 279 CPC)
*Keywords: admissibility of statement, confession, voluntariness, s 258 CPC, threat inducement promise, oppression, TIPO, ancillary hearing, voir dire, trial within a trial, s 279 CPC, Teo Yeow Chuah, beyond reasonable doubt, no caution, right to silence, handcuffed, 8 hours, signed without reading, long statement admission, how can statement be used against me*

**I** The issue is whether the Prosecution can admit [Client]'s [long statement containing a confession] against him, given that [he was threatened / held handcuffed for eight hours / signed it without reading it]. **C** A statement of the accused is admissible unless made under threat, inducement, promise or oppression (s 258 CPC); because [Client] alleges [oppression and a threat], the court must hold an ancillary hearing (s 279 CPC), at which the Prosecution must prove beyond reasonable doubt that the statement was voluntary (*Teo Yeow Chuah v PP* [2004] 2 SLR(R) 563 at [20]).
**R** An accused's statement is admissible as of right unless it was made under threat, inducement or promise, or oppression (s 258 CPC). Where the defence alleges this, the court must hold an ancillary hearing (voir dire) to decide admissibility (s 279 CPC). The Prosecution bears the burden of proving voluntariness beyond reasonable doubt (*Teo Yeow Chuah* at [20]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 258(1): the accused's statement is admissible (to a police officer of at least sergeant's rank, s 258(2)).
> - s 258(3): the court must refuse to admit it if its making appears to have been caused by an inducement, threat or promise having reference to the charge, proceeding from a person in authority, and sufficient to give the accused reasonable grounds for supposing that making it would gain him an advantage or avoid an evil of a temporal nature.
> - Explanation 1 (oppression): acts of a person in authority that tend to sap, and have in fact sapped, the maker's free will amount to a threat, inducement or promise.
> - Explanation 2: an otherwise admissible statement is not inadmissible merely because, e.g., (b) the accused was intoxicated; (d) he was not warned that he need not make it and that it might be used in evidence; (e) the recording officer did not fully comply with s 22 or 23.
> - s 279: where admissibility is challenged on TIPO grounds, the court must convene an ancillary hearing (voir dire, trial within a trial).
> - *Teo Yeow Chuah* [20]: the Prosecution must prove voluntariness beyond reasonable doubt.
> - If admitted, the statement is evidence of the facts in it; if the accused testifies inconsistently, it can be used to cross-examine and impeach him (0-31).
> - Failure to caution: not fatal by itself (Explanation 2(d)); but it may be weighed with other circumstances in deciding oppression.
> - The objection must be stated in the CFD (s 165(1)(d)); omission invites s 169 inferences (0-21).

**P** The test for oppression is whether the investigation was, by its nature, duration or other attendant circumstances, such as to affect the accused's mind and will so that he spoke when he would otherwise have stayed silent (*Tey Tsun Hang v PP* [2014] 2 SLR 1189 at [113]); the threshold is high — being handcuffed to a chair for 9 hours without food or drink was "highly inconsiderate" but not oppressive (*PP v Tan Boon Tat* [1990] 1 SLR(R) 287), and failing to offer sustenance for 7 hours does not automatically make a statement involuntary (*Fung Yuk Shing v PP* [1993] 2 SLR(R) 771). For a threat, inducement or promise the test is partly objective (was there one?) and partly subjective (did it operate on the accused's mind through hope of escape or fear of punishment?), and the Prosecution must prove voluntariness beyond reasonable doubt (*Chia Chien Wei Kelvin v PP* [1998] 3 SLR(R) 619 at [53]).

**A** First, identify each allegation and the TIPO head it engages: [the IO's "I'll lock you up for however long it takes" — threat]; [8 hours handcuffed in shorts in a cold room with no break — oppression]; [no caution on the right to silence — not by itself fatal (Explanation 2(d) to s 258)]; [signed without reading at 3am — goes to oppression and to weight and accuracy]. Second, state them in the CFD (0-21). Third, at trial, object when the Prosecution seeks to admit the statement; the court holds an ancillary hearing where the IO and [Client] testify; the Prosecution must prove voluntariness beyond reasonable doubt. Fourth, if excluded, the confession cannot be used; if admitted, [Client] may still challenge its weight and must explain the inconsistency with his trial evidence. Fifth, the admissibility ruling cannot be appealed until after verdict (0-38). **C** Therefore [Client] should challenge admissibility at an ancillary hearing, where the burden of proving voluntariness lies on the Prosecution.

*Source note: CL5 s34 (ss 258, 279; Teo Yeow Chuah [20]); CPC-Text s 258(1)–(3), Explanations 1–2; CL4 s12, s17–18; SQ22-CRI p2–3 (specimen answer: Tey Tsun Hang [113]; Tan Boon Tat; Fung Yuk Shing; Kelvin Chia [53]); PYP21-CRI Q1.2, PYP22-CRI Q1.2, PYP25 Q1.4.*

### pcp-w-0-33 — Closing submissions, judgment and proof beyond reasonable doubt; counsel's absence
*Keywords: closing submissions, s 230(1)(u), s 230(1)(v), final right of reply, judgment, beyond reasonable doubt, Jagatheesan, fanciful doubt, PP v GCK, reasonable doubt within the Prosecution's case, totality of evidence, discharge amounting to acquittal, s 230(1)(w), conviction, s 230(1)(x), PCR r 14(3)(c), r 14(3)(d), absent from trial*

**I** The issue is how the court will decide [Client]'s guilt, and [what counsel must do if he cannot attend the trial]. **C** The Prosecution must prove each element beyond reasonable doubt; a reasonable doubt may arise from within its own case or on the totality of evidence (*PP v GCK* [2020] 1 SLR 486), and if it does [Client] must be acquitted. [Counsel who cannot attend must have reasonable grounds, tell the client and try to arrange another practitioner (r 14(3)(c)–(d) PCR).]
**R** At the close of the defence case the defence may sum up and the Prosecution has the final reply (s 230(1)(u)–(v) CPC). The burden of proving guilt beyond reasonable doubt always lies on the Prosecution; fanciful doubts do not suffice (*Jagatheesan s/o Krishnasamy v PP* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 45 at [61]). A reasonable doubt is one supported by reasons logically connected to the evidence, arising within the Prosecution's case or on the totality of evidence (*GCK* at [131], [134]–[135]). If not guilty the court must order a discharge amounting to an acquittal; if guilty it convicts and proceeds under s 228 (s 230(1)(w)–(x)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 230(1)(u): defence may sum up; (v) Prosecution has the final right of reply.
> - Standard: beyond reasonable doubt; burden always on the Prosecution; not all conceivable doubts (*Jagatheesan* [61]).
> - *GCK* [131], [134]–[135]: a doubt is reasonable only if reasoned (logically connected to the evidence); it may arise within the Prosecution's case or on the totality of the evidence, including the defence.
> - s 230(1)(w): not guilty → discharge amounting to an acquittal; (x) guilty → conviction and s 228 sentencing procedure.
> - PCR r 14(3)(c)–(d): must not be absent from a trial or hearing without reasonable grounds; if absent, must (i) tell the accused the grounds beforehand, and (ii) make reasonable efforts to arrange another practitioner.

**P** The defence need not prove innocence: it is enough to show a reasoned doubt, either in the Prosecution's evidence itself or once the defence evidence is weighed (*GCK* at [134]–[135]).

**A** First, in closing, go element by element and identify the gaps: [identification rests on a description inconsistent with Client; the confession was excluded or is unreliable; Kelly's statement]. Second, frame each as a reasoned doubt connected to the evidence. **C** Therefore [Client] should be acquitted because the Prosecution has not proved [element] beyond reasonable doubt.

*Source note: CL5 s36–40 (s 230(1)(u)–(x); Jagatheesan [61]; GCK [131], [134]–[135]; r 14(3)(c)–(d)).*

## 0.VII Sentencing

- **Which sentencing principle dominates?**
  - Retribution, deterrence (general/specific), prevention, rehabilitation (*Law Aik Meng*); young or mentally ill offenders → rehabilitation (*Praveen*; *Lim Ghim Peow*) → [0-34](#pcp-w-0-34)
- **What sentence for this charge? Options?**
  - Fine, imprisonment, caning, probation, reformative training, community sentences (DRO, CSO, MTO, SDO), CT/PD → [0-35](#pcp-w-0-35)
  - Adjust from precedent or benchmark for aggravating and mitigating factors; lack of antecedents is neutral (*Chew Soo Chun*) → [0-35](#pcp-w-0-35)
  - Community sentence or MTO available? Barred for mandatory minimums, offences over 3 years, prior prison over 3 months (s 337) → [0-35](#pcp-w-0-35)
  - Probation? Any age if the sentence isn't fixed by law; with a mandatory minimum only if aged 18–20 at conviction and no previous such conviction (POA s 5(1)) → [0-35](#pcp-w-0-35)
- **Prosecution wants all sentences consecutive — respond**
  - s 307(1): 3+ imprisonment terms → at least 2 consecutive; fines aggregate → [0-36](#pcp-w-0-36)
  - One-transaction rule (same legally protected interest, proximity) (*Shouffee*) → [0-36](#pcp-w-0-36)
  - Totality: not substantially above the most serious offence's normal level; not crushing → [0-36](#pcp-w-0-36)
- **Start the sentence later / backdate to remand; pay fine by instalments; what happens to seized property?**
  - Deferment and backdating (s 318); fines (s 319); disposal of property (s 364) → [0-37](#pcp-w-0-37)

### pcp-w-0-34 — Sentencing principles: retribution, deterrence, prevention, rehabilitation
*Keywords: sentencing principles, retribution, deterrence, general deterrence, specific deterrence, prevention, rehabilitation, Law Aik Meng, R v Sargeant, Praveen s/o Krishnan, young offender, Lim Ghim Peow, mental disorder, psychiatric condition, why punish*

**I** The issue is which sentencing principle should dominate in sentencing [Client] for [offence]. **C** [Rehabilitation / deterrence] should be the dominant consideration because [Client is young / his depression contributed to the offence / the offence is prevalent], and the sentence sought should reflect that.
**R** The classical principles are retribution, deterrence, prevention and rehabilitation; the sentencing judge must decide which carries most weight on the facts (*R v Sargeant* (1974) 60 Cr App R 74, approved in *PP v Law Aik Meng* [2007] 2 SLR(R) 814 at [24]–[29]). Rehabilitation is the presumptive consideration for young offenders (*Praveen s/o Krishnan v PP* [2018] 3 SLR 1300), and may take precedence where deterrence is less effective because of a serious psychiatric condition or mental disorder (*Lim Ghim Peow v PP* [2014] 4 SLR 1287).

> **Rule in full**
> - Retribution: the offender pays for what he has done; past conduct; death, caning, imprisonment, compensation.
> - Deterrence: "cornerstone" of Singapore sentencing; general (deter like-minded others) and specific (persistent or premeditated offenders); future conduct; may not be relevant to offenders with mental disorders or intellectual disability who cannot make proper choices.
> - Prevention: offender poses a threat; disqualification, life imprisonment, preventive detention.
> - Rehabilitation: reform to prevent future crime; presumptive for young offenders (*Praveen*) and offenders whose mental disorders contribute to their offending (*Lim Ghim Peow*); probation, community sentences.
> - Examples (CL6 s10): rising offence (scams) → general deterrence; serial thief → specific deterrence; schizophrenic offender → rehabilitation (MTO); escalating sexual violence → prevention (PD); drink-driving death → retribution.

**P** In *Lim Ghim Peow* the Court of Appeal said rehabilitation may take precedence where deterrence is rendered less effective by a serious psychiatric condition, while still serving the public interest in reducing recidivism (CL6 s9).

**A** First, identify the features: [Client is 50, first offender, grieving, with depression linked to the thefts]. Second, deterrence is weakened if her condition affected her choices, and rehabilitation is engaged (*Lim Ghim Peow*), subject to proving the condition (0-15). [Or: Client is 18 — rehabilitation is presumptive (Praveen).] [Or: the offence is a prevalent scam — general deterrence dominates.] Third, translate into sentence: [a community-based sentence such as an MTO rather than imprisonment — available for theft (s 379 PC, up to 3 years) unless an exclusion in s 337(1) applies (e.g. a previous prison term over 3 months), and only if the appointed psychiatrist's report states the condition is treatable, she is suitable, and it contributed to the offence (s 339(3))]. **C** Therefore the court should treat [rehabilitation] as dominant and impose [sentence].

*Source note: CL6 s4–10 (Sargeant; Law Aik Meng [24]–[29]; Praveen; Lim Ghim Peow; examples); CPC-Text ss 337(1)–(2), 339(1)–(4); PC-Text s 379.*

### pcp-w-0-35 — Sentencing options and calibrating an individual sentence; probation
*Keywords: sentencing options, fine, imprisonment, caning, probation, discharge, reformative training, community sentence, DRO, CSO, MTO, mandatory treatment order, SDO, short detention order, corrective training, preventive detention, aggravating factors, mitigating factors, neutral factors, Chew Soo Chun, benchmark, starting point, sentencing framework, Sentencing Advisory Panel, guilty plea reduction, probation eligibility, maximum sentence*

**I** The issue is what sentence [Client] is likely to receive for [offence] and what options are open. **C** The court will start from [the precedents / the benchmark of X] and adjust for [aggravating factors A and mitigating factors B]; [a community sentence / probation / a fine] is [available / not available] because [reason].
**R** Sentencing options include fines, imprisonment, caning, probation and discharges, reformative training, community sentences (DRO, CSO, MTO, SDO), corrective training, preventive detention and death. An individual sentence is calibrated from precedents or benchmarks by aggravating factors (e.g. premeditation, group offending, intoxication, antecedents), mitigating factors (e.g. genuine remorse, youth) and neutral factors (e.g. lack of antecedents) (*Chew Soo Chun v PP* [2016] 2 SLR 78 at [30]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Options (CL6 s12): fines; imprisonment; caning; probation and discharges; reformative training; community sentences (DRO, CSO, MTO, SDO); corrective training / preventive detention; death penalty.
> - Calibrating (CL6 s14): aggravating, mitigating, neutral factors; used to distinguish precedents or adjust benchmarks.
>   - Illustration 1: same theft without premeditation → below the precedent's 10 weeks.
>   - Illustration 2: offensive weapon benchmark 6 months; violent antecedents → above.
>   - Illustration 3: statutory rape starting point 12 years; plea with genuine remorse → below.
> - Frameworks and SAP guidelines (CL6 s15–17): exist (e.g. SAP Guidelines on Reduction in Sentences for Guilty Pleas) but "will not be tested".
> - Maximum sentence (Cyril-Exam §7.7.3, secondary): near-maximum only for the gravest cases, considering seriousness, culpability and legislative intent (*Angliss Singapore*); may be imposed even on a plea.
> - Community sentences (s 337(1) CPC) are barred for, among others: offences with a sentence fixed by law, or a specified or mandatory minimum sentence of imprisonment, fine or caning; fine-only offences; offences punishable with over 3 years' imprisonment; and offenders previously sentenced to over 3 months' imprisonment or to CT/PD. An MTO is still possible in some of these cases (s 337(2)); MTO requirements in s 339(3).
> - Probation (s 5(1) Probation of Offenders Act 1951): where a person is convicted of an offence whose sentence is not fixed by law, the court may, if expedient having regard to the circumstances including the nature of the offence and the offender's character, make a probation order instead of sentencing him — supervision for not less than 6 months nor more than 3 years. There is no general age limit.
>   - Proviso: where the offence carries a specified minimum or mandatory minimum sentence of imprisonment, fine or caning, probation is available only if the offender (a) is at least 18 but under 21 at conviction, and (b) has no previous conviction for such an offence.
>   - A probation order may add requirements (residence, up to 12 months in an approved institution; unpaid community service) (s 5(2)–(5)).
>   - So in PYP21-CRI Q1.4 (robbery by night with a mandatory minimum; 25 at offence, 26 at conviction) probation was not available.
> - Court's sentencing limits (s 303 CPC): District Court — imprisonment up to 10 years, fine up to $30,000, caning up to 12 strokes; Magistrate's Court — imprisonment up to 3 years, fine up to $10,000, caning up to 6 strokes; General Division — any sentence authorised by law.

**P** Cyril's contact-session notes stress knowing the client's sentencing options before advising on plea, since some offences exclude community-based sentences (Cyril-Exam CS2).

**A** First, identify the prescribed punishment for [offence] and any mandatory minimum or caning (which rules out community sentences, s 337(1)(b), and probation unless the offender is 18–20 and a first such offender, POA s 5(1) proviso). Second, find the precedent or benchmark and place [Client]'s case: [no premeditation, low value, restitution — lower]; [antecedent of similar theft — higher]. Third, consider whether a non-custodial option is available and suited ([MTO for an offender whose psychiatric condition contributed — not barred by s 337(1), with a favourable appointed psychiatrist's report under s 339(3)]; [no community sentence where the offence carries a mandatory minimum, as for robbery by night in PYP21-CRI Q1]). **C** Therefore the likely sentence is [range], and I would submit for [option].

*Source note: CL6 s12–17 (options; Chew Soo Chun [30]; illustrations; frameworks and SAP not tested); CPC-Text ss 303(1)–(3), 337, 339 (unchanged on SSO-CPC); SSO-POA s 5(1)–(5) (current as at 10 Nov 2026; proviso para (a) amended wef 30 Dec 2025); Cyril-Exam §7.7.3 and CS2 notes (secondary); PYP21-CRI Q1.4, PYP23-CRI Q1.3.*

### pcp-w-0-36 — Aggregate sentences: s 307(1) CPC, the one-transaction rule and totality (Shouffee)
*Keywords: consecutive sentences, concurrent sentences, aggregate sentence, global sentence, s 307(1) CPC, at least two consecutive, three distinct offences, one-transaction rule, Mohamed Shouffee bin Adam, same legally protected interest, proximity time place, continuity, totality principle, crushing sentence, last look, fines aggregate, Raveen Balakrishnan, all four consecutive, 16 weeks*

**I** The issue is whether [the Prosecution's position that all four sentences of 4 weeks should run consecutively] is correct, and how the aggregate sentence should be fixed. **C** No: s 307(1) CPC requires only two terms to run consecutively; [the trespass and theft on each night form one transaction and should run concurrently], and the totality principle requires an aggregate of about [8 weeks], not [16 weeks].
**R** Where an accused is sentenced to imprisonment for at least three distinct offences, at least two terms must run consecutively (s 307(1) CPC); fines do not run concurrently but aggregate. Sentences for distinct offences committed on separate occasions should generally run consecutively, but sentences for offences committed in a single transaction — a single invasion of the same legally protected interest, proximate in time, place, continuity of action and purpose — should run concurrently (*Mohamed Shouffee bin Adam v PP* [2014] 2 SLR 998 at [27]–[34]). The totality principle then requires a "last look" to ensure the aggregate is proportionate and not crushing (*Shouffee*).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 307(1): convicted and sentenced to imprisonment for at least 3 distinct offences → at least 2 terms must run consecutively (so the court cannot order all to run concurrently).
> - Fines cannot run "concurrently"; they aggregate.
> - One-transaction rule (*Shouffee* [27]–[34]):
>   - distinct offences on separate occasions → generally consecutive;
>   - offences in a single transaction → concurrent;
>   - single transaction = single invasion of the same legally protected interest, and proximity in time, place, continuity of action, and continuity of purpose and design.
> - Totality principle (applied at the end, a "last look"):
>   - limb 1: the aggregate should not be substantially above the normal level of sentences for the most serious of the individual offences;
>   - limb 2: its effect should not be "crushing" and not in keeping with the offender's record and prospects.
> - Interaction (Cyril-Exam §7.7.2, from *Raveen Balakrishnan*): three offences in one transaction → one may be concurrent but two must be consecutive (s 307); two related plus one unrelated → related concurrent, unrelated consecutive; totality cannot be used to minimise punishment where the facts merit severity (*ADF v PP*), and remains subject to s 307(1).
> - Illustration (CL6 s19–22): three nights of house-breaking (3 months each) and theft (2 weeks each); each night's pair is one transaction; running all three transactions consecutively may be crushing; the court may run two house-breaking terms consecutively for 6 months.

**P** In the 2026 sample (criminal intimidation and possessing an offensive weapon in one incident, 6 months each), the guide answer applied the *Shouffee* test (same legally protected interest; proximity in time and place, continuity of action and purpose) and both limbs of totality.

**A** First, group the offences by occasion: [Night 1: trespass + theft of a macaw; Night 2: same; Night 3: same — six charges, four proceeded]. Second, apply the one-transaction rule: on each night the trespass was to commit the theft, at the same place and time, invading the same interest (the park's property), so each pair is one transaction and should run concurrently. Offences on different nights are separate occasions and would generally be consecutive. Third, apply s 307(1): with [four] terms of imprisonment, at least two must be consecutive — e.g. [one from Night 1 and one from Night 2], giving [8 weeks]. Fourth, apply totality: [16 weeks] is substantially above the normal level for one theft of this kind and crushing for a 50-year-old first offender with depression; [8 weeks] is proportionate. **C** Therefore I would submit that only two sentences should run consecutively, for an aggregate of [8 weeks].

*Source note: CL6 s19–22 (s 307(1); fines aggregate; Shouffee [20], [27]–[34]; totality limbs; illustration); Cyril-Exam §7.7.2 (Raveen Balakrishnan; ADF; McCrea — secondary); SQ guide p125 Q1(d); PYP21-CRI Q1.5, PYP22-CRI Q2.3, PYP25 Q2.4.*

### pcp-w-0-37 — Other sentencing orders: deferment, backdating, fines, withdrawal of charges, disposal of property
