*Keywords: deferment of sentence, defer sentence, settle affairs, backdating, backdate to remand, s 318 CPC, fines, s 319 CPC, instalments, extension of time to pay, default imprisonment, s 147 CPC withdrawal of remaining charges, disposal of property, s 364 CPC, forfeiture, confiscation, destruction, delivery to owner, disposal inquiry, seized property, stolen property*

**I** The issue is [whether [Client]'s sentence can be deferred or backdated / how the fine can be paid / what will happen to the seized [items]]. **C** [Answer under s 318 / s 319 / s 364 CPC.]
**R** A sentence of imprisonment takes effect from the date it is passed unless the court directs otherwise, so the court may defer it or backdate it (s 318(1) CPC). The court may allow time or instalments to pay a fine and impose a default imprisonment term (s 319). Where two or more charges are made and the accused is convicted on one or more, the Prosecution may with the court's consent withdraw the rest, which has the effect of an acquittal unless the conviction is set aside (s 147). The court may order the disposal of property connected with an offence that is before it or lawfully seized, by forfeiture, confiscation, destruction or delivery to any person (s 364).

> **Rule in full**
> - From Cyril-Exam §7.3–7.6 (not in CL6 slides); section text checked in CPC-Text.
> - Deferment (s 318(1); *Loh Kok Siew*): paramount consideration is the reasons for deferral; only for good reasons; consider previous adjournments, gravity, record and risk of reoffending, whether bail secures attendance.
> - s 318(1): imprisonment, corrective training or preventive detention takes effect from the date passed unless the court (or appellate court) otherwise directs; s 318(2): where deferred, the court may later fix another date and release the offender on bail or personal bond meanwhile.
> - Backdating (current s 318(3)–(5), as amended wef 31 Jul 2026): the court may direct that the sentence take effect on a date earlier than the date passed (s 318(3)); where the offender was remanded in custody or in a psychiatric institution for the offence, the court must consider backdating (s 318(4)); in fixing the date it must consider all the circumstances, including (a) the date of arrest, (b) the period remanded in custody, (c) the period remanded in a psychiatric institution, and (d) any period after arrest not in custody (s 318(5)). The section now also covers sentences for public protection and enhanced public protection.
> - Fines (s 319(1)): (a) no stated maximum → unlimited but not excessive; (b) before full payment the court may (i) allow and extend time, (ii) order instalments, (iii) attach the offender's property, (iv) direct a debtor of the offender to pay the court, (v) direct default imprisonment, consecutive with any other imprisonment.
> - Withdrawal of remaining charges (s 147): with the court's consent after conviction on one or more; effect of an acquittal unless the conviction is set aside, in which case the withdrawn charges may be tried.
> - Disposal of property (s 364(2)): property in respect of which an offence was committed, used or intended for an offence, or constituting evidence, and produced before or in the custody of the court or police → forfeiture, confiscation, destruction or delivery to any person; a disposal inquiry if competing claims.
> - Contact session (Cyril-Exam CS2): counsel may ask for backdating to first remand or deferment to settle affairs; neither is guaranteed; the bailor should be ready for increased bail.

**P** Imprisonment for default in paying a fine runs consecutively with any other term (Cyril-Exam §7.4).

**A** First, for deferment: [Client] needs [two weeks to arrange care for his elderly parents]; reasons are good, no prior adjournments, offence not grave, bail secures attendance — apply under s 318. For backdating: [he was remanded for 10 days] — ask that the sentence run from the date of remand. Second, for seized property, take each item: [stolen wallet — return to the owner]; [knife used in the robbery — forfeiture or destruction, though it has sentimental value]; [laptop with nothing incriminating — return to the accused]; [gambling proceeds — forfeiture]; [stolen Rolex pawned — competing claims of owner and pawnbroker, disposal inquiry]. These outcomes are applications of s 364(2) (and s 364(4): property includes what it was converted into); a disposal inquiry decides competing claims. **C** Therefore the court [may defer / backdate / order disposal of X to Y].

*Source note: Cyril-Exam §7.3–7.6 and CS2 notes (Loh Kok Siew — secondary, not in CL6); CPC-Text ss 147, 319(1), 364(1)–(4); SSO-CPC s 318 (current text, amended wef 31 Jul 2026), s 319 (no change to (1)(a)–(b)); PYP21-CRI Q2.3 (deferred sentence), Q2.6 (s 364 disposal of five items).*

## 0.VIII Appeals and other applications

- **Can the client appeal? Against what?**
  - Conviction (not if he pleaded guilty, s 375), sentence, or a final order; no interlocutory appeals (e.g. a voir dire ruling) → [0-38](#pcp-w-0-38)
  - Victim cannot appeal; only a party (s 377(1); *Huang Meizhe*) → [0-38](#pcp-w-0-38)
  - From the State Courts to the General Division of the High Court (Magistrate's Appeal) → [0-38](#pcp-w-0-38)
- **Timelines and documents**
  - Notice of Appeal within 14 days of sentence (Form 66); Petition of Appeal within 14 days of receiving the GD/ROP (Form 67), or the appeal lapses; grounds limited to the Petition → [0-39](#pcp-w-0-39)
- **Client doesn't want to spend a day in prison**
  - Stay of execution and bail pending appeal (ss 382–383; *Adith* factors) → [0-40](#pcp-w-0-40)
- **Risks of appealing a sentence**
  - Court may enhance even on the accused's own appeal (*Wong Tian Jun De Beers*); threshold manifestly excessive (s 394; *PP v UI*) → [0-41](#pcp-w-0-41)
- **Missed the 14 days** → criminal motion for extension (s 380; *Tan Peng Khoon*: delay, explanation, prospects) → [0-42](#pcp-w-0-42)
- **New evidence after trial** → s 392; *Ladd v Marshall* → [0-43](#pcp-w-0-43)

### pcp-w-0-38 — Right of appeal: what, by whom, and to which court (ss 374, 375, 377 CPC)
*Keywords: appeal, right of appeal, s 374 CPC, judgment, sentence, order, final order, Ang Cheng Hai, compensation order, driving disqualification, interlocutory ruling, Knight Glenn Jeyasingam, s 375 CPC, plead guilty no appeal against conviction, s 377(1), party only, victim no right of appeal, Huang Meizhe, PP appeal, Lim Choon Teck, Magistrate's Appeal, General Division, Court of Appeal, s 386*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can appeal against [his conviction / sentence / the ruling admitting his statement], and to which court. **C** [Client] may appeal against [sentence] to the General Division of the High Court [but not against conviction, because he pleaded guilty (s 375 CPC) / not against the interlocutory ruling until after verdict].
**R** An appeal lies against a judgment, sentence or order of a court only as the CPC or another written law provides (s 374(1) CPC); an "order" is one that finally disposes of the parties' rights (*Ang Cheng Hai v PP* [1995] 3 SLR(R) 151), and there is no standalone appeal against interlocutory rulings (*Knight Glenn Jeyasingam v PP* [1998] 3 SLR(R) 196). A convicted person may appeal against conviction (unless he pleaded guilty, s 375), sentence or order (s 374(4)); the Public Prosecutor against acquittal, sentence or order (s 374(3)). Only a party may appeal (s 377(1)). Appeals from the State Courts go to the General Division of the High Court (s 19 SCJA).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 374(1): appeals only as the CPC or other written law provides.
> - "Judgment" = decision convicting or acquitting; "sentence" includes death, imprisonment, fine, caning; "order" = one finally disposing of rights (*Ang Cheng Hai*), e.g. compensation order, driving disqualification.
> - No standalone appeal against interlocutory rulings (*Knight Glenn*).
> - s 374(3) PP: acquittal; sentence (usually manifestly inadequate; may also argue manifestly excessive — *PP v Lim Choon Teck* [2015] 5 SLR 1395); other order.
> - s 374(4) convicted person: conviction (only if he did not plead guilty, s 375); sentence (usually manifestly excessive); other order.
> - s 377(1): only a party may appeal; victims and their families have no right (*Huang Meizhe v A-G* [2011] 2 SLR 1149).
> - Appellate courts (one-tier system): General Division (s 19 SCJA) hears appeals from Magistrates' and District Courts, Family Courts exercising criminal jurisdiction, and Youth Courts; Court of Appeal (s 60D SCJA) hears appeals from the General Division's original criminal jurisdiction. Magistrate's Appeals are usually heard by one judge; CJ may direct 3 or more (odd number) for an important point of law (s 386(1)); Court of Appeal 3, or 5 or more (s 386(2)).
> - Criminal review, reference and revision: not in syllabus.

**P** In *Knight Glenn* the High Court held there is no appeal against an interlocutory ruling; such rulings are challenged in an appeal against the final outcome (CL7 s4).

**A** First, identify what [Client] wants to challenge: [his 16-week aggregate sentence after pleading guilty] — he cannot appeal his conviction (s 375) but may appeal the sentence (s 374(4)); [the ruling admitting his confession mid-trial] — no appeal now; raise it in an appeal against conviction. Second, the appeal from the [District/Magistrate's] Court lies to the General Division of the High Court as a Magistrate's Appeal. Third, go on to procedure and risks (0-39 to 0-41). **C** Therefore [Client] may appeal against [sentence] to the General Division, [but not against conviction].

*Source note: CL7 s3–9 (overview; s 374; Ang Cheng Hai; Knight Glenn; s 374(3)–(4); Lim Choon Teck; s 375; s 377(1); Huang Meizhe; SCJA ss 19, 60D; s 386); PYP21-CRI Q1.3 (appealing the admission ruling), Q1.6; PYP25 Q2.5.*

### pcp-w-0-39 — Appeal procedure and timelines: Notice and Petition of Appeal; summary rejection (ss 377, 378, 384 CPC)
*Keywords: notice of appeal, 14 days, s 377(2) CPC, Form 66, appeal against conviction time runs from sentence, s 381 prisoner, record of proceedings, grounds of decision, petition of appeal, s 378(1), Form 67, appeal lapses, s 378(3), grounds limited, s 378(6), summary rejection, s 384, procedural timelines*

**I** The issue is what [Client] must do, and by when, to appeal against [his sentence / conviction]. **C** [Client] must file a Notice of Appeal within 14 days of [sentence] (s 377(2) CPC), and then a Petition of Appeal within 14 days of receiving the record of proceedings and grounds of decision (s 378(1)), setting out every ground relied on, failing which the appeal lapses.
**R** A Notice of Appeal (Form 66, Criminal Procedure Rules 2018) must be filed within 14 days of the judgment, sentence or order; for an appeal against conviction, time runs from sentence (s 377(2) CPC). The Petition of Appeal (Form 67) must be filed within 14 days of receiving the record of proceedings or grounds of decision (s 378(1)); if it is not filed the appeal lapses (s 378(3)), and the appellant may not rely on grounds outside it without permission (s 378(6)). An appeal may be summarily rejected without a hearing (s 384).

> **Rule in full**
> - Timeline: judgment/sentence/order → 14 days → Notice of Appeal (bail or stay pending appeal, 0-40) → ROP and GD served → 14 days → Petition of Appeal → (possible summary rejection) → hearing.
> - s 377(2): NOA within 14 days; (a) for conviction, from the date of sentence. Form 66: state shortly the substance of the judgment, sentence or order; address for service; signed. s 381: an appellant in prison may simply tell the prison authorities he wishes to appeal.
> - ROP (charges, SOF, notes of evidence, exhibits) and GD (trial court's reasons) are prepared once the NOA is filed and served on both parties.
> - s 378(1): Petition within 14 days of receiving the ROP/GD; Form 67: substance of the decision appealed and sufficient particulars of the points of law or fact said to be in error; signed. s 378(3): failure to file → appeal lapses. s 378(6): no reliance on grounds outside the Petition without the appellate court's permission (applied practically for unrepresented appellants).
> - s 384: summary rejection where the grounds raise no question of law, the evidence suffices for conviction, and there is no material raising reasonable doubt or suggesting the sentence should be reduced.

**P** Cyril's 2025 exam note records that he guessed "30 days" for the appeal period; the correct period is 14 days from sentence (s 377(2); CL7 s11). Conflict: Cyril-Exam §7.7.3 note says "about 30 days (pretty sure I'm wrong)"; the course materials (14 days) are followed.

**A** First, compute the deadline: sentence on [date] → NOA by [date + 14 days], in Form 66. Second, apply for bail or a stay pending appeal at the same time if [Client] wants to stay out of prison (0-40). Third, on receiving the GD and ROP, file the Petition within 14 days, setting out every ground ([sentence manifestly excessive; failure to apply the one-transaction rule; totality]), since others need permission. Fourth, explain the risks: summary rejection if the grounds are weak, and possible enhancement (0-41). **C** Therefore [Client] must file the Notice of Appeal by [date] and the Petition within 14 days of receiving the grounds.

*Source note: CL7 s10–18 (timeline; s 377(2), Form 66; s 381; ROP/GD; s 378(1), (3), (6), Form 67; s 384); Cyril-Exam §7.7.3 end-note (Conflict: "30 days"); PYP21-CRI Q1.6, PYP25 Q2.5.*

### pcp-w-0-40 — Bail and stay of execution pending appeal (ss 382–383 CPC; Adith)
*Keywords: bail pending appeal, stay of execution, s 382 CPC, s 383 CPC, Adith s/o Sarvotham, not spend a day in prison, appeal does not stay sentence, length of sentence versus time for appeal, absconding, security*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can avoid starting his sentence while his appeal is pending. **C** An appeal does not automatically stay execution, but [Client] may apply for bail pending appeal (s 382 CPC) and a stay of execution (s 383), which the court is likely to grant because [his sentence is short relative to the time the appeal will take and he is not a flight risk] (*PP v Adith s/o Sarvotham* [2014] 3 SLR 649).
**R** The court may grant bail pending appeal (s 382 CPC). An appeal does not operate as a stay of execution, but the court may stay execution on reasonable terms (s 383). The relevant factors are set out in *Adith* at [29], [32].

> **Rule in full**
> - s 382: bail pending appeal.
> - s 383: appeal does not automatically stay execution; court may stay on reasonable terms.
> - *Adith* [29], [32] factors:
>   - likelihood of absconding;
>   - criminal record;
>   - possibility of offending or reoffending on bail;
>   - sufficiency of security;
>   - interests of a fair and just prosecution;
>   - prejudice to the convicted person;
>   - nature and gravity of the offence;
>   - length of sentence compared with the time the appeal is likely to take;
>   - whether prejudice can be ameliorated by other measures.

**P** In *Adith* the High Court identified the comparison between the length of the sentence and the time the appeal will take as a key factor: a short sentence that would be served before the appeal is heard makes the appeal nugatory without a stay (CL7 s13).

**A** First, file the NOA (0-39) and apply for bail pending appeal and a stay. Second, run the *Adith* factors: [Client] [has been on bail throughout without breach; no record; a [7.5-year] sentence (so the "nugatory" factor is weak) / a [16-week] sentence that could be served before the appeal is heard (strong)]; [offence serious]; offer security and conditions. **C** Therefore [Client] is [likely / unlikely] to obtain a stay and bail pending appeal.

*Source note: CL7 s10, s13 (ss 382, 383; Adith [29], [32]); PYP21-CRI Q1.6 ("does not want to spend a day in prison"), Q2.3; PYP25 Q2.5.*

### pcp-w-0-41 — Powers on appeal, altering the charge, enhancing sentence; threshold for intervention (ss 390, 394 CPC)
*Keywords: powers of appellate court, s 390 CPC, dismiss appeal, retrial, reverse acquittal, alter finding, reduce or enhance sentence, alter charge on appeal, s 390(4), GDC v PP, enhancement on accused's appeal, Wong Tian Jun De Beers, risk of appeal, s 394 CPC, manifestly excessive, manifestly inadequate, wrong in law, against the weight of evidence, PP v UI, Siew Boon Loong, substantial alteration*

**I** The issue is [whether [Client]'s appeal against sentence is likely to succeed and what risks it carries]. **C** The appellate court will interfere only if the sentence is wrong in principle or manifestly excessive (s 394 CPC; *PP v UI* [2008] 4 SLR(R) 500), and on hearing [Client]'s appeal it may also enhance the sentence even without a Prosecution cross-appeal (*Wong Tian Jun De Beers v PP* [2021] SGHC 273).
**R** The appellate court may dismiss the appeal if there is no sufficient ground for interference, or, on an appeal against sentence, reduce or enhance the sentence or alter its nature (s 390(1) CPC). A judgment, sentence or order may be reversed only if wrong in law, against the weight of the evidence, or, for sentence, manifestly excessive or inadequate (s 394). Intervention requires an error as to the factual basis, a failure to appreciate the material, an error of principle, or manifest excess or inadequacy (*PP v UI* at [12]), requiring substantial rather than minute alteration (*PP v Siew Boon Loong* [2005] 1 SLR(R) 611 at [22]). The court may enhance on the accused's own appeal (*Wong Tian Jun De Beers*), and may alter the charge, cautiously and without prejudice to the accused (s 390(4)–(8); *GDC v PP* [2020] 5 SLR 1130 at [29]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 387(1): court must hear the appellant, may hear the respondent, must hear the reply.
> - s 390(1): may dismiss if no sufficient ground; otherwise
>   - (a) appeal against acquittal: reverse and order retrial, or find guilty and sentence;
>   - (b) appeal against conviction: reverse and acquit/discharge or order retrial; alter the finding; reduce or enhance sentence; alter its nature;
>   - (c) appeal against sentence: reduce or enhance, or alter its nature;
>   - (d) other order: alter or reverse.
> - s 390(4)–(8): may amend the charge on appeal against acquittal or conviction if sufficient evidence; must ask if the accused intends to offer a defence (s 390(6)); then order a trial or convict (s 390(7)(b), (8)). *GDC* [29]: exercise cautiously; proceedings below would have taken the same course (*Sim Wen Yi Ernest v PP* [2016] 5 SLR 207 at [12]); concern more acute after trial than after a plea.
> - Enhancement on the accused's appeal without a cross-appeal: *Wong Tian Jun De Beers*.
> - s 394: reverse or set aside only if wrong in law, against the weight of evidence, or (sentence) manifestly excessive or inadequate.
> - *PP v UI* [12]: (a) error as to proper factual basis; (b) failure to appreciate materials; (c) wrong in principle; (d) manifestly excessive or inadequate.
> - *Siew Boon Loong* [22]: manifestly excessive = unjustly severe, requiring substantial alterations rather than minute corrections.

**P** In *GDC* the High Court warned that the power to alter a charge on appeal "should be exercised cautiously, subject always to careful observance of the safeguards against prejudice to the defence" (at [29]).

**A** First, identify the error: [the trial judge ran all four sentences consecutively, failing to apply the one-transaction rule — an error of principle; the 16-week aggregate is manifestly excessive and needs substantial alteration to 8 weeks]. Second, advise the risk: the court may dismiss the appeal or even enhance the sentence (*Wong Tian Jun De Beers*), and a weak appeal may be summarily rejected (s 384); costs and time in custody if no stay. Third, weigh: [the error is clear, so prospects are reasonable]. **C** Therefore [Client] has [reasonable] prospects, but must accept the risk that the sentence could be increased.

*Source note: CL7 s19–25 (ss 387(1), 390(1), (4)–(8); GDC [29]; Sim Wen Yi Ernest [12]; Wong Tian Jun De Beers; s 394; PP v UI [12]; Siew Boon Loong [22]); PYP25 Q2.5 ("potential risks").*

### pcp-w-0-42 — Criminal motion to extend time to appeal (s 380 CPC; Tan Peng Khoon)
*Keywords: appeal out of time, extension of time, criminal motion, s 380 CPC, interests of justice, Tan Peng Khoon, length of delay, explanation for delay, prospect of success, Amarjeet Singh, missed 14 days, pleaded guilty three weeks ago*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], who was sentenced [three weeks ago] and did not file a Notice of Appeal within 14 days, can still appeal. **C** He may apply by criminal motion to the General Division for permission to appeal out of time under s 380 CPC; the court will consider the length of the delay, the explanation and the prospects of success (*PP v Tan Peng Khoon* [2016] 1 SLR 713).
**R** The appellate court may permit a person debarred from appealing for non-compliance with the CPC to appeal if it considers it in the interests of justice, on terms (s 380(1) CPC). It considers the length of the delay, the explanation for it, and whether the appeal has some prospect of success (*Tan Peng Khoon* at [38], [45]). A criminal motion is the vehicle for such ancillary relief tethered to the appeal (*Amarjeet Singh v PP* [2021] 4 SLR 841 at [27]–[28], [33]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 380(1): the appellate court may, on the application of a person debarred from appealing for non-compliance, permit him to appeal in the interests of justice, on terms.
> - *Tan Peng Khoon* [38], [45]: (a) length of delay in prosecuting the appeal; (b) explanation for the delay; (c) some prospect of success; heightened concern for prejudice where the Prosecution seeks the extension.
> - Criminal motions (*Amarjeet Singh* [27]–[28], [33]): ancillary relief supporting a primary action (trial, appeal, revision) — e.g. extension of time to file the Notice or Petition of Appeal; adducing additional evidence; bail where a lower court refused (not in syllabus).

**P** The three *Tan Peng Khoon* factors are weighed together: a short delay with a good explanation and some merit favours an extension; a long unexplained delay in a hopeless appeal does not (CL7 s27).

**A** First, the delay: sentence on [date], about [one week] past the deadline — short. Second, the explanation: [he did not know of the 14-day limit / changed lawyers] — explain on affidavit. Third, merits: [the sentence appears manifestly excessive because…]. Fourth, also ask for deferment or a stay so that the appeal is not nugatory ([his sentence starts in a week]; 0-37, 0-40). **C** Therefore I would file a criminal motion under s 380 for an extension, with a supporting affidavit, and seek a stay of the sentence pending its hearing.

*Source note: CL7 s26–27 (Amarjeet Singh [27]–[28], [33]; s 380(1); Tan Peng Khoon [38], [45]); PYP21-CRI Q2.3.*

### pcp-w-0-43 — Fresh evidence on appeal (s 392 CPC; Ladd v Marshall)
*Keywords: fresh evidence, additional evidence on appeal, s 392 CPC, Ladd v Marshall, non-availability, reasonable diligence, relevance, reliability, criminal motion, finality, miscarriage of justice*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can rely on [new evidence] in his appeal. **C** The appellate court may admit additional evidence if necessary (s 392 CPC), applying the cumulative *Ladd v Marshall* conditions of non-availability, relevance and reliability; [the evidence is / is not] likely to be admitted because [reason].
**R** In dealing with an appeal the appellate court may, if it thinks additional evidence necessary, take it itself or direct the trial court to take it (s 392(1) CPC). The evidence must satisfy three cumulative conditions: it could not have been obtained with reasonable diligence for the trial (non-availability); it is relevant; and it is reliable (*Ladd v Marshall*). The test balances avoiding a miscarriage of justice against finality.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 392(1): appellate court may take additional evidence itself or direct the trial court to take it, if necessary.
> - *Ladd v Marshall* (cumulative):
>   - non-availability: could not have been obtained with reasonable diligence for use at trial;
>   - relevance;
>   - reliability.
> - Brought by criminal motion with the appeal (CL7 s26).

**P** The conditions balance two opposing considerations: avoiding a miscarriage of justice and ensuring finality in proceedings (CL7 s28).

**A** First, non-availability: [the CCTV was only disclosed after trial / a witness surfaced later] — could it have been found with reasonable diligence? Second, relevance: [it goes to identity]. Third, reliability: [timestamped footage — reliable]. **C** Therefore I would file a criminal motion under s 392 to adduce [the evidence], which [is likely to] satisfy *Ladd v Marshall*.

*Source note: CL7 s26, s28 (s 392(1); Ladd v Marshall conditions).*

## 1.I Therapeutic justice, mediation and the family lawyer

- **How should the family lawyer approach the case? (TJ)**
  - Forward-looking, problem-solving, collaborative, judge-led; counsel are not mouthpieces (*VDZ v VEA*; *VDX v VDY*; *BOI v BOJ*) → [1-1](#pcp-w-1-1)
- **Client asks about mediation: advantages, process, how to prepare**
  - My duties: tell the client of all dispute resolution options, advise amicable resolution, child's welfare (PCR r 15A) → [1-2](#pcp-w-1-2)
  - Court must consider reconciliation (s 139F); must order mediation/counselling where children under 21 (s 139I); may advise otherwise (s 139J) → [1-2](#pcp-w-1-2)
  - FJC process: FDR conference, counselling, mediation, co-mediation; documents to bring; confidential → [1-2](#pcp-w-1-2)
- **Before filing: offer of amicable resolution; case conferences** → [1-3](#pcp-w-1-3)

### pcp-w-1-1 — Therapeutic justice and the family lawyer's role
*Keywords: therapeutic justice, TJ, TJ Model, lens of care, forward-looking, problem-solving, non-adversarial, judge-led, VDZ v VEA, VDX v VDY, BOI v BOJ, not mouthpieces, parental responsibility, s 46 WC, co-parenting, costs orders*

**I** The issue is how [Client]'s [divorce / children's] dispute should be approached in light of the Family Justice Courts' therapeutic justice approach. **C** [Client] should be advised that the court will take a non-adversarial, forward-looking, problem-solving approach focused on the family's and children's future, and that I will conduct the case collaboratively rather than to "win" against [Spouse].
**R** Therapeutic justice is a non-adversarial, problem-solving system that allows healing and the recasting of a positive future; it is "not merely an ideal" but "a necessity" (*VDZ v VEA* [2020] 2 SLR 858 at [75], [77]). Family practitioners must be aware of how they influence parenting disputes, including through the language of correspondence, and work as a team to problem-solve (*VDX v VDY* [2021] SGHCF 2 at [40]). Counsel are not mere mouthpieces of their clients and must counsel, and if necessary admonish, them (*BOI v BOJ* [2018] SGCA 61 at [139]).

> **Rule in full**
> - TJ Model (FJC, 21 Oct 2024; FJC PD 2024 Part 7I): "helping families accept the past and move towards their best possible future … a judge-led process where parties and their lawyers, along with other professionals, work together to find timely and enduring solutions … within the framework of the law" — forward-looking; non-adversarial, judge-led; collaborative; problem-solving; multi-disciplinary; procedural fairness.
> - *VDZ v VEA* [75]: non-adversarial, problem-solving; the journey should allow healing, restoring and recasting a positive future; [77]: TJ is a necessity; damaged relationships cannot be repaired by material recompense; healing must take place.
> - *VDX v VDY* [40]: counsel must be aware of how they influence the parenting dispute (language, mindset); working as a team delivers TJ.
> - *BOI v BOJ* [139]: counsel are not mere mouthpieces; must counsel and even admonish emotional clients and observe their duty to the court; guard against taking on the client's persona.
> - TJ "hardware" (FL2 s15): Mandatory Co-Parenting Programme (s 94A); offer of amicable resolution (FJ(G)R P 4 r 1); simplified uncontested track; Part 10A WC; judge-led approach (P 8); CAPS; review of access orders; family support programmes.
> - s 46 WC: parental responsibility to safeguard the children's welfare (*VET v VEU* [2020] 4 SLR 1120 at [15]–[16]).
> - TJ Model para 19: costs orders against conduct inconsistent with TJ (FL2 s32).

**P** Cyril notes that TJ is "not an examinable doctrine but an approach and mindset" (Cyril-Exam §9); use it to frame advice, not as a stand-alone rule.

**A** First, frame the advice: [Client] wants to [use the divorce to punish Spouse's adultery / cut the father out of the children's lives]. Explain that the court looks forward, not back; that the children's welfare is paramount; and that hostile tactics may be counter-productive and attract costs (TJ Model para 19). Second, explain how I will act: measured correspondence, cooperation with opposing counsel on parenting issues (*VDX v VDY*), and candid advice to [Client] where his position is unreasonable (*BOI v BOJ*). **C** Therefore [Client] should approach the dispute in a way that supports a workable co-parenting future, which is also how the court will assess it.

*Source note: FL-SYL [1] (VDZ v VEA [75]–[79]; VDX v VDY [40]); FL1 s3–6; FL2 s4–32 (TJ Model; VDZ [75], [77]; hardware; VET v VEU; VDX [40]; BOI v BOJ [139]; para 19); Cyril-Exam §9.*

### pcp-w-1-2 — Mediation and counselling: PCR r 15A, Part 10A WC (ss 139F, 139I, 139J) and FJC mediation
*Keywords: mediation, counselling, family support programme, PCR r 15A, dispute resolution options, amicable resolution, Part 10A WC, s 139F reconciliation, s 139I mandatory mediation and counselling, children below 21, s 139J, s 139B confidentiality, FDR conference, Family Dispute Resolution, judge mediator, co-mediation, advantages of mediation, prepare for mediation, documents, consent order, $2 million, private mediation*

**I** The issue is [what the advantages and process of mediation are and how [Client] should prepare / whether [Client] must attend mediation or counselling]. **C** Because [Client]'s divorce involves children under 21, the court must order the parties to attend mediation and/or counselling under s 139I WC unless it is not in their interests; mediation at the FJC is confidential, keeps the outcome in the parties' hands, and saves cost and acrimony, and [Client] should prepare by [documents and realistic positions].
**R** A practitioner in family proceedings must, from the time they are contemplated, inform the client of all reasonably available dispute resolution options, advise amicable resolution where reasonably possible, advise the client to be constructive, and advise the client to consider the child's welfare (r 15A PCR). The court must from time to time consider the possibility of reconciliation (s 139F WC). In proceedings for divorce, nullity or judicial separation where there are children below 21, the court must order mediation and/or counselling unless not in the interests of the parties or children (s 139I); in other proceedings it may advise attendance (s 139J). Things said in mediation or counselling are inadmissible (s 139B).

> **Rule in full**
> - PCR r 15A (family proceedings): must (a) inform the client of all reasonably available dispute resolution options (including mediation and counselling); (b) advise the client to consider resolving amicably whenever reasonably possible; (c) advise the client to be constructive and reasonable; (d) advise the client to consider the child's welfare and the potentially adverse impact of proceedings on the child, and that the court decides on the child's welfare, not the parents' or child's wishes.
> - s 139F: court considers reconciliation, having regard to the nature of the case, the evidence and the parties' attitude; may adjourn, interview the parties (with consent), nominate a conciliation officer, advise a family support programme.
> - s 139I: in Part 10 proceedings (divorce, nullity, judicial separation) with children below 21 (or prescribed classes), the court MUST order mediation and/or counselling for the parties and/or children, and MAY advise a family support programme; may dispense if not in their interests. Non-compliance: stay, costs, any order; failure to follow advice is not contempt.
> - s 139J: other proceedings — court may advise mediation, counselling or a programme; same consequences.
> - s 139B (Cyril-Exam §8): anything said or done or prepared for reconciliation, mediation, counselling or a programme is inadmissible.
> - FJC process (FL8 s8–14): FDR conference (judge mediator, solicitors and clients, court counsellor; sets the issues); counselling / intake session (parties alone; children's arrangements); mediation session (judge mediator, solicitors and clients; all issues, focus on children); co-mediation (high-conflict custody); settlement recorded in a draft consent order.
> - FJC PD: no court mediation for contested asset issues where gross known assets are $2 million or more and no contested child issues (from 1 Jan 2019), or ICAA applications (from 1 Jul 2020) — parties advised to use private mediation.
> - Advantages (FL8 s25–26): non-adversarial; parties decide; control over the outcome; avoids an imposed decision; practical solutions without blame; saves time and costs; avoids the trauma of contested hearings.
> - Preparation (FL8 s15–21): explain process and purpose; explore options and advise whether positions are reasonable; open mind and readiness to compromise; written case summary; documents (CPF statements, housing loan, bank statements, valuation, renovation costs, payslips, tax, vehicle and hire-purchase documents); for maintenance, income and monthly expenses; for assets, a list with values.

**P** Contested divorces may still be resolved at mediation: counselling helps where the parties doubt whether the marriage has broken down, and once breakdown is clear the parties explore an uncontested divorce (FL8 s17; FL-CS1 s15).

**A** First, discharge r 15A: tell [Client] of mediation, counselling and the FJC's and private options, and advise amicable resolution. Second, explain that with [two children under 21] the court must order mediation/counselling (s 139I). Third, describe the process and its advantages for [Client], including confidentiality (s 139B) [important for parents who want to avoid publicity]. Fourth, prepare: a written summary, a list of assets and values with documents, income and expense evidence, and realistic ranges for division and maintenance; focus discussions on the children's arrangements. [If the asset pool exceeds $2 million and children are not contested, the court will not mediate assets; suggest private mediation.] **C** Therefore [Client] should approach mediation prepared and with an open mind, as it offers the best chance of a durable settlement recorded as a consent order.

*Source note: FL-SYL [96]–[97]; FL8 s2–28 (ss 139F, 139I, 139J; process; PD thresholds; r 15A; advantages; preparation); FL1 s5, s9; Cyril-Exam §8 (s 139B and Part 10A summary); PYP21-FAM Q3, PYP22-FAM Q3.*

### pcp-w-1-3 — Offer of amicable resolution and the judge-led approach (FJ(G)R Parts 4 and 8)
*Keywords: offer of amicable resolution, FJ(G)R Part 4 rule 1, reasonable grounds, reject offer, costs, indemnity costs, Part 22 rule 5, offer to settle, judge-led approach, Part 8, case conference, assigned judge, Objectives, child's welfare paramount, without prejudice at case conference*

**I** The issue is what [Client] must do before filing, and how the court will manage the case. **C** [Client] must make an offer of amicable resolution before commencing proceedings unless there are reasonable grounds not to (FJ(G)R P 4 r 1(2)), and should not reject [Spouse]'s offer without reasonable grounds, on pain of costs; the case will then be judge-led through case conferences (P 8).
**R** A party must make an offer of amicable resolution before commencing an action unless there are reasonable grounds not to, and must not reject one without reasonable grounds (FJ(G)R P 4 r 1(2)–(3)). The court may disallow or reduce costs, or order costs against, a party who did not make an offer (P 22 r 5(c)), and an offering party who obtains a judgment no less favourable than the offer is, subject to discretion, entitled to indemnity costs from the date of the offer (P 4 r 4(3)). The court takes a judge-led approach, managing issues, evidence and timelines through case conferences (P 8).

> **Rule in full**
> - FJ(G)R 2024 applies to proceedings commenced on or after 15 Oct 2024 (and variations filed on or after that date); FJR 2014 to earlier proceedings.
> - Objectives (P 1 r 4): fair access to justice; expeditious proceedings; cost-effective resolution; efficient use of court resources; fair and practical results; the child's welfare as paramount.
> - P 4 r 1(2): offer of amicable resolution before commencement unless reasonable grounds; r 1(3): not to reject without reasonable grounds; replaces the FJR 2014 "offer to settle".
> - P 22 r 5(c): costs consequences for not making an offer; P 4 r 4(3): indemnity costs from the offer date if the result is no less favourable.
> - Judge-led approach (P 8): identify issues and parties; ensure relevant evidence; control timelines. Powers include ordering mediation and counselling, allowing oral applications, interviewing children, appointing experts or independent counsel, limiting affidavits and submissions, restricting disclosure.
> - Case conferences (P 8 r 5): consider settlement; give directions; dispense with or extend requirements; make orders for a child's welfare summarily, after hearing the parties; WP or confidential communications disclosed at a case conference remain inadmissible (P 8 r 7). Assigned judge (PD para 79).

**P** The offer of amicable resolution is part of the TJ "hardware" before divorce, alongside the Co-Parenting Programme (FL2 s15).

**A** First, before filing, advise [Client] to send [Spouse] an offer of amicable resolution on [divorce / ancillary matters], unless there are reasonable grounds not to ([family violence]). Second, consider any offer received on its merits; rejecting it without reasonable grounds risks costs. Third, explain the judge-led process: case conferences to narrow issues and move towards resolution. **C** Therefore [Client] should make a reasonable offer before filing and engage with case management.

*Source note: FL9 s2–6 (FJ(G)R 2024 commencement; Objectives P 1 r 4; P 4 r 1(2)–(3), r 4(3); P 22 r 5(c)), s12–15 (P 8; case conferences; PD paras 79–80).*

## 1.II Before filing: programmes, jurisdiction and procedure

- **Client wants a divorce — any programme first?**
  - Children under 21: Mandatory Co-Parenting Programme before filing (s 94A); certificates filed → [1-4](#pcp-w-1-4)
- **Do the Singapore courts have jurisdiction? (foreigners, PRs, expatriates)**
  - Either party domiciled in Singapore (citizens deemed, s 3(5)), or habitually resident 3 years before filing (s 93) → [1-5](#pcp-w-1-5)
  - Employment Pass holders here 5 years: habitual residence; check long absences (*Lee Mei-Chih*) → [1-5](#pcp-w-1-5)
  - Nullity: also if both reside in Singapore → [1-5](#pcp-w-1-5)
- **How is a divorce started and run under the 2024 Rules?**
  - Originating application (Form 2); contested and uncontested tracks; ancillary matters after Interim Judgment; disclosure → [1-6](#pcp-w-1-6)
- **Client doesn't want, or can't yet get, a divorce**
  - Children: Guardianship of Infants Act (ss 3, 5); maintenance during marriage (s 69); property dispute (s 59); judicial separation (s 101) → [1-7](#pcp-w-1-7)
- **Draft the Statement of Particulars** → [1-42](#pcp-w-1-42)

### pcp-w-1-4 — Mandatory Co-Parenting Programme (s 94A WC)
*Keywords: Mandatory Co-Parenting Programme, CPP, parenting programme, mandatory parenting programme, s 94A WC, children below 21, before filing divorce, certificate, counterclaim, stay of proceedings, costs, required programmes*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] must attend any programme before commencing divorce proceedings. **C** Yes: since [Client] and [Spouse] have [a child] below 21, [Client] must complete the Mandatory Co-Parenting Programme under s 94A WC before filing; without it he cannot file the divorce application [and Spouse cannot file a counterclaim without completing it].
**R** A parent of a child below 21 must complete an approved parenting programme within the prescribed time before filing for divorce or a counterclaim (s 94A WC). The court may stay proceedings until a defaulting party completes the programme and make costs orders against him, and may order parties to attend at any time (s 94A(5), (7)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 94A(1): every parent with a child below 21 must complete a parenting programme within the time prescribed (s 180 WC).
> - s 94A(3): no originating application for divorce, and no cross-application, may be filed by a prescribed party unless he (a) has completed the programme, (b) is an excluded party, or (c) has the court's permission under (4).
> - s 94A(8): anything said or prepared in the programme is inadmissible.
> - s 94A(5): court may order parties to complete a programme at any time.
> - s 94A(7)(b): if not completed, court may stay proceedings until the defaulting party completes it and order costs.
> - Certificates of completion of both parties are filed with the divorce application (FL9 s9).
> - Distinct from post-filing mandatory mediation/counselling under s 139I (1-2).

**P** In the 2026 sample the guide answer required s 94A — a certificate of completion filed with the divorce papers, including any counterclaim — and distinguished it from s 139I mediation and counselling after filing (SQ guide p126–127).

**A** First, confirm there is a child below 21: [one daughter aged 9 months / two children aged 7 and 9]. Second, advise [Client] to complete the Co-Parenting Programme before filing; [Spouse] must also complete it to file a counterclaim, and its purpose is to help parents understand the impact of divorce on children and plan co-parenting arrangements. **C** Therefore [Client] must attend the Co-Parenting Programme before commencing the divorce.

*Source note: FL-SYL [2]–[3]; FL2 s15; FL9 s8–9; FL-CS1 s10; WC-Text s 94A(1), (3)–(8); Cyril-Exam §1; SQ guide p126–127; PYP23-FAM Q3.2, PYP25 Q3.1.*

### pcp-w-1-5 — Jurisdiction for divorce and nullity: domicile or habitual residence (s 93 WC)
*Keywords: jurisdiction, Family Justice Courts, s 93 WC, domicile, s 3(5) WC, citizen deemed domiciled, domicile of origin, domicile of choice, Peter Rogers May v Pinder Lillian Gek Lian, habitual residence, 3 years, Lee Mei-Chih v Chang Kuo Yuan, absences, Employment Pass, foreigner, PR, Malaysian, Vietnamese, US expatriate, nullity both reside, forum non conveniens, stay of divorce, proceedings overseas, parallel proceedings, lis alibi pendens, Spiliada, more appropriate forum, BDA v BDB, AZS v AZR, TDX v TDY, custody proceedings abroad*

**I** The issue is whether the Family Justice Courts have jurisdiction to hear [Client]'s divorce. **C** They do, because [Client / Spouse] is [domiciled in Singapore as a citizen (s 3(5) WC) / has been habitually resident in Singapore for the three years before commencement] (s 93(1) WC).
**R** The court has jurisdiction in divorce only if either party is domiciled in Singapore at commencement, or habitually resident in Singapore for three years immediately preceding it (s 93(1) WC); for nullity it is enough that both parties reside in Singapore (s 93(2)). A citizen is deemed domiciled here until the contrary is proved (s 3(5)). A domicile of choice requires voluntary residence as an inhabitant and an intention to remain indefinitely (*Peter Rogers May v Pinder Lillian Gek Lian* [2009] SGHC 90). Habitual residence means normal residence apart from temporary absences, voluntarily adopted with a settled purpose (*Lee Mei-Chih v Chang Kuo Yuan* [2012] SGHC 180). Having jurisdiction, the court may still stay the proceedings if another forum is clearly or distinctly more appropriate (forum non conveniens), applying the two-stage *Spiliada* test (*BDA v BDB* [2012] SGHC 209 at [17], [21]–[22]; *AZS v AZR* [2013] SGHC 102 at [11]); in custody proceedings the question is which forum would more effectively evaluate the child's best interests (*TDX v TDY* [2015] SGHCF 4 at [14]–[15]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 93(1): either party (a) domiciled in Singapore at commencement; or (b) habitually resident in Singapore for 3 years immediately preceding commencement.
> - Nullity: sufficient if both parties reside in Singapore at commencement (s 93(2); FL3 s8 note).
> - Domicile = where one intends one's permanent home:
>   - deemed: a citizen is deemed domiciled until the contrary is proved (s 3(5));
>   - origin: at birth, the father's (legitimate) or mother's (illegitimate or posthumous) domicile, until a domicile of choice or dependence is acquired (*Peter Rogers May* [2006] SGHC 39 at [13]);
>   - choice: (i) voluntary residence as an inhabitant, not a casual visitor; (ii) intention to remain indefinitely; physical presence required; length of residence is evidence of intention (*Peter Rogers May* [2009] SGHC 90).
> - Forum non conveniens (FL-LP competency 4: cross-border family disputes; no FL-SYL paragraph or slide):
>   - applies to civil family proceedings, e.g. a s 69 maintenance application (*BDA v BDB* [2012] SGHC 209 at [17]) and divorce (*AZS v AZR* [2013] SGHC 102 at [11]);
>   - stage 1: is the other forum "clearly or distinctly more appropriate"?; stage 2: are there special circumstances (e.g. juridical advantage) for which justice requires that a stay be refused? (*BDA v BDB* at [21]–[22]); "essentially a factors-based test" ([24]); nationality is of limited significance, residence and domicile better show connection ([29]);
>   - lis alibi pendens: parallel proceedings abroad are an important factor but carry little weight if started for strategic reasons (*AZS v AZR* at [13], [22]); France held clearly more appropriate and the divorce stayed ([23]);
>   - custody: *Spiliada* applies; the child's welfare is paramount, but the question is which forum "would more effectively evaluate the best interests of the child"; decide the forum first, then any fresh orders; habitual residence is relevant but not the sole factor; Hong Kong, first seised and further advanced, was preferred since two sets of proceedings were not in the child's welfare (*TDX v TDY* [2015] SGHCF 4 at [12]–[15], [20]–[22], [33], [56]–[62]); under the ICAA, by contrast, habitual residence can be conclusive (*TUC v TUD* [2017] SGHCF 12 at [53]–[56]) (1-23).
> - Habitual residence (*Lee Mei-Chih*): same as "ordinarily resident"; habitually and normally resident apart from temporary or occasional absences; voluntarily adopted; a degree of settled purpose (general or specific — education, business, employment, family); whether absences break the 3-year continuum — 12 months' total absence held substantial, so not habitually resident.

**P** In *Lee Mei-Chih* a total of 12 months' absence on overseas trips within the three years broke habitual residence, despite legitimate reasons (FL3 s11).

**A** First, check domicile: [Peter is a Singapore citizen, so deemed domiciled (s 3(5)) — jurisdiction under s 93(1)(a)]. Second, if neither is a citizen, check habitual residence: [Bao Ho and Linh, Vietnamese, have lived and worked here on Employment Passes for 5 years; their residence is voluntary with a settled purpose (employment, children's schooling)] — satisfied unless long absences break the three years (*Lee Mei-Chih*). [A Malaysian couple who moved here in June 2020 and have not returned meet the 3 years by [date].] [A domicile of choice may also be argued if they intend to stay indefinitely.] Third, if [Spouse] has started proceedings abroad or applies for a stay: is [country] clearly more appropriate — where do they reside, where are the assets and children, which court is first seised and further advanced (*BDA v BDB*; *AZS v AZR*; for children, *TDX v TDY*)? **C** Therefore the court has jurisdiction under s 93(1)([a/b]) WC [and is unlikely to stay the proceedings].

*Source note: FL-SYL [8]; FL3 s8–11 (s 93; s 3(5); Peter Rogers May [2006] [13] and [2009]; Lee Mei-Chih); Cyril-Exam §2.2, §3.1 (s 93(2) nullity); FL-LP competency 4; eLit (BDA v BDB [17]–[33]; AZS v AZR [11]–[23]; TDX v TDY [12]–[62]; TUC v TUD [53]–[56]); SQ guide p126; PYP23-FAM Q3.1, PYP25 Q3.1, PYP21-FAM Q1.*

### pcp-w-1-6 — Divorce procedure under the Family Justice (General) Rules 2024
*Keywords: divorce procedure, FJ(G)R 2024, originating application for divorce, Form 2, writ for divorce, Applicant, Respondent, contested divorce, uncontested divorce, simplified track, brief particulars, Form 4, notice to contest, 14 days, cross-application, AEIC, interim judgment, ancillary matters, First Ancillary Affidavit, Form 15, 28 days, disclosure, Form 84, Form 17, consent, originating application Form 53*

**I** The issue is how [Client]'s divorce will proceed under the Family Justice (General) Rules 2024. **C** [Client] will file a matrimonial (originating) application for divorce in Form 2; if [Spouse] contests, the divorce goes to trial on the contested track; once Interim Judgment is granted, the ancillary matters proceed by ancillary affidavits, disclosure and a hearing (or by consent on the uncontested track).
**R** The FJ(G)R 2024 apply to proceedings commenced on or after 15 October 2024, with "Applicant/Respondent" and "originating application for divorce" replacing "Plaintiff/Defendant" and "writ". Divorce is a two-stage process: dissolution of the marriage, then ancillary matters (custody, care and control, access, maintenance, division of assets, costs).

> **Rule in full**
> - Contested track (FL9 s8): pre-filing (CPP s 94A; offer of amicable resolution) → OA in Form 2 (P 2 r 2: fact under s 95A; brief particulars; claims for ancillary relief) → Respondent files a notice to contest in Form 4 within 14 days (P 2 r 4) to object to the divorce or ancillary relief, make ancillary claims, file a cross-application or challenge jurisdiction → if objecting to the divorce, Respondent files a Reply (and any cross-application) within 28 days with brief particulars of the objections (P 2 r 5) → exchange of AEICs (all material facts and evidence; objections to an AEIC in Form 101 within 14 days; P 15 r 16) → request for trial in Form 6 (P 2 r 7) → trial (order of trial P 15 r 13); mediation along the way.
> - Uncontested track (FL9 s9): Form 2, AEIC verifying it, Respondent's consent (to the application, ancillary claims and manner of service), CPP certificates, draft ancillary reliefs order (and CPF checklist) if ancillary matters agreed, request for hearing.
> - Ancillary matters (FL9 s10): First Ancillary Affidavit in Form 15 within 28 days after Interim Judgment (P 2 r 16); disclosure under Part 9 (summons in Form 84, objection or compliance affidavit); Second Ancillary Affidavit (P 2 r 16(3)); binding summaries in Form 17 (P 2 r 18); hearing and orders.
> - Other originating applications (FL9 s11): Form 53 with supporting affidavit; reasonable steps to serve personally within 14 days (28 if abroad); Respondent's affidavit or jurisdiction challenge within 14 days of service (28 if abroad); may counterclaim.
> - FL-CS1 s15: may start contested but be directed to mediation and counselling to reach an uncontested divorce and consent orders.

**P** The slides present this as a flow; the Form and rule numbers above are read from the slide images (FL9 s8–11), checked visually on 10 Nov 2026.

**A** First, choose the track: [Spouse will consent to divorce on mutual agreement and ancillary matters are agreed — uncontested, simplified]; [Spouse denies adultery — he may file Form 4 within 14 days to contest; consider a fact he will accept (1-11 to 1-16)]. Second, plan the ancillary stage after Interim Judgment: prepare [Client]'s Form 15 affidavit with documents within 28 days, and seek disclosure of [Spouse]'s assets if needed (1-37). **C** Therefore [Client] should file in Form 2 on [fact], aiming for an uncontested track, and prepare the ancillary affidavit for filing after Interim Judgment.

*Source note: FL9 s2–4, s7–11; FL-CS1 s4, s15 (terminology changes; two-stage process). Form and rule numbers read from the FL9 slide images.*

### pcp-w-1-7 — Relief without divorce: Guardianship of Infants Act, maintenance, property disputes, judicial separation
*Keywords: no divorce, unmarried parents, Guardianship of Infants Act, GIA s 3, GIA s 5, within 3 years of marriage, children arrangements without divorce, maintenance during marriage, s 69 WC, MSS, s 59 WC property dispute, judicial separation, s 101 WC, deed of separation, PPO without divorce*

**I** The issue is what relief [Client] can obtain without filing for divorce, given that [the parties have been married under three years / he does not want a divorce / they are unmarried]. **C** [Client] may apply under the Guardianship of Infants Act for orders on the children, under s 69 WC for maintenance for herself and the children, under s 59 WC on a property dispute, for a protection order, or for judicial separation under s 101 WC, which allows the court to make ancillary orders.
**R** Applications for custody, care and control and access may be made under ss 3 and 5 of the Guardianship of Infants Act where there are no divorce proceedings (e.g. unmarried parents, or married parents within the three-year bar), with the child's welfare paramount (s 3 GIA). Maintenance during the marriage (ss 68–70 WC), protection orders (Part 7) and property disputes (s 59 WC) are available independently of divorce. Judicial separation is available on the same facts as divorce (s 101 WC) and empowers the court to make ancillary orders.

> **Rule in full**
> - GIA ss 3, 5 (FL4 s3; FL-CS1 s17): unmarried parents; married parents with no divorce proceedings; married parents barred by s 94 (3-year bar); non-parents. s 3: welfare of the infant is the first and paramount consideration; neither parent has a superior right.
> - Without divorce (FL6 s43): (1) wife's or incapacitated husband's maintenance (s 69, standalone); (2) protection orders against family violence (Part 7); (3) child maintenance (ss 68–70; same principles); (4) custody etc. under the GIA; (5) property disputes during marriage (s 59; general property law applies).
> - Judicial separation (FL-CS1 s7): s 101 WC; different from a deed of separation; reason to apply — the court may make ancillary orders. Cyril: where adultery is known within the first 3 years, judicial separation may be sought, and a judgment of judicial separation relied on later in divorce proceedings (Cyril-Exam §3.3.1). s 101(1): either party may apply for judicial separation on the s 95A facts, ss 95 and 95A applying with modifications; s 101(2): after judgment the applicant need no longer cohabit; s 102: judicial separation is no bar to a later divorce application (WC-Text).

**P** A wife who files under s 69 while divorce proceedings are imminent should note that once the marriage ends she must claim under s 113 instead (FL5 s10; Cyril-Exam §5.2.2) — see 1-25.

**A** First, identify why divorce is not available or wanted: [married only 18 months]. Second, match the relief: [children — GIA application for care and control and access]; [money — s 69 maintenance for her and the children]; [violence — PPO (1-38)]; [a formal separation with ancillary orders — judicial separation]. Third, note that divorce can follow after three years, or earlier on exceptional hardship or depravity (1-10). **C** Therefore [Client] should apply for [relief] now without divorcing.

*Source note: FL4 s3, s16 (GIA ss 3, 5); FL6 s43; FL-CS1 s7, s17; Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 and §4 (judicial separation; GIA — secondary).*

## 1.III Nullity

- **Is the marriage void or voidable?**
  - Void from the start (Muslims, subsisting marriage, under 18 without licence, prohibited degrees, marriage of convenience, same sex, no valid licence) (s 105) → [1-8](#pcp-w-1-8)
  - Voidable until annulled: non-consummation (incapacity or wilful refusal), no valid consent, mental disorder, VD, pregnant by another (s 106) → [1-9](#pcp-w-1-9)
  - Sham or immigration marriage: not mistake (*Tan Ah Thee*); void only if s 11A conditions met → [1-8](#pcp-w-1-8), [1-9](#pcp-w-1-9)
- **Bars: conduct leading Respondent to believe no annulment; 3 years; knowledge** (s 107) → [1-9](#pcp-w-1-9)

### pcp-w-1-8 — Void marriages (s 105 WC); void and voidable distinguished
*Keywords: nullity, void marriage, void ab initio, s 105 WC, s 3(4) Muslims, subsisting marriage, bigamy, s 11, under 18, s 9, special marriage licence, prohibited degrees, s 10, marriage of convenience, s 11A, immigration advantage, gratification, same sex, s 12, no valid licence, s 13, s 104 judgment of nullity, voidable distinguished, Tan Ah Thee*

**I** The issue is whether [Client]'s marriage is void. **C** The marriage is void under s 105 WC because it is invalid under [s 11 — Spouse was still married to someone else / s 11A — it was a marriage of convenience], so it never existed in law; [Client] may nonetheless seek a judgment of nullity (s 104) for certainty.
**R** A marriage is void if invalid under s 3(4) (both parties Muslim), ss 5 and 11 (subsisting prior marriage), s 9 (party under 18 without a special licence), s 10 (prohibited degrees), s 11A (marriage of convenience, for marriages after 1 October 2016), s 12 (same sex) or s 13 (no valid licence or unauthorised solemniser) (s 105 WC). A void marriage is void whether or not a judgment is obtained, but either spouse may seek a judgment of nullity (s 104). A voidable marriage, by contrast, is valid until annulled (*Tan Ah Thee v Lim Soo Foong* [2009] 3 SLR(R) 957).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 105: void if invalid by virtue of:
>   - s 3(4): both parties Muslim;
>   - ss 5 and 11: subsisting prior marriage;
>   - s 9: party below 18 without a special marriage licence;
>   - s 10: parties within prohibited degrees;
>   - s 11A: marriage of convenience (after 1 Oct 2016);
>   - s 12: parties of the same sex;
>   - s 13: solemnised without a valid licence or by an unauthorised person.
> - s 11A(1): (a) a party enters the marriage knowing or having reason to believe its purpose is to assist a party to obtain an immigration advantage; and (b) gratification is offered, given or received as an inducement or reward for entering it. s 11A(2): not void if both parties believed on reasonable grounds that it would result in a genuine marital relationship.
> - s 104: either spouse may seek a judgment of nullity; a void marriage is void whether or not a judgment is obtained.
> - Void vs voidable (FL3 s4; FL-CS1 s7): void = void from the beginning; voidable = valid until annulled; a judgment of nullity of a voidable marriage annuls it only from the time the judgment is made final, and the marriage is treated as having existed until then (s 110(2)).
> - Only parties to the marriage may seek a judgment of nullity (Cyril-Exam §2.2, citing *Tan Ah Thee*).

**P** In *Tan Ah Thee* the High Court held that an improper motive for marrying does not make a marriage void or voidable; sham marriages are valid unless they fall within s 11A (FL3 s4, s7).

**A** First, find the defect: [Spouse's earlier marriage in [country] was never dissolved — s 11]; [the marriage was arranged so that Spouse could obtain a long-term visit pass, and $X was paid — s 11A, unless both reasonably believed it would be genuine]. Second, conclude that the marriage is void ab initio and advise a judgment of nullity for certainty (s 104), on jurisdiction under s 93 (both residing in Singapore suffices for nullity, 1-5). **C** Therefore the marriage is void under s 105 read with s [X] WC.

*Source note: FL-SYL [4]–[5]; FL3 s3–4, s7 (s 105; s 104; s 11A(1)–(2); Tan Ah Thee); FL-CS1 s7, s11; WC-Text s 110(2); Cyril-Exam §2.1–2.3.*

### pcp-w-1-9 — Voidable marriages and bars to relief (ss 106, 107 WC)
*Keywords: voidable marriage, s 106 WC, non-consummation, incapacity, wilful refusal, Kwong Sin Hwa, Chinese customary rites, Heng Joo See, deceive the court, lack of consent, duress, mistake, unsoundness of mind, Tan Ah Thee, mental disorder unfit for marriage, venereal disease, pregnant by another man, s 107 bars, 3 years, ignorant of facts, approbation*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain a judgment of nullity on the ground that [Spouse wilfully refused to consummate the marriage]. **C** The marriage is voidable under s 106(b) WC, and [Client] may have it annulled, since none of the bars in s 107 applies.
**R** A marriage is voidable only on the grounds in s 106 WC: non-consummation owing to either party's incapacity (a) or the respondent's wilful refusal (b); lack of valid consent through duress, mistake, unsoundness of mind or otherwise (c); mental disorder making a party unfit for marriage (d); the respondent's communicable venereal disease (e); or the respondent's pregnancy by another man (f). Refusing to undergo an agreed pre-condition to consummation can be wilful refusal (*Kwong Sin Hwa v Lau Lee Yen* [1993] 1 SLR(R) 90). Relief is barred in the circumstances in s 107.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 106 (exhaustive): (a) non-consummation due to incapacity of either party; (b) non-consummation due to the respondent's wilful refusal; (c) either party did not validly consent (duress, mistake, unsoundness of mind or otherwise); (d) either party, though capable of consenting, was suffering from mental disorder making him unfit for marriage; (e) respondent had a communicable venereal disease at the time of marriage; (f) respondent was pregnant by another man at the time of marriage.
> - *Kwong Sin Hwa*: a condition to go through Chinese rites before consummation is not unlawful; refusing the pre-condition can be wilful refusal.
> - *Heng Joo See v Ho Pol Ling* [1993] 2 SLR(R) 763: non-consummation is easy to allege; the court rescinded a judgment obtained by deception — duty not to deceive the court.
> - *Tan Ah Thee*: mistake vitiating consent is narrow; improper motives are not a ground.
> - s 107 bars:
>   - (1) the respondent satisfies the court that (a) the applicant, knowing it was open to him to avoid the marriage, so conducted himself towards the respondent as to lead the respondent reasonably to believe he would not; and (b) it would be unjust to the respondent to grant the judgment;
>   - (2) for s 106(c)–(f), proceedings must be instituted within 3 years of the marriage;
>   - (3) for s 106(e)–(f), the applicant must have been ignorant of the facts alleged at the time of the marriage;
>   - (4) (1) replaces the old rules on approbation, ratification or lack of sincerity.

**P** Wilful non-consummation is the most common ground; lack of consent is next (FL3 s6–7).

**A** First, identify the ground: [Spouse insisted on a Chinese tea ceremony before consummation, then refused to hold it — wilful refusal by refusing the agreed pre-condition (Kwong Sin Hwa)]. Second, check the bars: [Client did not lead Spouse to think he would not seek annulment]; [for grounds (c)–(f), filed within 3 years]; [for (e)/(f), he did not know]. Third, remind [Client] of the duty of candour (*Heng Joo See*). **C** Therefore [Client] can apply for a judgment of nullity under s 106([b]).

*Source note: FL-SYL [6]–[7]; FL3 s4–7 (s 106; Kwong Sin Hwa; Heng Joo See; Tan Ah Thee); WC-Text s 107(1)–(4); Cyril-Exam §2.4.*

## 1.IV Divorce

- **Married under 3 years — can client file now?**
  - No, unless exceptional hardship to Applicant or exceptional depravity of Respondent (s 94; *Ng Kee Shee*); alternatives: GIA, s 69, PPO, judicial separation → [1-10](#pcp-w-1-10), [1-7](#pcp-w-1-7)
- **What must be proved?**
  - Irretrievable breakdown, proved by one of six facts, and just and reasonable (s 95; s 95A) → [1-11](#pcp-w-1-11)
  - Spouse wants to reconcile / says "I still love her" — not enough (*William Cheng*; *Cheong Kim Seah*) → [1-11](#pcp-w-1-11)
- **Which fact?**
  - Adultery + intolerable; caught on PI report; lived together over 6 months after finding out? → [1-12](#pcp-w-1-12)
  - Respondent denies adultery / no proof: improper association as unreasonable behaviour; mutual agreement → [1-13](#pcp-w-1-13), [1-16](#pcp-w-1-16)
  - Violence, verbal abuse, financial irresponsibility, neglect: unreasonable behaviour (*Wong Siew Boey*) → [1-13](#pcp-w-1-13)
  - Left the home 2 years ago without consent: desertion → [1-14](#pcp-w-1-14)
  - Separate bedrooms, separate households: 3 years with consent, 4 without → [1-15](#pcp-w-1-15)
  - Both agree it's over: mutual agreement in writing (s 95A(6)) → [1-16](#pcp-w-1-16)
- **Does adultery help on custody or assets?** No — fault is generally irrelevant to ancillary matters; only gross conduct → [1-12](#pcp-w-1-12), [1-33](#pcp-w-1-33)

### pcp-w-1-10 — The three-year bar and its exceptions (s 94 WC)
*Keywords: three-year bar, 3 years since marriage, s 94 WC, exceptional hardship, exceptional depravity, Ng Kee Shee v Fu Gaofei, Tan Chew Kwee Jenn, sanctity of marriage, early divorce, leave to file within 3 years*

**I** The issue is whether [Client], married [for 18 months], can file for divorce now. **C** Ordinarily she cannot, because three years have not passed since the marriage (s 94(1) WC); she may do so only if she shows exceptional hardship suffered by her or exceptional depravity by [Spouse], which on these facts is [likely / unlikely].
**R** No divorce may be filed unless three years have passed since the marriage, unless the case is one of exceptional hardship suffered by the applicant or exceptional depravity on the part of the respondent (s 94 WC). Exceptional hardship is "something quite out of the ordinary and more than what an ordinary person should reasonably be asked to bear" (*Ng Kee Shee v Fu Gaofei* [2005] 4 SLR 762 at [23]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 94(1): no divorce application until 3 years have passed since the date of the marriage.
> - Exception: exceptional hardship suffered by the applicant, or exceptional depravity on the part of the respondent (s 94(2)); the court considers the interests of any child and the probability of reconciliation (s 94(3)) and may refer the parties to a conciliation officer (s 94(4)); permission obtained by misrepresentation or concealment may lead to a delayed final judgment or dismissal (s 94(2)(a)–(b)); filing may be based on matters that occurred within the 3 years (s 94(5)).
> - *Ng Kee Shee* [21]: the laudable intention of s 94 is to promote the sanctity of marriage; the wife "had absolutely no regard for the union" and entered the arranged marriage capriciously; making the husband wait would visit her wrongs on him. [23]: exceptional hardship is quite out of the ordinary.
> - Exceptional depravity example: *Tan Chew Kwee Jenn v Tan Chee Kiong John* [2006] SGDC 221 (sex with 16 different women over two years, filmed — Cyril-Exam §3.2.1).

**P** Ordinary marital discord does not amount to exceptional hardship, given the purpose of promoting the sanctity of marriage (*Ng Kee Shee* at [21], [23]).

**A** First, count from the date of marriage to the intended filing: [married Jan 2024; three years on Jan 2027]. Second, if early filing is wanted, assess the exception: [Spouse left within weeks and never returned, admitting the marriage was a mistake — comparable to Ng Kee Shee]; [ordinary unhappiness — not enough]. Third, if the exception fails, offer alternatives: wait; judicial separation; GIA and s 69 applications; a PPO (1-7, 1-38). **C** Therefore [Client] [may apply now on the ground of exceptional hardship / should wait until [date] or pursue interim relief].

*Source note: FL-SYL [9]–[10]; FL3 s12–13 (s 94; Ng Kee Shee [21], [23]; Tan Chew Kwee Jenn); WC-Text s 94(1)–(5); Cyril-Exam §3.2; SQ guide p126.*

### pcp-w-1-11 — The sole ground: irretrievable breakdown, and "just and reasonable" (s 95 WC)
*Keywords: sole ground, irretrievable breakdown, s 95 WC, s 95(2), six facts, s 95A(1), just and reasonable, William Cheng v Chai Mei Leng, reconciliation, unilateral desire to reconcile, Cheong Kim Seah v Lim Poh Choo, professions of love, financial hardship, interests of children, own fault*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain a divorce, given that [Spouse wants to save the marriage]. **C** Yes: the court will grant a divorce if the marriage has irretrievably broken down, proved by one of the facts in s 95A(1) WC, and it is just and reasonable to grant it (s 95(2)); [Spouse]'s unilateral wish to reconcile does not prevent it (*William Cheng v Chai Mei Leng* [1999] SGCA 26).
**R** The sole ground for divorce is that the marriage has irretrievably broken down (s 95 WC), proved by one of six facts in s 95A(1); the applicant cannot rely on his own adultery, behaviour or desertion, and the court must be satisfied that it is just and reasonable to grant the divorce. That proviso is for the most extreme cases where it would still be wrong to dissolve the marriage, considering the parties' conduct and the interests of any children (*William Cheng*). A unilateral desire for reconciliation is not enough without reciprocity (*William Cheng*), and professions of love and care do not rebut a proved breakdown (*Cheong Kim Seah v Lim Poh Choo* [1992] SGHC 231).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 95(2): court must be satisfied that (i) the marriage has irretrievably broken down (primary); (ii) the applicant does not rely on his or her own adultery, unreasonable behaviour or desertion; (iii) it is just and reasonable to grant the divorce (secondary).
> - s 95A(1) six facts: (a) adultery; (b) unreasonable behaviour; (c) desertion for 2 years; (d) 3 years' separation with consent; (e) 4 years' separation; (f) divorce by mutual agreement.
> - *William Cheng*: "just and reasonable" governed by s 95(4) — applies only in the most extreme cases where it would still be wrong to dissolve, having regard to all the circumstances including the parties' conduct and the interests of any children; financial hardship per se insufficient (wife had not shown grave financial hardship, [43]); unilateral desire for reconciliation meaningless without reciprocity.
> - Children (Leong Wai Kum, cited FL3 s25): exceptionally the court might refuse or delay judgment if a child would respond particularly badly; no case law.
> - *Cheong Kim Seah*: once separation is proved or admitted, professions of love and care cannot rebut breakdown.

**P** A refusal on the "just and reasonable" proviso is highly exceptional; where breakdown is proved there is usually no reason not to dissolve the marriage (Cyril-Exam §3.3.2; FL3 s24).

**A** First, identify the fact relied on (1-12 to 1-16) and prove it. Second, [Spouse] says [she still loves him and wants counselling]; under *William Cheng* a one-sided wish to reconcile does not stop the divorce, and her assurances of love do not rebut breakdown (*Cheong Kim Seah*). Third, consider whether anything makes divorce unjust: [her claimed financial hardship is not grave; the children's welfare can be protected by ancillary orders]. **C** Therefore the court is likely to find irretrievable breakdown and grant the divorce.

*Source note: FL-SYL [11]–[14]; FL3 s14–15, s24–26 (s 95(2); s 95A; William Cheng [43]; Leong Wai Kum; Cheong Kim Seah); Cyril-Exam §3.3, §3.3.2.*

### pcp-w-1-12 — Adultery (s 95A(1)(a), (2) WC)
*Keywords: adultery, s 95A(1)(a), intolerable to live with, consensual sexual intercourse, Tan Meng Hock, private investigator, surveillance report, PI report, hotel, identity of co-respondent, admitted adultery, inclination and opportunity, 6 months living together, s 95A(2), forgave and reconciled, new act of adultery, improper association, adultery irrelevant to ancillary matters*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can divorce [Spouse] on the fact of adultery, and the likelihood of success. **C** [Client] can rely on adultery only if she proves that [Spouse] committed adultery and that she finds it intolerable to live with him (s 95A(1)(a) WC); because [she continued to live with him for over six months after learning of it], she cannot rely on [that act] (s 95A(2)), so she should instead rely on [unreasonable behaviour / mutual agreement].
**R** To establish adultery the applicant must show that the respondent committed adultery and that the applicant finds it intolerable to live with the respondent (s 95A(1)(a) WC). Adultery is consensual sexual intercourse (*Tan Meng Hock v Tay Mui Keow* [1992] SGHC 218). Living together for periods totalling up to six months after the adultery became known is disregarded; if they lived together for more than six months after the applicant knew of it, that act cannot be relied on, though later acts can (s 95A(2)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Two elements: (1) the respondent committed adultery; (2) the applicant finds it intolerable to live with the respondent.
> - Adultery = consensual intercourse (*Tan Meng Hock*).
> - Evidence (FL3 s16–17): identify the other person if known; an admission accepted by the court is conclusive; otherwise evidence, usually a private investigator's testimony, or inclination plus opportunity; uncontested divorces proceed on allegations; contested ones need the PI report or other evidence. If no proof, plead improper association as unreasonable behaviour.
> - s 95A(2): (a) living together for periods totalling up to 6 months after the adultery became known is disregarded in deciding intolerability; (b) if they lived together for more than 6 months after the applicant knew, that act cannot be relied on (new acts can).
> - Cyril (Cyril-Exam §3.3.1): the respondent may admit the adultery and the particulars; the other person must be identified if known (FL3 s16); the 6 months is a total and may be made up of several periods (s 95A(2): "for a total of 6 months or less").

**P** The fact relied on does not generally affect the ancillary matters, so a client choosing adultery "to help on custody and assets" should be told it will not: custody is decided on the child's welfare (1-17) and division on contributions, with conduct relevant only if gross (1-33) (FL6 s16–25).

**A** First, the evidence: [PI report of a hotel stay with a colleague; photos and texts] — inclination and opportunity, likely sufficient if contested; [Spouse denies]. Second, intolerability and the six-month rule: [Client found out 8 months ago and kept living with him, trying to reconcile] — over six months, so she cannot rely on that act unless there are later acts; [Celeste obtained the report 4 weeks ago and moved out of the master bedroom long before] — within six months. Third, alternatives: [his behaviour since — the vase incident; improper association] as unreasonable behaviour (1-13), or, since [Spouse] will consent to divorce but not admit adultery, mutual agreement (1-16) or separation (1-15). Fourth, correct the misconception about ancillary matters. **C** Therefore [Client] [can / cannot] proceed on adultery; the safer course is [fact].

*Source note: FL3 s15–17 (s 95A(1)(a), (2); Tan Meng Hock; evidence; improper association); Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 1; FL6 s16, s25; SQ p66 and guide p126–127; PYP23-FAM Q4.1, PYP25 Q3.2, PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-13 — Unreasonable behaviour (s 95A(1)(b), (3) WC; Wong Siew Boey)
*Keywords: unreasonable behaviour, behaviour, s 95A(1)(b), cannot reasonably be expected to live with, Wong Siew Boey v Lee Boon Fatt, subjective objective test, personalities, cumulative effect, active or passive, violence, verbal abuse, financial irresponsibility, gambling, neglect, improper association, s 95A(3), 6 months*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can divorce [Spouse] on the fact of unreasonable behaviour. **C** Yes: [Spouse]'s [violence, gambling and withdrawal of household money] is behaviour such that [Client] cannot reasonably be expected to live with him (s 95A(1)(b) WC), assessed in light of their personalities and the cumulative effect (*Wong Siew Boey v Lee Boon Fatt* [1994] SGHC 35).
**R** The applicant must establish the respondent's behaviour and that, as a result, the applicant cannot reasonably be expected to live with the respondent (s 95A(1)(b) WC). Whether the applicant finds it intolerable is answered subjectively; whether he or she can reasonably be expected to live with the respondent is objective but takes account of the personalities of the parties, the cumulative effect of the conduct, which may be active or passive, and conduct need not be directed at the applicant if relevant to the marriage (*Wong Siew Boey*). Living together for up to six months after the last incident is disregarded (s 95A(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - "Unreasonable" qualifies the expectation that the applicant continue to live with the respondent, not the applicant's behaviour.
> - *Wong Siew Boey*: (1) intolerability answered substantively, reasonableness of the applicant's attitude irrelevant; (2) whether the applicant can reasonably be expected to live with the respondent — objective, but looking at the personalities before the court; (3) cumulative effect (e.g. neglect, indifference); active or passive behaviour; (4) behaviour need not be confined to the applicant but must be relevant to the marriage.
> - Two things: (1) establish the behaviour (e.g. family violence, chronic financial irresponsibility); (2) it makes it unreasonable to expect the applicant to live with the respondent.
> - s 95A(3): a period of living together of up to 6 months is disregarded. Cyril: if more than 6 months, the respondent may argue it shows the behaviour was not unreasonable.
> - Examples (Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 2): improper association; family violence (verbal abuse, physical violence, outbursts); financial irresponsibility; irresponsibility towards children.

**P** Where adultery cannot be proved, the applicant can still rely on improper association as unreasonable behaviour (FL3 s16).

**A** First, list the behaviour: [repeated physical abuse, including on 29 June 2021]; [secret withdrawal of over $1 million from the joint account to fund gambling]; [threw a vase that scarred her face]; [ongoing association with a KTV hostess]. Second, intolerability is subjective — [Client] finds it intolerable. Third, objectively, given [her personality and the cumulative effect], she cannot reasonably be expected to live with him. Fourth, check s 95A(3): [she left immediately / stayed less than 6 months after the last incident]. **C** Therefore [Client] can rely on unreasonable behaviour, which is [the strongest available fact].

*Source note: FL-SYL [15]; FL3 s16, s18–19 (Wong Siew Boey; two elements; s 95A(3)); Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 2; PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM, PYP23-FAM Q4.1, PYP25 Q3.2.*

### pcp-w-1-14 — Desertion for two years (s 95A(1)(c), (4) WC)
*Keywords: desertion, s 95A(1)(c), 2 years, s 92 WC abandonment against will, without consent, actual desertion, constructive desertion, intention to desert, bring marital union to an end, reasonable cause, overseas job, capacity, reconciliation periods, s 95A(4)*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can divorce [Spouse] on the fact of desertion. **C** Yes, if [Spouse] left without [Client]'s consent, intending to end the marriage, and without reasonable cause, for a continuous period of at least two years before filing (s 95A(1)(c) WC); on the facts [this is / is not] satisfied because [reason].
**R** Desertion implies abandonment against the wish of the deserted spouse (s 92 WC).
