The applicant must show that the respondent deserted him or her for a continuous period of at least two years immediately before filing; it requires physical separation and an intention to bring the marital union to an end, which may be inferred from conduct, by a spouse with capacity to form it and without reasonable cause; desertion may be actual or constructive.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 92: "desertion" implies an abandonment against the wish of the person charging it.
> - Elements (FL3 s20): respondent deserted the applicant for at least 2 years; left without the applicant's consent; actual or constructive desertion; proof of the conduct of separation and the intention to desert (may be inferred from conduct where appropriate); intention to end the marital union; capacity to form the intention; no reasonable cause (e.g. necessity of an overseas job or study negates intention).
> - Constructive desertion: conduct equivalent to driving the other spouse out (Cyril-Exam, citing *Seah Cheng Hock* — secondary).
> - s 95A(4): desertion is still continuous even if the parties lived together for a total of 6 months or less during the period, but that time does not count towards the 2 years (so more than 6 months together breaks continuity).

**P** If the separation is by mutual consent there is no desertion, but the time may count towards separation under s 95A(1)(d)–(e) (Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 3) — see 1-15.

**A** First, the conduct: [Spouse moved out on [date], over 2 years ago]. Second, consent and intention: [Client objected; Spouse said the marriage was over and has not returned] — intention inferred; [if he left for an overseas posting, no intention]. Third, continuity: [a 3-month return in between is disregarded but does not count]. **C** Therefore [Client] [can / cannot] rely on desertion.

*Source note: FL3 s20 (s 92; elements); WC-Text s 95A(4); Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 3 (constructive desertion — secondary).*

### pcp-w-1-15 — Separation: three years with consent or four years (s 95A(1)(d)–(e), (5), (7) WC)
*Keywords: separation, living apart, 3 years separation with consent, 4 years separation, s 95A(1)(d), s 95A(1)(e), same roof, separate bedrooms, separate households, s 95A(7), Leong Kwek Keong v Lee Ying Kuan, Tan Lee Tiang v Chia Thuan Hwa, intention to end consortium, no-fault, reconciliation periods*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can divorce on the basis of separation, given that the parties [still live in the same flat but in separate rooms]. **C** Yes: if they have lived apart — separate households and an intention to end the marriage — for [three years and Spouse consents / four years], [Client] may rely on s 95A(1)([d/e]) WC, even under the same roof (s 95A(7)), provided their household is genuinely separate (*Tan Lee Tiang v Chia Thuan Hwa* [1993] SGHC 256).
**R** The applicant may rely on the parties having lived apart for a continuous period of at least three years before filing with the respondent's consent (s 95A(1)(d)), or at least four years without consent (s 95A(1)(e)). Living apart requires separation of households and an intention to end the marital union, not mere physical separation (*Leong Kwek Keong v Lee Ying Kuan* [1990] SGHC 8); parties under the same roof must show separate households (*Tan Lee Tiang*; s 95A(7)).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 95A(1)(d): living apart for a continuous period of at least 3 years immediately preceding filing, and the respondent consents.
> - s 95A(1)(e): living apart for a continuous period of at least 4 years immediately preceding filing.
> - "Living apart": separation of households plus a mental element (intention to end the union, by choice not necessity); possible under the same roof with separate bedrooms and households (s 95A(7)).
> - *Leong Kwek Keong*: living apart is more than physical separation; requires intention to terminate consortium (husband slept elsewhere but spent his free time with the family and had regular relations — not living apart).
> - *Tan Lee Tiang*: same roof — must show separate households (parties who ate and lived together and jointly cared for the children ran one household).
> - s 95A(5): living apart is still continuous even if the parties lived together for a total of 6 months or less, but that time does not count towards the 3 or 4 years.

**P** Separation is the no-fault, amicable route (FL3 s21), useful where the respondent denies fault but accepts the marriage is over.

**A** First, date the separation: [Client moved out of the master bedroom in June 2022]. Second, assess whether there were separate households: [separate meals, finances, no conjugal relations, no joint outings] — or [they still ate together and cared jointly for the child, as in Tan Lee Tiang — one household]. Third, compute the period to filing: [3 years from June 2025 with consent; 4 years from June 2026 without]. Fourth, if the period is not met, consider mutual agreement (1-16). **C** Therefore [Client] [can rely on 3 years' separation with consent from [date] / should rely on another fact].

*Source note: FL3 s21–22 (s 95A(1)(d)–(e), (7); Leong Kwek Keong; Tan Lee Tiang); WC-Text s 95A(5); Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Facts 4–5; PYP23-FAM Q4.1, PYP25 Q3.2.*

### pcp-w-1-16 — Divorce by mutual agreement (s 95A(1)(f), (6) WC)
*Keywords: divorce by mutual agreement, DMA, s 95A(1)(f), s 95A(6), written agreement, reasons for breakdown, efforts to reconcile, arrangements for finances and children, reconciliation possibility, consents to divorce but denies adultery, no-fault*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] and [Spouse] can divorce by mutual agreement. **C** Yes: since both accept the marriage has broken down, they may rely on s 95A(1)(f) WC by signing a written agreement setting out the reasons, their efforts at reconciliation and the arrangements for their finances and children (s 95A(6)), which avoids proving [Spouse]'s adultery.
**R** Irretrievable breakdown may be proved by the parties' agreement that the marriage has irretrievably broken down (s 95A(1)(f) WC). The agreement must be in writing and state the reasons leading the parties to that conclusion, the efforts made to reconcile, and the consideration given to arrangements for their financial affairs and any child (s 95A(6)). The court will consider the possibility of reconciliation.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 95A(6): an agreement in writing stating
>   - the reasons leading the parties to conclude that their marriage has irretrievably broken down;
>   - the efforts the parties have made to reconcile;
>   - the consideration the parties have given to arrangements for their financial affairs and any child of the marriage.
> - The court considers the possibility of reconciliation (FL3 s23; s 139F).

**P** In the 2026 sample, where the husband would consent to divorce but denied adultery, the guide answer pointed to mutual agreement (and behaviour) as alternatives (SQ guide p127).

**A** First, confirm both accept breakdown: [Colin will consent to the divorce though he denies adultery]. Second, draft the agreement: [reasons — the parties have grown apart and the marriage had broken down before the affair; reconciliation — counselling in 2023; arrangements — joint custody, care and control to Client, maintenance and division to be agreed or decided]. Third, explain that this avoids a contested adultery trial and suits the TJ approach, and that the fact relied on does not affect ancillary matters (1-12). **C** Therefore the parties should proceed by mutual agreement under s 95A(1)(f).

*Source note: FL3 s15, s23 (s 95A(1)(f), (6)); Cyril-Exam §3.3.1 Fact 6; SQ guide p127; PYP25 Q6 (divorce by mutual agreement).*

## 1.V Children

- **Who counts as a child of the marriage? What is the test?**
  - Children of both, adopted, stepchildren accepted into the family, under 21 (ss 92, 122); welfare paramount (s 125; s 3 GIA; *Tan Siew Kee*) → [1-17](#pcp-w-1-17)
  - Husband's child by another woman whom wife raised — child of the marriage? → [1-17](#pcp-w-1-17)
- **Client wants sole custody**
  - Custody = major decisions (education, religion, health); joint or no order is the norm; sole only exceptionally (abuse, harmful non-cooperation) (*CX v CY*) → [1-18](#pcp-w-1-18)
- **Who gets care and control? Father wants to split the twins / shared care**
  - Welfare: continuity and status quo (*Wong Phila Mae*), young child and mother (*Soon Peck Wah*), siblings together; shared needs cooperation (*AQL v AQM*); split rare (*AFK v AFM*) → [1-19](#pcp-w-1-19)
  - Father took the child by force; child now resists the mother → status quo created unilaterally carries less weight → [1-19](#pcp-w-1-19)
- **Access: client wants to cut the other parent off**
  - Joint parenthood; no access only in extreme cases (*BG v BF*); supervised access exceptionally (*APE v APF*); conditions → [1-20](#pcp-w-1-20)
  - Client fears for the child / child has not seen the father for years → supervised only on evidence, not suspicion (*BKJ v BKK*); convincing evidence to deny access (*Tay Ah Hoe*; *BHL v BHM*) → [1-20](#pcp-w-1-20)
- **Reports, the child's wishes, interviewing the child, psychologist** → [1-21](#pcp-w-1-21)
  - Other parent wants the child assessed by a psychologist → leave needed; may not be in the child's interests (*JAC v JAD*) → [1-21](#pcp-w-1-21)
- **Taking the child overseas / relocating home with the children**
  - Holiday under a month vs relocation; written consent or leave; offence; injunction (s 126(3)–(5), s 131) → [1-22](#pcp-w-1-22)
  - Leave to relocate: welfare; no presumption; caregiver's wish vs left-behind parent's bond (*BNS v BNT*; *UYK v UYJ*) → [1-22](#pcp-w-1-22)
- **Child taken to or kept in Singapore from abroad (Hague)**
  - Wrongful removal or retention (Art 3); return forthwith within a year, later unless settled (Art 12) → [1-23](#pcp-w-1-23)
  - Defences: not exercising custody, consent or acquiescence; grave risk of harm or intolerable situation; mature child objects (Art 13); human rights (Art 20) → [1-23](#pcp-w-1-23)
  - Mother alleges violence; high burden; return on undertakings (*BDU v BDT*) → [1-23](#pcp-w-1-23)

### pcp-w-1-17 — Child of the marriage and the welfare principle (ss 92, 122, 125 WC; s 3 GIA)
*Keywords: child of the marriage, s 92 WC, s 122 WC, below 21, adopted child, stepchild, accepted as member of family, illegitimate child, welfare of the child, paramount consideration, s 125 WC, s 3 GIA, wishes of the parents, wishes of the child, Tan Siew Kee v Chua Ah Boey, welfare widest sense*

**I** The issue is how the court will decide arrangements for [the children], and whether [Raymond] is a child of the marriage. **C** The court will make orders on custody, care and control and access with the children's welfare as the paramount consideration (s 125(2) WC), giving weight to the parents' and children's wishes subject to welfare; [Raymond, Husband's son by another woman whom Wife accepted and raised as part of the family, is likely a child of the marriage under s 92].
**R** On granting divorce, nullity or judicial separation, the court may make orders on custody, care and control and access for any child of the marriage below 21 (ss 122, 124–126 WC). A child of the marriage includes any child of both spouses, an adopted child, and any other child who was a member of their family when they ceased to live together (s 92). The welfare of the child is the paramount consideration; subject to it, the court has regard to the wishes of the parents and of the child where of an age to express an independent opinion (s 125(2); s 3 GIA). "Welfare" is taken in its widest sense (*Tan Siew Kee v Chua Ah Boey* [1987] SGHC 52 at [12]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 125(1): the court may place a child in the custody or care and control of the father or mother or, in exceptional circumstances, another relative, child welfare organisation or suitable person.
> - s 125(2): paramount consideration is the welfare of the child; subject to this, regard to (a) the wishes of the parents; (b) the wishes of the child, where of an age to express an independent opinion.
> - s 3 GIA: welfare of the infant is the first and paramount consideration; neither parent has a superior right.
> - Welfare is "the golden thread" (*BNS v BNT* [2015] SGCA 23).
> - *Tan Siew Kee* [12]: welfare in its widest sense — general well-being and all aspects of upbringing (religious, moral, physical), happiness, comfort and security; a loving parent and a stable home; not measured in money (also *Lim Chin Huat Francis v Lim Kok Chye Ivan* [1999] 2 SLR(R) 392 at [86]).
> - "Child of the marriage" (s 92, per Cyril-Exam §4.1 and FL-CS1 s12): includes children of void marriages, adopted children, and any other child, whether or not a child of either spouse, who was a member of the family of the husband and wife when they ceased to live together or immediately before proceedings, whichever first occurred; parties to a void marriage are deemed husband and wife (s 92, WC-Text).

**P** The parties' wishes are considered but are subordinate to welfare: PCR r 15A requires counsel to advise that the court decides on the child's welfare and best interests, not the wishes of parent or child (FL8 s24).

**A** First, identify the children: [twins aged 12; Raymond, 16, Husband's son by an affair, raised by Wife since infancy and close to the twins] — Raymond was a member of the family when they ceased to live together, so he is likely a child of the marriage. Second, set the test: welfare is paramount; the parents' wishes [Wife wants sole care; Husband wants Raymond] and the children's wishes [Raymond at 16 can express an independent view] are relevant but subordinate. **C** Therefore the court will decide [the children's arrangements] on their welfare, including [Raymond].

*Source note: FL-SYL [16]–[19]; FL4 s2, s4, s16–19 (s 125; s 3 GIA; BNS v BNT; Tan Siew Kee [12]; Lim Chin Huat Francis [86]); FL-CS1 s12 (s 122 read with s 92); Cyril-Exam §4.1 (s 92 categories — secondary); PYP22-FAM (Raymond).*

### pcp-w-1-18 — Custody: joint, sole or no order (s 126 WC; CX v CY)
*Keywords: custody, sole custody, joint custody, no order as to custody, s 126 WC, upbringing and education, major decisions, CX v CY, ZO v ZP, acrimony, uncooperative parent, exclude the other parent, abuse, exceptional circumstances, s 46 WC parental responsibility, APE v APF, sole custody threshold high, father overseas, estranged parent, enrichment classes*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can obtain sole custody of [the children]. **C** It is unlikely: custody concerns major decisions on the child's upbringing, and the court ordinarily makes a joint custody (or no) order; sole custody is reserved for exceptional cases such as abuse, which [are not present here] (*CX v CY* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 690).
**R** A custody order entitles the person given custody to decide all questions relating to the child's upbringing and education (s 126 WC) — major, long-term decisions. It is in the child's interest to have both parents in his life; a joint custody order is appropriate where a parent is uncooperative and inclined to exclude the other, no order where there is no dispute on serious matters, and sole custody only in exceptional circumstances, such as physical, sexual or emotional abuse; acrimony alone does not justify sole custody (*CX v CY*, affirmed in *ZO v ZP* [2011] SGCA 25).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 126: custody order may be on conditions; entitles the custodian to decide all questions on the child's upbringing and education.
> - *CX v CY*:
>   - s 46(b) WC parental responsibility; the child's interest is to have both parents in his life;
>   - joint and no custody orders are similar in effect; no order where there is no actual dispute on serious matters;
>   - joint custody where one parent is uncooperative and inclined to exclude the other, as a reminder to consult;
>   - the CA rejected making sole custody orders merely because parties are acrimonious; sole custody is rare, only in exceptional circumstances (e.g. physical, sexual or emotional abuse of the child); per Cyril, also where cooperation is impossible even after mediation and counselling and the non-cooperation harms the child ([38]).
> - Affirmed in *ZO v ZP*.
> - Non-core: *APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17 (FL-SYL [27]; affirmed [2015] SGCA 47):
>   - "the threshold for granting a sole custody order is a high one" ([13]);
>   - the father had spent long periods overseas and the child was estranged from him, but he had stayed involved in major decisions (schooling, enrichment classes), his decisions were sound and he was not abusive ([13]–[19]);
>   - the mother did not show an exceptional case, so joint custody was ordered ([20]).

**P** In *CX v CY* the mother had tried to exclude the father from the child's life by denying access, and the Court of Appeal made a joint custody order to signal that she must cooperate (Cyril-Exam §4.2). Likewise, in *APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17 at [13]–[20] (non-core), the High Court held that the threshold for sole custody is a high one and ordered joint custody although the father had spent long periods overseas, because he had stayed involved in the major decisions on the child's schooling and enrichment, his decisions were sound and he had not been abusive.

**A** First, explain the distinction to [Client]: custody is about major decisions [schooling, religion, major medical treatment]; day-to-day care is care and control (1-19); losing sole custody does not mean losing the children. Second, apply *CX v CY*: [Husband's infidelity and the parties' bitterness] are not exceptional circumstances; there is no allegation that he abused the children; [if anything, Client's wish to cut him off suggests joint custody is needed as a reminder to consult]. [If he forcibly took the child and manipulates him against Client, that is serious but usually addressed through care and control and access conditions rather than sole custody.] [If Client relies on Husband's long absences abroad or the child's estrangement from him: absence alone is not exceptional where he still takes part in major decisions (*APE v APF* [13]–[20]).] **C** Therefore the court is likely to order joint custody [or no order], not sole custody.

*Source note: FL-SYL [20]–[22], [27] (non-core); FL4 s5–6 (s 126; CX v CY; ZO v ZP); Cyril-Exam §4.2 (CX v CY at [18]–[19], [26], [32], [35]–[38], [42] — paragraph numbers from Cyril, secondary); eLit (APE v APF [2015] SGHC 17 at [13]–[20]); PYP23-FAM Q5.1, PYP25 Q6.*

### pcp-w-1-19 — Care and control: sole, shared or split (s 126(2A)–(2B) WC)
*Keywords: care and control, sole care and control, shared care and control, split care and control, day-to-day care, primary caregiver, Soon Peck Wah v Woon Che Chye, young child, maternal bond, Wong Phila Mae v Shaw Harold, continuity, status quo, ABW v ABV, unilateral status quo, siblings together, Kim Chun Ahe, AQL v AQM, TAT v TAU, AFK v AFM, twins, split the children, took the child by force, manipulation*

**I** The issue is to whom the court will give care and control of [the children], and whether [Husband]'s proposal to [split them / share care] will be accepted. **C** The court is likely to give [Client] sole care and control of both children, with liberal access to [Husband], because she has been their primary caregiver, keeping the siblings together promotes stability, and [shared care is impractical given the parties' conflict and Husband's travel].
**R** An order for care and control gives a parent authority over day-to-day matters and the child resides with that parent; it may contain conditions, including where the child is to reside and a prohibition on taking the child out of Singapore (s 126(2A)–(2B) WC). Orders may be sole, shared or (uncommonly) split. Relevant welfare factors include continuity of care and the status quo (*Wong Phila Mae v Shaw Harold* [1991] 1 SLR(R) 680; *ABW v ABV* [2014] SGHC 29), contrasted with a status quo created by a unilateral act; a preference, all things being equal, for the mother of a young child (*Soon Peck Wah v Woon Che Chye* [1997] 3 SLR(R) 430); and keeping siblings together (*Kim Chun Ahe v Ng Siew Kee* [2002] SGDC 276).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 126(2A): care and control may be subject to conditions; (2B): may (a) set where the child resides; (b)–(c) provide for visits and access by the other parent or family; (d) prohibit taking the child out of Singapore.
> - Types (FL4 s8):
>   - sole care and control to one parent (plus access to the other);
>   - shared care and control (roughly equal time; two homes) — *TAT v TAU* [2018] SGHCF 11; *AQL v AQM* [2011] SGHC 264 (non-core); generally where parents can cooperate;
>   - split care and control (children divided) — uncommon — *AFK v AFM* [2010] SGDC 32 (non-core; large age gap).
> - Welfare factors (FL4 s19):
>   - all things equal, mother preferred for a young child (*Soon Peck Wah*);
>   - preserve the status quo and continuity (*ABW v ABV*; *Wong Phila Mae*), but not a status quo created by a unilateral act;
>   - siblings should not be separated (*Kim Chun Ahe*).
> - Cyril-Exam §4.3: *Wong Phila Mae* — children settled in schools; disruption causes emotional stress; *Soon Peck Wah* — child of 4; maternal bond; older children's views become relevant.

**P** In *AFK v AFM* a split order was made because of a large age gap: the teenage elder children had their own lives and the youngest had a settled routine with the mother (Cyril-Exam §4.3) — a reason absent where the children are twins or close in age.

**A** First, identify the primary caregiver and the status quo: [Client has cared for both children; Husband took Joseph by force five months ago, travels often and leaves him with neighbours] — the current arrangement for Joseph is a status quo created unilaterally and carries little weight. Second, siblings: [Joseph (7) and Joy (5) / the 4-year-old twins] should stay together; there is no age gap or special reason for a split. Third, young children: [Soon Peck Wah] supports the mother where all else is equal. Fourth, shared care: requires cooperation and practicality; [the parties' hostility and Husband's travel] make it unsuitable. Fifth, [Joseph's resistance to Client] should be explored through a welfare report (1-21) and addressed by access arrangements, not by rewarding the removal. **C** Therefore [Client] is likely to obtain sole care and control of both children, with liberal access to [Husband].

*Source note: FL-SYL [23]–[29] ([27]–[29] non-core); FL4 s7–8, s19–20 (s 126(2A)–(2B); TAT v TAU; AQL v AQM; AFK v AFM; Soon Peck Wah; ABW v ABV; Wong Phila Mae; Kim Chun Ahe); Cyril-Exam §4.3; PYP23-FAM Q5.2, PYP25 Q6, PYP21-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-20 — Access: types, supervised access and conditions
*Keywords: access, reasonable access, liberal access, scheduled access, overnight access, holiday access, overseas access, video calls, supervised access, unsupervised access, APE v APF, DSSA, supervised exchange, family service, BG v BF, joint parenthood, no access extreme, Tay Ah Hoe v Kwek Lye Seng, convincing evidence, incapable of caring for the child, BHL v BHM, BKJ v BKK, risk of abuse or danger, mere suspicion, unsubstantiated fear, TIX v TIY, priority of access, estranged, repair the relationship, step up access, handover at police post, conditions on access, Koh Cher Hau, Jaya Latchmee, cut off contact*

**I** The issue is what access [Husband] will have to [the children], given that [Client] wants to cut off contact [or wants his access supervised]. **C** The court will almost certainly grant [Husband] access, because joint parenthood applies to access and only extreme circumstances, shown by convincing evidence, justify none (*BG v BF* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 233; *BHL v BHM* [2013] SGHC 92 at [12]); access will be unsupervised unless [Client] proves an exceptional circumstance such as a real risk of harm or an estrangement needing repair (*APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17 at [32]).
**R** The parent without care and control is given access so that he can maintain regular contact with the child, and the principle of joint parenthood applies equally to access; it requires extreme circumstances before a parent is allowed no access (*BG v BF*), and convincing evidence that the parent is incapable of caring for the child before reasonable access is denied (*BHL v BHM* at [12], citing *Tay Ah Hoe v Kwek Lye Seng* [1996] SGHC 120, where access was denied or supervised only for the child's safety, at [5]). Unsupervised access is the starting point; supervised access is ordered only in exceptional circumstances — serious welfare concerns, an estrangement needing serious repair, or factors making unsupervised access difficult to implement (*APE v APF* at [30], [32]) — and a mere unsubstantiated fear or suspicion of abuse is not enough (*BKJ v BKK* [2013] SGDC 261). Conditions may be attached (s 126(2B) WC).

> **Rule in full**
> - Access is the opportunity for the non-resident parent to spend time with the child and the child's right to maintain the relationship (FL4 s10): reasonable or liberal; scheduled or specified; weekday, weekend, overnight; holidays and special occasions; overseas access (itinerary, passports); technology (video calls, messages).
> - *BG v BF*: purpose is regular contact; joint parenthood applies to access; the child should interact with both parents; no access only in extreme circumstances.
> - Supervised access (*APE v APF* at [32]), exceptional; unsupervised access is awarded unless:
>   - serious welfare concerns (violence, inappropriate parenting if the child is left with that parent);
>   - the child is estranged from the parent and the relationship needs serious repair (e.g. the child is fearful of meeting the parent);
>   - factors make unsupervised access difficult to implement (e.g. handovers so acrimonious that unsupervised access cannot work without detriment to the child);
>   - a third party is present (DSSA supervised exchange or visitation; possibly the care-and-control parent); reports and access reviews may be directed; supervision may be lifted once it is no longer needed (Cyril-Exam §4.4.1).
> - Conditions (FL4 s12): e.g. no third party (a girlfriend) on overseas holidays (*Koh Cher Hau v Seah Poh Suan*, DCA 5032/1998); no visits at school outside access hours (*Jaya Latchmee v Vijaya Kumaran*, DP 1246/1999).
> - Non-core cases (FL-SYL [31]–[34]):
>   - *Tay Ah Hoe v Kwek Lye Seng* [1996] SGHC 120: welfare is paramount but the parents' rights and expectations "must be considered as well"; with no evidence that access would harm the child it was granted; where a parent abuses the child, access may be denied or permitted "only ... under supervision or other conditions for the safety of the child" ([5]); the custodial parent "has the duty to encourage that child to meet, know and love the other parent" and children "should never be used as pawns" ([6]); unsupervised access on Sundays 5pm–9.30pm, with handover at the Ang Mo Kio police post ([7]). (eLitigation numbers the two heading lines as [1]–[2]; the pinpoints are eLitigation's.) The "convincing evidence" wording is *BHL v BHM*'s summary of this case at [12].
>   - *BHL v BHM* [2013] SGHC 92: the court "should promote joint parental responsibility" ([11]); "This principle of joint parenthood is equally applicable to access orders"; denying reasonable access requires "convincing evidence that the parent is incapable of caring for the child" ([12]); the wife's allegations did not justify refusing the husband overnight access ([13]–[17]).
>   - *BKJ v BKK* [2013] SGDC 261: supervised access "will normally only be necessary if there is a risk of abuse or some other kind of danger to the child"; a mere unsubstantiated fear or suspicion does not justify it. The judgment is not publicly available (not on eLitigation; paywalled elsewhere), so cite it through *APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17 at [21], which quotes it at [23]; Cyril-Exam §4.4.1 gives [16]. Best-supported pinpoint: [23], as quoted in *APE v APF* [21].
>   - *TIX v TIY* [2015] SGFC 146: supervised access is ordered only if supervision is necessary (Nic-Fam §4.3.3.1.1; Shang; Timo — secondary). The judgment is not publicly available, so there is no pinpoint; FL-SYL [34] also lists "priority of access", which no source explains — in an answer, cite *TIX v TIY* only for the necessity point and rely on *APE v APF* [32] for the circumstances.
>   - *APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17: the child had not seen the father since 2012, so access was supervised initially, through a family service, which was "more suitable presently" ([25], [37]).

**P** In *APE v APF* [2015] SGHC 17 at [25], [32], [37], the child had not seen the father since 2012; rather than deny access, the court ordered supervised access at first, through a family service, so that the relationship could be repaired (Cyril-Exam §4.4.1). In *BHL v BHM* at [12]–[17] the High Court applied the joint-parenthood principle to hold that the wife's allegations did not justify refusing the husband overnight access.

**A** First, explain to [Client] that access is the children's right as much as [Husband]'s, and that his infidelity is not a ground to deny it. Second, assess the evidence of risk: [no violence towards the children] → unsupervised, liberal access (weekday, weekend, overnight, holidays); [a history of violence or drunkenness, supported by police reports, medical reports or a protection order] → supervised or specified access with review; [only Client's suspicion] → not enough (*BKJ v BKK*). Third, estrangement: [the children have not seen Husband for two years / are afraid of him] → supervised access at a DSSA, then stepped up, rather than no access (*APE v APF*). Fourth, implementation: [handovers end in shouting matches] → supervised exchange or handover at a neutral venue. Fifth, propose conditions to address genuine concerns: [no alcohol during access; no new partner present; no visits at school]. Sixth, tell [Client] that supervision is a stage, not an end: the order can be reviewed once [Husband stays sober / the relationship is repaired]. **C** Therefore [Husband] will likely have [liberal / initially supervised] access, and [Client] should focus on evidence-based conditions rather than denying access.

*Source note: FL-SYL [30]–[34] ([31]–[34] non-core); FL4 s9–13 (types; APE v APF; conditions cases; BG v BF); Cyril-Exam §4.4 (non-core cases summarised); Nic-Fam §4.3.3 and the peer muggers in Nic's `3. Peer Muggers` (Shang, Cel, Timo: Tay Ah Hoe, TIX v TIY, BKJ v BKK — secondary); eLit (BHL v BHM [11]–[17]; APE v APF [13]–[37], quoting BKJ v BKK [23]; Tay Ah Hoe [5]–[7]); PYP23-FAM Q5.2, PYP21-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-21 — Evidence in children's cases: welfare reports, the child's views, judicial interviews
*Keywords: evidence in children's cases, s 130 WC, welfare officer, child welfare reports, custody evaluation report, access evaluation report, CAPS, Child Representative, parenting coordinator, WKM v WKN, judicial interview, ZO v ZP, AZB v AZC, child's wishes, expert evidence, psychological assessment, child psychologist, mental health professional, JAC v JAD, Rule 35 FJR, P 10 r 4 FJ(G)R, leave of court, parental alienation*

**I** The issue is how the court will find out what is best for [the child], including [his wishes and the allegation of manipulation]. **C** The court must have regard to the advice of a person trained in child welfare (s 130 WC) and may call for a custody or access evaluation report and interview [the child] if mature enough (*ZO v ZP* [2011] SGCA 25); a party may not have [the child] psychologically assessed without the court's permission.
**R** In proceedings concerning a child the court must have regard to the advice of a welfare officer or person trained in child welfare, though it is not bound by it (s 130 WC; *WKM v WKN* [2024] SGCA 1 at [67]). Evidence includes the parties', third parties', the child's, expert evidence and welfare reports. The court may ascertain the child's wishes through a judicial interview: children should have a "voice", not necessarily a "choice"; the discretion to interview is exercised for the child's welfare, judges are not compelled to interview, and they must be aware of the limitations and give appropriate weight to the views expressed (*ZO v ZP*; *AZB v AZC* [2016] SGHCF 1 at [11]–[12], [20]–[21], [24]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Evidence (FL4 s21): (1) parties; (2) third parties; (3) the child; (4) expert evidence (r 35 FJR 2014); (5) child welfare reports (s 130 WC; *WKM v WKN* [67]):
>   - Custody Evaluation, Access Evaluation and Specific Issue Reports by FJC's CAPS;
>   - Child Protection Social Reports;
>   - Supervised Visitation or Exchange Reports by Family Service Centres;
>   - Child Representative reports;
>   - Parenting Coordinator reports;
>   - court expert reports;
>   (6) judicial interview.
> - *ZO v ZP*: court's approach to judicial interviews (non-core per FL-SYL [39]); child must be mature enough to convey independent views.
> - *AZB v AZC* [2016] SGHCF 1 (FL-SYL [40]):
>   - Art 12 UN Convention on the Rights of the Child: children should have a "voice", not necessarily a "choice" ([11]–[12]);
>   - concerns: judges are untrained, children may be intimidated or coached, confidentiality is hard ([15]); but abandoning interviews "is to throw the baby out with the bathwater" (citing *ZO v ZP* at [16]) ([19]); judges must "be aware of the limitations and give the appropriate weight to the views expressed" ([20]);
>   - the discretion to interview is "exercised for the children's welfare"; a judge may interview on her own motion; s 125(2)(b) WC ([21]); in the judge-led system an interview need not draw the child into the fray ([22]–[23]);
>   - "judges are not compelled to interview children": alternatives are a Child Representative, assessors (ss 27–28 Family Justice Act 2014) and social welfare, custody evaluation and access evaluation reports ([24]);
>   - process: open-ended, not leading, questions; do not make the child choose between parents; consider age, maturity, whether the child wants to speak, any pressure from a parent, loyalty conflicts and alienation ([25]);
>   - outcome: the children wished to speak and were mature enough ([26]); their views corroborated a material change in circumstances, care and control stayed with the father, and the mother received overnight alternate-weekend access with a review in six months ([35]–[40]).
> - Psychological assessment (non-core; FL-SYL [38]):
>   - *JAC v JAD* [2014] SGDC 379: subjecting a child to psychological assessment may not be in the best interests of the child (FL-SYL [38]). The judgment is not publicly available, so cite it for that proposition only, from the syllabus, with no pinpoint;
>   - a party must not, without the court's permission, cause a child to be examined or assessed by a medical practitioner, psychologist, counsellor, social worker or mental health professional to prepare expert evidence, and evidence from an unauthorised examination needs the court's permission (r 35(1), (4) FJR 2014, quoted in *XZE v XZF* [2026] SGFC 45 at [8]; still applies to divorces filed before 15 Oct 2024); for later cases the application is under P 10 r 4 FJ(G)R 2024, in Form 90 with the Expert Witness Template (Form 93) and a draft letter of instruction (Form 91), and the court may appoint an expert from the IMH panel (FJC PD 2024 para 95(1)–(3));
>   - the court's own welfare reports (CAPS custody or access evaluation; s 130) are the usual route to professional views of the child.
> - FJC Practice Directions 2024 on children's issues (FL-SYL [37], non-core): solicitors tell the court at the hearing whether there are relevant children and whether welfare arrangements are made (s 123 WC) (para 10); permission to proceed without the parenting programme needs an affidavit giving reasons (s 94A(4); para 11); urgent child-welfare issues at a case conference need 48 hours' notice (para 80(4)); mandatory counselling and mediation where there is a child under 21, attendance compulsory unless excused, e.g. for family violence (paras 81(3), 82); court-ordered private mediation only where no child issues are contested (para 84); the counsellor may involve the child if in the child's interest (para 85(4)); enforcement of access orders by compliance bond for breaches from 2 Jan 2025 (paras 46A–46H); Child Representative appointed from a panel, must act expeditiously and inform Child Protection Services of suspected abuse (para 98); Parenting Coordination Programme (para 99); expert examination of a child (para 95); in the Therapeutic Justice model, children's welfare is prioritised, child issues may be heard first and an early child report obtained (paras 90B(2)(d), 90F(6)–(7)); urgent applications to stop a child leaving Singapore (para 105).

**P** The child's wishes are relevant under s 125(2)(b) only where he is of an age to express an independent opinion, and the weight given depends on maturity and whether the views are his own (FL-SYL [19], [22]).

**A** First, propose a Custody Evaluation Report by CAPS to assess [both homes, Joseph's resistance and whether it reflects Husband's influence]. Second, if [Joseph, 7] is mature enough and wishes to speak, suggest a judicial interview rather than having the parties report his views, explaining to [Client] that the court listens to the child's voice but he is not choosing (*AZB v AZC* [11]–[12], [26]); [if he is young or under pressure from a parent] rely on the reports and a Child Representative instead ([24]–[25]). Third, advise [Client] not to arrange a private psychologist's assessment of the child without the court's leave; if [Husband] asks for one, oppose it as not in the child's best interests (*JAC v JAD*, non-core), especially where [a CAPS report is already being prepared / the child has been interviewed / the child is already anxious about the dispute], and point to the s 130 report as the proper route. **C** Therefore [Client] should ask the court to call for a welfare report [and consider a judicial interview].

*Source note: FL-SYL [35]–[40] ([36]–[39] non-core); PD24 paras 10–11, 46A–46H, 80–85, 90B–90F, 95, 98–99, 105; eLit (AZB v AZC [11]–[40]; XZE v XZF [2026] SGFC 45 [8]); FL4 s21 (s 130; WKM v WKN [67]; report types; r 35 FJR 2014); Cyril-Exam §4.5 (r 35 leave — secondary).*

### pcp-w-1-22 — Taking a child out of Singapore and relocation (s 126(3)–(5), s 131 WC; BNS v BNT) (non-core)
*Keywords: relocation, relocate overseas, move back home with the children, take child out of Singapore, s 126(3) WC, written consent of both parents, leave of court, holiday less than one month, s 126(4), s 126(5) offence, s 131 injunction, BNS v BNT, welfare overrides any other consideration, no presumption, primary caregiver's reasonable wishes, transfer insecurity to the child, left-behind parent, stronger the bond the larger the void, poorly conceived plan, Canada, UYK v UYJ, UKY v UYJ, trailing spouse, no presumption for or against relocation, attachment, developmental needs, AZB v AYZ, AYD v AYE, TAA v TAB, sole custody still needs consent, Spain, AZB v AYZ, AYD v AYE, US, reasonable and in good faith, return to Hong Kong, fact-centric*

**I** The issue is whether [Client] can take [the children] to live in [Hong Kong] after the divorce. **C** Once a custody or care and control order is in force, [Client] may not take the children out of Singapore (except for less than one month) without [Husband]'s written consent or the court's leave (s 126(3)–(4) WC); on an application for leave the children's welfare is paramount, there is no presumption in favour of the primary caregiver's wish to relocate, and the loss of [Husband]'s relationship with them weighs heavily (*BNS v BNT* [2015] SGCA 23 at [19]–[26]), so [her prospects depend on the strength of her plan and of the children's bond with Husband].
**R** While an order for custody or care and control is in force, no one may take the child out of Singapore except with the written consent of both parents or the leave of the court, save that the person with custody or care and control (or a person with that person's written consent) may do so for less than one month (s 126(3)–(4) WC); contravention is an offence (s 126(5)), and the court may restrain removal by injunction (s 131). On relocation, the welfare of the child is paramount and "ought to override any other consideration" (*BNS v BNT* at [19]); there is no legal presumption in favour of relocation ([22]); the primary caregiver's reasonable wish to relocate is an important but not a singularly determinative factor, relevant chiefly where refusing it would transfer the caregiver's unhappiness and insecurity to the child ([20]–[21]); and the loss of the child's relationship with the left-behind parent is an important consideration, not determinative — "the stronger the bond, the larger the resultant void" ([25]–[26]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 126(3): where a custody or care and control order is in force, no person may take the child out of Singapore except with the written consent of both parents or the leave of the court.
> - s 126(4): (3) does not prevent the person with custody or care and control, or a person with that person's written consent, taking the child out of Singapore for less than one month (a holiday, not a relocation).
> - s 126(5): contravention is an offence — fine up to $5,000, imprisonment up to 12 months, or both.
> - s 131: court may grant an injunction restraining removal of the child from Singapore.
> - s 126(3) applies even to a parent with sole custody and care and control: in *TAA v TAB* [2015] SGHCF 1 the father, who had sole custody, care and control, left for Spain with two of the children after leave was refused, which gave "rise to a situation of breach of s 126(3)" ([2]–[3], [31]–[32]); no presumption in favour of relocation; parents must "place the needs of their children before their own" ([9]–[11], [17]–[19]); relocation refused ([34]).
> - *BNS v BNT* [2015] SGCA 23 (CA):
>   - welfare paramount, overriding any other consideration ([19]);
>   - no legal presumption in favour of relocation on the primary caregiver's reasonable wishes ([22]);
>   - the caregiver's wish is important but "not a singularly determinative factor"; it matters where refusal would transfer the caregiver's insecurity to the child ([20]–[21]);
>   - loss of the relationship with the left-behind parent is important but not determinative; the stronger the bond, the larger the void ([25]–[26]);
>   - intensely fact-centric (FL4 s14).
> - *UYK v UYJ* [2020] SGHCF 9 (FL-SYL [83] lists it as "UKY v UYJ"):
>   - welfare is the paramount consideration ([25]); "There is no presumption for or against relocation" ([37]);
>   - factors include the child's age, attachment to each parent and developmental needs ([37]); the position of the "trailing spouse" who moved for the family ([33]–[34]); relocation cases are very fact-specific ([34], [38]);
>   - British parents moved to Singapore in 2018 under a joint letter of intention, which is evidence of their intentions though not binding ([16]–[19], [48]–[50]); the mother was the main caregiver ([23]); how settled the child is depends on years spent here, age and family ties ([51]–[54]); no sufficient evidence of parental alienation ([59]); loss of the relationship with the father is "an important factor", mitigated by good physical and virtual access, and more can be expected of the father, who can travel ([63]–[66]); necessity of the move is "not a requirement but is a factor" ([69]); appeal dismissed: relocation to the UK upheld, care and control to the mother ([78], [84]).
> - Further cases (FL-SYL [83], non-core):
>   - *AZB v AYZ* [2012] SGHC 108: test from *Re C* — was the primary caregiver's wish to relocate reasonable and in good faith ([12]); the child's welfare is "inextricably intertwined" with the caregiver's well-being ([14]); considerable weight to a reasonable, good-faith application, and in most cases it is likely to be granted ([20]) — but note that *BNS v BNT* later rejected any presumption; loss of contact with the other parent defeats it only if "so detrimental as to negate the benefits" ([38]–[39]); relocation to the US upheld ([58]–[59]);
>   - *AYD v AYE* [2012] SGHC 42: the mother, primary caregiver with an American fiancé, had "a good reason" to settle in the US; hindrance to the father's access (he lived in India) weighed less than the children's interest in being with their mother ([20]); leave upheld, but the custody order was varied from sole to joint since acrimony alone does not justify sole custody ([23]).
> - Practical: the applicant needs a detailed plan — housing, school, support network, work, and a contact plan for the left-behind parent (overseas and holiday access, video calls, sharing travel costs) (FL4 s10; Cyril-Exam §4.4).

**P** In *BNS v BNT* at [5], [14]–[15], [32], [35] the Court of Appeal refused a mother's application to relocate the children to Canada: the father had a strong, active relationship with them, and the mother's plan was poorly thought through and coloured by her hostility towards him, so the loss to the children outweighed her wish to move.

**A** First, the legal position: [is an order in force? if not yet, one will be made at the ancillary hearing; meanwhile Husband may seek an injunction (s 131)]; a holiday of under one month is allowed (s 126(4)), but a move needs [Husband]'s written consent or leave (s 126(3)). Second, the caregiver's wish: [Client is the primary caregiver; her wish to return to her family in Hong Kong is genuine and reasonable]; would refusal leave her [isolated, depressed] so that her unhappiness affects the children (*BNS v BNT* [20]–[21])? Third, the left-behind parent: [Husband sees the children every weekend and is closely involved] → a strong bond means a large void ([25]–[26]); [he rarely exercises access] → less weight. Fourth, the children: [ages, schooling, friends, attachment to each parent, developmental needs] (*UYK v UYJ* [37]). Fifth, the plan and motives: [school place, housing, job, grandparents' support; proposal for holiday and video access and to share travel costs]; a poorly conceived plan or hostility to the other parent counts against her (*BNS v BNT*). Sixth, history: [the family moved to Singapore only for Husband's job, as a "trailing spouse"] (*UYK v UYJ* [33]–[34]). Seventh, warn that taking the children without consent or leave is an offence (s 126(5)), would count heavily against her, and, if the destination is a Hague Contracting State, may lead to a return order (1-23). **C** Therefore [Client] should seek [Husband]'s written consent or apply for leave, with a detailed plan for continued contact, recognising that relocation is not presumed and that [the children's close bond with Husband] is her main obstacle.

*Source note: FL-SYL [81]–[83] (non-core); FL4 s7, s10, s14 (s 126(2B)(d), (3)–(5); s 131; BNS v BNT); WC-Text s 126(3)–(5); Cyril-Exam §4.4.3; eLit (BNS v BNT [5]–[35]; UYK v UYJ [16]–[84]; TAA v TAB [2]–[34]; AZB v AYZ [12]–[59]; AYD v AYE [3]–[23]); PYP21-FAM (return to Hong Kong).*

### pcp-w-1-23 — International child abduction (ICAA; Hague Convention; BDU v BDT) (non-core)
*Keywords: child abduction, International Child Abduction Act, ICAA, Hague Convention, wrongful removal, wrongful retention, kept child in Singapore after holiday, habitual residence, return order, return forthwith, within one year, settled in new environment, Article 13 defences, consent, acquiescence, grave risk, intolerable situation, child objects, Article 20, burden of proof high, not lightly invoked, undertakings, protective measures, not merits of custody, summary proceedings, BDU v BDT, Germany, TUC v TUD, Contracting State*

**I** The issue is whether [the child], [brought to / kept in] Singapore by [Mother] without [Father]'s consent, must be returned to [country]. **C** If [country] is a Contracting State that has accepted Singapore's accession and [the child] was habitually resident there, the retention is wrongful and a return order will be made unless [Mother] proves one of the limited Article 13 exceptions, which carry a high burden and are not lightly invoked (*BDU v BDT* [2014] SGCA 12 at [38]); even an allegation of family violence will usually be met by ordering return on protective undertakings, as in *BDU v BDT*.
**R** The International Child Abduction Act 2010 implements the Hague Convention on the Civil Aspects of International Child Abduction, which aims to secure the prompt return of children wrongfully removed to or retained in a Contracting State. The primary issue is the child's country of habitual residence at the time of removal or retention; upon proof of wrongful removal or retention in Singapore of a child habitually resident elsewhere, a return order will be made unless one of the limited Article 13 defences applies (*BDU v BDT* [2014] 2 SLR 725; *TUC v TUD* [2017] 4 SLR 877). The court does not decide the substantive merits of custody, which are for the courts of habitual residence (*BDU v BDT* at [26]); the parent invoking an Article 13 exception bears the burden of proof, the standard is high, and Article 13 "will not be lightly invoked" ([38]).

> **Rule in full**
> - ICAA 2010 incorporates the Hague Convention 1980; prompt return of wrongfully removed or retained children between Contracting States.
> - Primary issue: the child's habitual residence immediately before the removal or retention.
> - Wrongful removal or retention proved → return order unless a limited Article 13 defence applies.
> - Applies only between Singapore and Contracting States that have accepted Singapore's accession.
> - Hague Convention text (Schedule to the ICAA):
>   - Art 3: removal or retention is wrongful where (a) it breaches rights of custody (joint or alone) under the law of the State of habitual residence immediately before it, and (b) those rights were actually exercised, or would have been but for the removal or retention; custody rights may arise by law, judicial or administrative decision, or agreement.
>   - Art 12: if proceedings start within one year of the wrongful removal or retention, the court shall order return forthwith; after a year, it shall still order return unless the child is now settled in its new environment.
>   - Art 13 (the person opposing return must establish): (a) the person having care of the child was not actually exercising custody rights at the time, or consented to or later acquiesced in the removal or retention; or (b) there is a grave risk that return would expose the child to physical or psychological harm or otherwise place the child in an intolerable situation. The court may also refuse if the child objects and has attained an age and degree of maturity at which it is appropriate to take account of its views. The court must consider information on the child's social background from the home State's authorities.
>   - Art 20: return may also be refused if not permitted by the requested State's fundamental principles on human rights and fundamental freedoms.
> - *BDU v BDT* [2014] SGCA 12 (CA):
>   - the court is not concerned with the substantive merits of custody ([26]);
>   - the parent relying on Art 13 bears the onus of proof; the standard is high; Art 13 "will not be lightly invoked" ([38]);
>   - Art 13(b) is an "emergency clause" ([42]); the burden is "stringent" ([43]); the exception must not swallow the rule but is given the meaning its words require ([44]); the harm must be "harm to a degree that also amounts to an intolerable situation" ([45]); even if Art 13(b) is made out the court "is not, ipso facto, duty-bound to refuse the return" — a fact-centric discretion ([46]–[47]); the abducting parent cannot rely on a risk of harm of his or her own making, e.g. by refusing available treatment ([49]–[50], [60], [76]); undertakings show good faith, may be a prerequisite to return, are short-lived and must not usurp the home court ([52]–[54]), and should be "carefully tailored" so as to be enforceable by the court of habitual residence, e.g. by being made orders there ([55]–[57], [81]–[82]); the abducting parent should "have his or her day in the court of habitual residence" ([83]); Hague cases are "to be dealt with as expeditiously as possible" and summarily ([88]–[89]);
>   - facts: Singaporean mother, German father, child born in Germany in 2010; the mother kept the child in Singapore ([1]–[8]); her allegations of violence were not established — "it is impossible to decide whether the aggressor was the mother or the father" ([16(a)]); return to Germany ordered subject to detailed undertakings by both parties, including for the mother's treatment ([21]–[23]).
> - FJC does not mediate ICAA applications (from 1 Jul 2020) (FL8 s22).

**P** In *BDU v BDT* [2014] SGCA 12 at [1]–[23], a Singaporean mother kept the couple's German-born child in Singapore and alleged violence by the father; the evidence did not show who the aggressor was, and the Court of Appeal ordered the child's return to Germany on detailed undertakings by both parents, leaving custody to the German courts.

**A** First, habitual residence: [the child lived in Japan with both parents until removal]. Second, wrongful removal or retention: [Mother brought him here (or kept him after a holiday) without Father's consent, in breach of custody rights Father was exercising (Art 3)]. Third, time: [proceedings started within a year — return forthwith (Art 12); later — return unless the child is settled]. Fourth, defences, for [Mother] to prove to a high standard (*BDU v BDT* [38]): [Father consented or acquiesced (Art 13(a))]; [grave risk of physical or psychological harm or an intolerable situation (Art 13(b)) — evidence of violence directed at or affecting the child, not bare allegations]; [the child, mature enough, objects]. Fifth, protective measures: even if risk is shown, propose undertakings tailored to it [Father to provide separate housing and maintenance, not to harass Mother, withdraw any criminal complaint, and the parties to seek interim orders from the home court], on which the court may still order return (*BDU v BDT* [21]–[23]). [If Mother's risk comes from her own condition, e.g. untreated depression, she cannot rely on a risk she can avoid by treatment (*BDU v BDT* [49]–[50], [60]).] Sixth, remind [Mother] that her welfare arguments about where the child should live belong before the home court; the Singapore court decides only return ([26]). **C** Therefore a return order is likely unless [defence], and at most [Mother] can secure protective undertakings for the return.

*Source note: FL-SYL [84] (non-core); FL4 s15 (ICAA; BDU v BDT; TUC v TUD); FL8 s22; SSO-ICAA Schedule, Hague Convention Arts 3, 12, 13, 20 (current as at 10 Nov 2026); eLit (BDU v BDT [1]–[91]).*

## 1.VI Maintenance

- **Maintenance for the children**
  - Both parents' duty (s 68); order on neglect or refusal (s 69(2); *UHA v UHB*); needs vs ability to pay (s 69(4)); apportion by income ratio (*WBU v WBT*); not luxuries (*APE v APF*) → [1-24](#pcp-w-1-24)
  - Child over 21 (NS, university, disability) (s 69(5)); stepchild (s 70) → [1-24](#pcp-w-1-24)
- **Maintenance for the wife (or incapacitated husband)**
  - Still married, no divorce: s 69; divorce filed: s 113 → [1-25](#pcp-w-1-25)
  - Quantum in divorce: s 114 factors; financial preservation; supplementary to division; nominal maintenance not automatic (*ATE v ATD*) → [1-26](#pcp-w-1-26)
  - Wife has her own income (dividends) — maintenance? → [1-26](#pcp-w-1-26)
- **How long does maintenance last? Can it be varied?** (s 117; *Tan Bee Giok*) → [1-27](#pcp-w-1-27)

### pcp-w-1-24 — Maintenance of children (ss 68, 69, 70, 127 WC)
*Keywords: child maintenance, s 68 WC duty of parents, illegitimate child, WX v WW, s 69(2) neglect or refusal, UHA v UHB, reasonable maintenance, s 69(4) factors, needs vs ability to pay, WBU v WBT, apportionment, income ratio, APE v APF, luxurious lifestyle, s 69(5) beyond 21, national service, university, disability, s 69(3) who may apply, s 127 in divorce, s 70 stepchild, EB v EC, TDT v TDS, accepted as member of family, lump sum*

**I** The issue is how much maintenance [Husband] must pay for [the children], and for how long. **C** Both parents must maintain the children (s 68 WC); the court will assess the children's reasonable needs against each parent's ability to pay (s 69(4)) and is likely to apportion the cost broadly by income ratio (*WBU v WBT* [2023] SGHCF 3), so that [Husband] pays about [X]% of [the children's reasonable expenses of $Y].
**R** It is the duty of a parent to maintain or contribute to the maintenance of his or her children, legitimate or not (s 68 WC; *WX v WW* [2009] 3 SLR(R)). The court may, on proof that a parent has neglected or refused to provide reasonable maintenance, order monthly or lump sum payments (s 69(2)); the burden is on the applicant (*UHA v UHB* [2019] SGHCF 12). The primary considerations are the child's needs and the parents' ability to pay among the s 69(4) factors; the equal duty does not mean equal quantum (*WBU v WBT*). In divorce the court orders maintenance under s 127, applying the same principles.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 68: duty to maintain or contribute to the maintenance of children (accommodation, clothing, food, education reasonable to means and station), legitimate or illegitimate (*WX v WW*).
> - s 69(2): on due proof that a parent has neglected or refused to provide reasonable maintenance for a child unable to maintain himself, order monthly sums or a lump sum.
> - *UHA v UHB*: burden on the applicant; reasonable maintenance assessed as a sum on the facts, practically; neglect or refusal requires that the non-paying parent knew or ought to have known of the needs; a less than reasonable mode of provision may be neglect.
> - s 69(4) factors (Cyril-Exam §5.1.1): financial needs of parents and child; income, earning capacity, property and resources; disabilities; age and duration of the marriage; contributions to the family's welfare; standard of living before the neglect; the manner of the child's education or training; conduct, if inequitable to disregard. Primary: needs vs ability to pay.
> - *WBU v WBT*: equal duty ≠ equal quantum; apportionment fact-specific; income ratio is one way; a budget by category helps.
> - *APE v APF* (per Cyril): extravagant items (bird's nest, $1,200 tuition, large allowances) are not reasonable needs.
> - s 69(5): to 21, or beyond for a child with a disability, serving full-time national service, receiving education or training, or other special circumstances.
> - s 69(3) applicants: the child himself if over 21; a guardian or person with actual custody; siblings over 21; a person appointed by the Minister.
> - s 70: a non-parent who voluntarily accepted the child as a member of the family must maintain him if the parents fail to; duty ceases when the child is taken away on the breakdown of the marriage (e.g. interim judgment) (*EB v EC* [2006] 2 SLR(R) 475; *TDT v TDS* [2016] 4 SLR 145).
> - s 127: maintenance in matrimonial proceedings; Parts 8 and 9 (ss 68, 69) apply.

**P** In *WBU v WBT* the High Court apportioned the child's maintenance by the parents' income ratio, while noting that apportionment depends on the facts, including earning capacity, assets and commitments (FL5 s5).

**A** First, list the children's reasonable monthly expenses: [school fees, food, transport, therapy for the younger child's cerebral palsy, helper] — strip out extravagances (*APE v APF*). Second, compare the parents' incomes: [Husband $12,000; Wife nil since she stopped work to care for the child]; apportion by income ratio and earning capacity — [Husband bears most]. Third, duration: [the 18-year-old about to enter NS — maintenance may extend beyond 21 under s 69(5)]; [the disabled child — likely beyond 21]. Fourth, [Raymond, the husband's son raised by Wife] — Husband as parent must maintain him; Wife's s 70 duty ends on the breakdown. **C** Therefore [Husband] should pay about [$X] a month for the children, [extending beyond 21 for Y].

*Source note: FL-SYL [44]–[47]; FL5 s3–8 (ss 68, 69(2)–(5), 70, 127; WX v WW; UHA v UHB; WBU v WBT; EB v EC; TDT v TDS); Cyril-Exam §5.1 (s 69(4) factors; APE v APF; s 69(3) — secondary); PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM. The slide cites *WX v WW* as "[2009] 3 SLR(R)" without a page.*

### pcp-w-1-25 — Maintenance during marriage (s 69 WC) and after divorce is filed (s 113): which applies
*Keywords: maintenance during marriage, s 69 WC, married woman, incapacitated husband, Muslim wife, foreign wife, void marriage, voidable marriage, s 3(2A), s 113 WC, divorce filed, Foo Ah Yan v Chiam Heng Chow, temporary relief, misconduct, no-fault, MSS, interim maintenance*

**I** The issue is under which provision [Client] should claim maintenance for herself. **C** [While the marriage subsists and no divorce is filed, she may claim under s 69 WC on proof that Husband has neglected or refused to maintain her; once divorce proceedings are filed, the claim lies under s 113.]
**R** Section 69 WC provides temporary relief for a wife or incapacitated husband during a subsisting marriage, and is not an ancillary matter; it applies to a "married woman" (including Muslim and foreign wives in Singapore and parties to a voidable marriage until annulled, but not a void one), but not to a Muslim incapacitated husband (s 3(2A)). Once the marriage is terminated or proceedings are brought, the claim is under s 113 for maintenance of a wife or former wife or incapacitated husband. The considerations and goals differ (*Foo Ah Yan v Chiam Heng Chow* [2012] SGCA 15), and the right to maintenance is independent of misconduct, though misconduct may affect the amount.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 69: maintenance of wife / incapacitated husband during marriage; not an ancillary matter.
>   - "married woman" includes Muslim and foreign wives in Singapore;
>   - party to a void marriage is not a married woman; a voidable marriage is valid until annulled;
>   - not available to a Muslim incapacitated husband (s 3(2A));
>   - after termination or annulment, no longer a married woman — claim under s 113.
> - s 69 = temporary relief in a subsisting marriage; s 113 = maintenance of ex-wife or incapacitated ex-husband (*Foo Ah Yan*).
> - Right independent of misconduct (no-fault divorce), though the amount may be affected.
> - Practical (Cyril-Exam §5.2.2): filing under s 69 when divorce is imminent is risky, as divorce ends the s 69 basis; interim maintenance may be sought pending the ancillary hearing (FL-CS1 s7).

**P** In *Foo Ah Yan* the Court of Appeal distinguished the two regimes: the spouse's position during marriage and after its breakdown differ, so the considerations and goals vary (FL5 s11).

**A** First, the status: [no divorce yet; Husband stopped depositing money into the joint account] → s 69 on proof of neglect or refusal. [Divorce already filed] → s 113 (interim maintenance pending ancillary matters). Second, for an incapacitated husband, confirm incapacity [and that he is not Muslim]. **C** Therefore [Client] should claim under [s 69 now / s 113 in the divorce].

*Source note: FL-SYL [41]–[43], [48]–[49]; FL5 s10–11 (s 69; s 3(2A); Foo Ah Yan; misconduct); FL-CS1 s7, s13; Cyril-Exam §5.2.2; PYP22-FAM (husband cut off the joint account).*

### pcp-w-1-26 — Maintenance of a wife or incapacitated husband on divorce (ss 113, 114 WC)
*Keywords: spousal maintenance, wife maintenance, s 113 WC, s 114 WC factors, needs and ability to pay, financial preservation, Quek Lee Tiam v Ho Kim Swee, standard of living before breakdown, exert herself, gainful employment, remarriage, BG v BF even out inequities, Foo Ah Yan, ATE v ATD, supplementary to division, nominal maintenance, $1, general insurer, wife with own income, dividends, standard of living, lump sum*

**I** The issue is whether, and how much, maintenance [Client] can expect from [Husband] on divorce. **C** The court may order maintenance under s 113 WC, assessed mainly on [Client]'s needs and [Husband]'s ability to pay among the s 114 factors; since maintenance supplements the division of assets (*ATE v ATD* [2016] SGCA 2), and [Client] [will receive a substantial share of assets / has her own income], she can expect [modest / substantial / no] maintenance.
**R** The court may order a husband to pay maintenance to his wife or former wife, or a wife to her incapacitated husband, during or after matrimonial proceedings (s 113 WC), having regard to the s 114 factors, of which the needs of the wife and the husband's ability to pay are dominant. The basis is financial preservation, so that the former wife can continue to live, so far as practicable, at the level she enjoyed immediately before the breakdown (*Quek Lee Tiam v Ho Kim Swee* [1995] SGHC 23 at [18]) or evening out financial inequities (*BG v BF* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 233; *Foo Ah Yan v Chiam Heng Chow* [2012] SGCA 15). Maintenance is supplementary to the division of assets, and nominal maintenance to preserve a future claim is not ordered as a matter of course (*ATE v ATD*).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 113: power to order maintenance for wife/former wife or incapacitated husband/former husband, during or after proceedings.
> - s 114(1) factors (Cyril-Exam §5.2.2): income, earning capacity, property and resources now and in the foreseeable future; financial needs, obligations and responsibilities; standard of living before breakdown; age of each party and duration of the marriage; physical or mental disability; contributions to the family's welfare, including homemaking and caring; value of any benefit (e.g. pension) lost on divorce.
> - s 114(2): the court is to endeavour to place the parties, so far as practicable and, having regard to their conduct, just, in the financial position they would have been in had the marriage not broken down and each had properly discharged his or her financial obligations to the other.
> - Basis: financial preservation (*Quek Lee Tiam*, non-core); evening out inequities (*BG v BF*; *Foo Ah Yan*).
> - Non-core: *Quek Lee Tiam v Ho Kim Swee* [1995] SGHC 23 (FL-SYL [51]):
>   - "the principle of financial preservation": the former wife "can continue to live at a level as she did immediately before the breakdown of the marriage" ([18]);
>   - the obligation may end on a material change, such as her remarriage or the husband's later impecuniosity ([18]); her prospects of remarriage bear on a lump sum ([21]);
>   - "She has to exert herself, secure a gainful employment, and earn as much as reasonably possible" ([22]);
>   - claims to the former lifestyle are proved by evidence of expenses actually incurred (Timo, secondary); lump sum of $1.2m if the husband transferred the Arang Court maisonette to her as offered, or $1.8m if not — so the property she received reduced the maintenance ([3], [23]); the former matrimonial home (a pre-marriage gift from the husband's father) was excluded from division because her renovation was not substantial improvement ([25]).
> - *ATE v ATD*: supplementary to division; nominal maintenance not ordered as a matter of course; the former husband is not a "general insurer".
> - Cyril: maintenance not meant to create lifelong dependency; a wife who receives a large share of assets may get none (*UTL v UTM*, secondary).

**P** In *ATE v ATD* the Court of Appeal declined to make a nominal order as a matter of course, since something more must be shown to justify preserving a future claim (FL5 s9). In *Quek Lee Tiam* at [18], [22] (non-core) the High Court described the aim as financial preservation of the former wife's pre-breakdown standard of living, but required her to exert herself to find gainful employment and earn what she reasonably could.

**A** First, the division comes first: [Client will receive about $X from the division]. Second, needs: [she stopped work 3 years ago to care for the disabled child; reasonable personal expenses $Y]. Third, ability to pay: [Husband earns $12,000 gross]. Fourth, earning capacity and duration: [15-year marriage; she was a teacher earning $8,000 and may return to work in time] — she will be expected to exert herself to earn (*Quek Lee Tiam* [22]). Fifth, standard of living: [prove the pre-breakdown lifestyle with bank statements and receipts]; financial preservation aims at that standard so far as practicable (*Quek Lee Tiam* [18]). [Or: Client receives about S$170,000 a year in dividends — her needs are largely met; maintenance unlikely beyond nominal, which is not automatic.] **C** Therefore [Client] can expect maintenance of about [$Z / a lump sum / none].

*Source note: FL-SYL [43], [50]–[51] ([51] non-core); FL5 s9 (ss 113, 114; Quek Lee Tiam; BG v BF; Foo Ah Yan; ATE v ATD; s 117); WC-Text s 114(2); eLit (Quek Lee Tiam [18]–[23]); Timo §6.1.2 (secondary); Cyril-Exam §5.2.2 (s 114(1) list; UTL v UTM — secondary); PYP21-FAM (wife with HKD 1m dividends), PYP22-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-27 — Duration and variation of maintenance orders (s 117 WC; Tan Bee Giok)
*Keywords: duration of maintenance, s 117 WC, death, remarriage, variation of maintenance, s 118 WC, material change of circumstances, misrepresentation, mistake of fact, Tan Bee Giok v Loh Kum Yong, subsisting order required, nominal maintenance, APE v APF [2015] SGCA 47, preserve right to maintenance*

**I** The issue is [how long [Client]'s maintenance will last / whether [Client] can apply to vary or obtain maintenance later]. **C** [A maintenance order lasts, unless it says otherwise, until the death of either party or the recipient's remarriage (s 117 WC); a later variation requires a subsisting order (*Tan Bee Giok v Loh Kum Yong* [1996] SGCA 69), so if no order is made now, [Client] may be unable to claim later.]
**R** Duration of maintenance orders is governed by s 117 WC. A variation application can be made only where there is a subsisting maintenance order (*Tan Bee Giok*), which is why the courts sometimes preserved a wife's right by a nominal order (*APE v APF* [2015] SGCA 47); following *ATE v ATD* [2016] SGCA 2, nominal orders are not made as a matter of course.

> **Rule in full**
> - s 117: unless expressed for a shorter period or rescinded, (a) unsecured maintenance expires on the death of either spouse or former spouse, or on the remarriage of the former wife (or incapacitated former husband) receiving it; (b) secured maintenance expires on the recipient's death or remarriage (former spouse).
> - Variation requires a subsisting order (*Tan Bee Giok*).
> - *APE v APF* [2015] SGCA 47: preservation of the wife's right to maintenance (FL-SYL [54]).
> - *ATE v ATD*: nominal maintenance not automatic (1-26).
> - s 118: the court may at any time vary or rescind any subsisting maintenance order, on the application of either party, where the order was based on misrepresentation or mistake of fact or there has been a material change in circumstances.

**P** Cyril notes that *ATE v ATD* has qualified the older practice of nominal maintenance to preserve the right (Cyril-Exam §5.3).

**A** First, duration: [the order to Client will end on her remarriage or either party's death, unless a fixed period is set]. Second, future claims: if [Client] has no present need but may later (e.g. [deteriorating health]), she must show "something more" for a nominal order (*ATE v ATD*); otherwise she may be shut out (*Tan Bee Giok*). Third, variation: if [Husband loses his job], he may apply under s 118 to vary or rescind on a material change in circumstances. **C** Therefore [Client] should [seek an order now, even if modest] to preserve the ability to vary later.

*Source note: FL-SYL [52]–[54]; FL5 s9 (s 117; ATE v ATD); WC-Text ss 117, 118; Cyril-Exam §5.2.2–5.3 (Tan Bee Giok; APE v APF).*

## 1.VII Division of matrimonial assets

- **How will the court divide the assets?**
  - Power after divorce: "just and equitable" (s 112(1)); marriage as an equal partnership (*NK v NL*); no presumption of equality (*Lock Yeng Fun*) → [1-28](#pcp-w-1-28)
  - Foreign divorce, Singapore assets: permission, substantial ground, appropriate forum (Chapter 4A, ss 121B–121G; *UFN v UFM*) → [1-28](#pcp-w-1-28)
- **Step 1: what goes into the pool?**
  - Acquired during marriage: yes; pre-marital asset used as the family home or substantially improved: yes (s 112(10); *USB v USA*) → [1-29](#pcp-w-1-29)
  - Flat bought before marriage, lived in 2 years, wife paid renovation → [1-29](#pcp-w-1-29)
  - Gift or inheritance from parents; bungalow gifted as a wedding present; lottery or windfall → [1-30](#pcp-w-1-30)
  - Jewellery, watches or a car one spouse gave the other → included unless de minimis (*Wan Lai Cheng*; *Tan Hwee Lee*) → [1-30](#pcp-w-1-30)
  - Lottery / 4D / TOTO win during the marriage → matrimonial; presumed equal (*BOI v BOJ*) → [1-30](#pcp-w-1-30)
  - Wife worked in Husband's pre-marital business; paid for renovation → substantial improvement, direct causal link (*Koh Kim Lan*; *Chen Siew Hwee*) → [1-29](#pcp-w-1-29)
  - Which date for the pool and for values? (IJ; AM hearing; bank and CPF at IJ) → [1-31](#pcp-w-1-31)
- **Step 2–3: how are contributions weighed?**
  - Global or classification methodology (*NK v NL*; *UTQ v UTR*) → [1-32](#pcp-w-1-32)
  - ANJ: direct ratio, indirect ratio, average, adjust; s 112(2) factors → [1-33](#pcp-w-1-33)
  - Long single-income marriage: equal division trend (*TNL v TNK*); short marriages: direct contributions; huge pool by one spouse (*Yeo Chong Lin*) → [1-34](#pcp-w-1-34)
  - Homemaker who gave up work for a disabled child → [1-33](#pcp-w-1-33), [1-34](#pcp-w-1-34)
- **Prenup / postnup / consent order** → [1-35](#pcp-w-1-35)
  - Foreign prenup: valid under its proper law; not enforceable in itself; significant, even critical weight; custody terms presumed unenforceable (*TQ v TR*) → [1-35](#pcp-w-1-35)
- **Spouse says the property belongs to his parents / brother** → [1-36](#pcp-w-1-36)
- **Hidden assets; client wants to keep an account secret; spouse dissipated $1m gambling**
  - Duty of full and frank disclosure; adverse inference: quantification or uplift (*UZN v UZM*); I cannot help conceal → [1-37](#pcp-w-1-37)

### pcp-w-1-28 — The power to divide matrimonial assets: "just and equitable" (s 112(1) WC)
*Keywords: division of matrimonial assets, s 112(1) WC, just and equitable, equal cooperative partnership, NK v NL, Lim Choon Lai v Chew Kim Heng, no presumption of equality, Lock Yeng Fun v Chua Hock Chye, broad brush, Chapter 4A, foreign divorce, overseas divorce, divorced abroad, financial relief after foreign divorce, s 121B, s 121C, s 121D permission, substantial ground, s 121F appropriate forum, s 121G, UFN v UFM, ancillary power*

**I** The issue is on what basis the court will divide [the parties'] matrimonial assets. **C** On granting the divorce, the court may divide the matrimonial assets in such proportions as it thinks just and equitable (s 112(1) WC), treating marriage as an equal cooperative partnership of efforts in which financial and homemaking contributions are both recognised (*NK v NL* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 743).
**R** The court has power, on or after granting a judgment of divorce, judicial separation or nullity, to order the division of any matrimonial asset in such proportions as it thinks just and equitable (s 112(1) WC). Division is founded on marriage as "an equal co-operative partnership of efforts", recognising both economic and homemaking roles (*NK v NL*). There is no starting presumption of equal contribution (*Lim Choon Lai v Chew Kim Heng* [2001] 2 SLR(R) 260), nor any norm of equal division (*Lock Yeng Fun v Chua Hock Chye* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 520). After a foreign divorce, annulment or judicial separation that is recognised in Singapore, either party may, with the court's permission, apply for financial relief under Chapter 4A WC, and the court may then make any order it could make under ss 112, 113 or 127(1) (ss 121B, 121D, 121G); permission needs only a solid, not plainly unmeritorious, case that it is prima facie appropriate for a Singapore court to grant relief (*UFN v UFM* [2019] SGCA 54 at [29]) (non-core).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 112(1): power to order division of any matrimonial asset in such proportions as the court thinks just and equitable.
> - Singapore uses an open, "just and equitable" regime, not a presumption of equality (FL6 s4).
> - *NK v NL*: equal cooperative partnership; contributions of both spouses equally recognised; translated into economic assets under s 112(2).
> - Broad brush approach (*NK v NL*; *Yeo Chong Lin v Tay Ang Choo Nancy* [2011] 2 SLR 1157; *ANJ v ANK* [2015] 4 SLR 1043; *UYQ v UYP* [2020] 1 SLR 551).
> - Power is ancillary: assets divided after the marriage is dissolved; all matrimonial assets are subject to division.
> - Chapter 4A WC (non-core; FL-SYL [85]) — financial relief after foreign matrimonial proceedings (WC-Text):
>   - s 121B: the marriage was dissolved or annulled, or the parties legally separated, by proceedings in a foreign country, and that is entitled to recognition in Singapore → either party may apply for financial relief;
>   - s 121C jurisdiction: one party (a) domiciled in Singapore, or (b) habitually resident in Singapore for a continuous year, on the date of the permission application or the date the foreign divorce took effect;
>   - s 121D: permission of the court is needed first; it is not granted unless "there is substantial ground" for the application; it may be granted even though a foreign court has ordered payment or transfer; conditions may be attached;
>   - s 121E: interim financial provision where the applicant or a child is in immediate financial need;
>   - s 121F: the court must dismiss unless satisfied it is appropriate for a Singapore court to make the order, having regard in particular to (a) the parties' connection with Singapore; (b) with the country of the divorce; (c) with any other country; (d) financial benefit received or likely under any agreement or foreign law; (e) any foreign order and how far it has been or will be complied with; (f) any right to seek relief abroad and why it was not exercised; (g) matrimonial assets available in Singapore; (h) enforceability; (i) time since the divorce;
>   - s 121G: any order available under s 112, 113 or 127(1) as if the divorce had been granted in Singapore; ss 112(2)–(10) and 114–120 apply;
>   - s 121A(2): only foreign proceedings, decrees or orders on or after 1 Jun 2011.
> - *UFN v UFM* [2019] SGCA 54 ([2019] 2 SLR 650) (CA; first Chapter 4A appeal) ([1]):
>   - leave (s 121D) filters out "plainly unmeritorious cases"; "substantial ground" means solid, i.e. not plainly unmeritorious; the applicant need only show it is "prima facie appropriate" for a Singapore court to grant relief; the threshold is "not high" ([22]–[23], [29]); leave applications should be heard ex parte ([26]–[28]);
>   - the s 121F factors are "statutory signposts" at the leave stage; at the substantive stage the court must conclude that it is appropriate, not merely prima facie so ([31]–[34]);
>   - the applicant need not show Singapore is the more appropriate forum ([47]); factors: connection with Singapore ([43]–[47]); fairness and practicality of any foreign relief, respecting comity and avoiding a "second bite of the cherry" ([48]); no need to exhaust foreign remedies, but the reasons for not seeking them are scrutinised according to the strength of the connection ([49]); enforceability ([50]–[57]);
>   - the husband's appeal against leave was dismissed ([65]).
