> - Duty of full and frank disclosure (*USB v USA* [2020] 2 SLR 588); failure → adverse inferences (*UZN v UZM* [2021] 1 SLR 426) (1-37).
> - Practical steps (FL6 s39–40): identify assets under s 112(10) (1-29); note third-party rights (1-36); value and compute net pool (assets minus debts); for an HDB flat, the Matrimonial Property Plan and HDB/CPF information; classify the marriage; apply ANJ or not (1-33, 1-34); consider practical outcomes (who can afford to keep the flat; HDB and CPF feasibility).

**P** In *Lock Yeng Fun* the Court of Appeal discouraged treating equality as the starting point or norm, as it could lead a judge to seek equality regardless of the facts (FL6 s17).

**A** First, set out the method: identify the pool (1-29 to 1-31), choose the methodology (1-32), apply the ANJ approach or the long single-income approach (1-33, 1-34), and adjust for adverse inferences (1-37). Second, flag practical outcomes: [whether Client can keep the HDB flat and service the loan; CPF refunds]. [If the divorce was granted abroad (non-core): (i) recognition and jurisdiction — [the Japanese divorce is recognised; Client has lived here for ten years] (ss 121B–121C); (ii) apply for permission, showing substantial ground — [the foreign court made no order on the Singapore condominium and CPF] (s 121D); (iii) appropriate forum under s 121F — [both lived here; the assets are here; no relief was obtained or available abroad; applied promptly]; (iv) the court then divides as under s 112 (s 121G).] **C** Therefore the court will divide the pool justly and equitably according to the parties' direct and indirect contributions.

*Source note: FL-SYL [55], [85] (non-core); WC-Text ss 121A–121G; eLit (UFN v UFM [1]–[65]); Cyril-Exam §6; FL6 s3–8, s17, s39–40 (s 112(1); NK v NL; Lim Choon Lai; Lock Yeng Fun; broad brush cases; Chapter 4A; UFN v UFM; USB v USA; UZM v UZN); PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM, PYP23-FAM Q6, PYP25 Q5.*

### pcp-w-1-29 — Identifying matrimonial assets (s 112(10) WC; USB v USA)
*Keywords: matrimonial assets, s 112(10) WC, acquired during marriage, pre-marital asset, ordinarily used by the family, matrimonial home, substantially improved, USB v USA, four categories, transformed pre-marital asset, portion paid during marriage, CPF, HDB flat, condominium bought before marriage, renovation, rented out, sole name, Koh Kim Lan Angela, substantial improvement, direct causal link, indirect contributions too remote, Chen Siew Hwee, family business, shares in husband's company, BJS v BJT, tenanted property, Ryan Neil John, minimal increase in value, Ong Boon Huat, asset excluded although acquired during marriage, disowned purchase*

**I** The issue is which of [the parties'] assets fall into the pool of matrimonial assets, in particular [the Aljunied apartment Husband bought before the marriage]. **C** All assets acquired during the marriage are matrimonial assets regardless of whose name they are in (s 112(10)(b) WC); [the Aljunied apartment], though acquired before the marriage, became a matrimonial asset because it was ordinarily used as the family home [and was substantially improved by Wife's renovation], so its entire value goes into the pool (*USB v USA* [2020] 2 SLR 588).
**R** A matrimonial asset is (a) an asset acquired before the marriage by one or both parties that was ordinarily used or enjoyed by both parties or their children for shelter, transportation, household, educational, recreational, social or aesthetic purposes, or substantially improved during the marriage by the other party or both; and (b) any other asset acquired during the marriage by one or both parties; but excludes gifts and inheritances not substantially improved and not the matrimonial home (s 112(10) WC). *USB v USA* identifies four categories. Substantial improvement requires effort or money's worth by the other spouse (or both) that bears "a direct causal link to the substantial improvement of the asset" (*Koh Kim Lan Angela v Choong Kian Haw* [1993] SGCA 83 at [27]); indirect financial contributions alone are too vague and remote (*Chen Siew Hwee v Low Kee Guan* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 605 at [51]) (non-core).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 112(10)(a): an asset acquired before the marriage by one or both parties (i) ordinarily used or enjoyed by both parties or one or more of their children while the parties are residing together for shelter or transportation or for household, education, recreational, social or aesthetic purposes; or (ii) substantially improved during the marriage by the other party or both.
> - s 112(10)(b): all other assets acquired during the marriage by one or both parties.
> - Proviso: gifts and inheritances (not substantially improved and not the matrimonial home) excluded (1-30).
> - *USB v USA* four categories:
>   - (i) quintessential matrimonial assets (s 112(10)(b)) and the matrimonial home — entire value in the pool;
>   - (ii) transformed pre-marital assets (ordinary use or substantial improvement) — entire value;
>   - (iii) pre-marital assets meeting neither limb — only the portion paid for during the marriage;
>   - (iv) gifts or inheritances transformed (matrimonial home or substantial improvement) — entire value.
> - Possibility of discounting (*TNC v TND* [2017] SGCA 34, raised in FL6 s14).
> - Examples (FL6 s16): immovable property, businesses and shares, jewellery and watch collections, insurance policies, unit trusts, cars, savings, fixed deposits, CPF balances acquired during the marriage.
> - Common client mistake: solely held assets thought to be "my own" — all assets acquired during the marriage are matrimonial (FL6 s13).
> - Substantial improvement (non-core; FL-SYL [63]–[64]):
>   - the other spouse's (or both spouses') efforts "must bear a direct causal link to the substantial improvement of the asset" (*Koh Kim Lan Angela v Choong Kian Haw* [1993] SGCA 83 at [27]); improvement in an economic sense, by money or money's worth (Cel; Timo, secondary);
>   - *Chen Siew Hwee* at [51]: "no direct causal connection whatsoever" between the wife's contributions and the husband's shares; indirect financial contributions alone are too vague and remote; the shares stayed out of the pool;
>   - *Koh Kim Lan*: that the asset was initially a gift "is no bar" ([26]); the wife's relatively minor role in the husband's business (sales trips, entertaining clients) sufficed to bring it in; the small extent of her effort is reflected in the quantum, not by exclusion ([31]); she received 15% ([45]);
>   - *BJS v BJT* [2013] SGHC 130: to bring the husband's business in, "the wife needs to prove that she had substantially improved the business" — hard with a short marriage and an established business ([32]); a property stayed in only occasionally when not tenanted out was not "ordinarily used", which needs "some form of substantiality" ([23]–[24]);
> - Excluding an asset acquired during the marriage (FL-SYL [58(f)], non-core): *Ong Boon Huat Samuel v Chan Mei Lan Kristine* [2007] SGCA 19 (FL-SYL cites the earlier procedural decision [2006] SGCA 26): a flat bought in the husband's sole name after the marriage had broken down, which the wife had disowned along with its liabilities, was excluded though it "technically falls under the definition of a matrimonial asset"; the power to divide is discretionary and the court "may generally decline to do so where a valid reason is given"; she "cannot now claim what she once rejected" ([20]–[27]); in a short, childless marriage division usually follows direct financial contributions ([28]).
>   - minimal increases in value are disregarded (*Ryan Neil John v Berger Rosaline* [2000] SGHC 236, per Shang and Nic-Fam, secondary);
>   - examples: renovation paid for by the other spouse that raises a property's value; work that measurably raises a business's turnover or profit; not cleaning, nominal directorships or minor admin (Cel, secondary).

**P** *USB v USA* defined matrimonial assets as "all the material gains of the marital partnership" (FL6 s14). In *Koh Kim Lan* (non-core) the Court of Appeal brought the husband's company shares into the pool because the wife's efforts in the business, though relatively minor, bore a direct causal link to its growth, and reflected their small extent in her 15% share ([27], [31], [45]).

**A** First, list each asset and classify it: [HDB flat in joint names bought during the marriage — category (i)]; [CPF balances accrued during the marriage; car; bank accounts in each name — category (i)]; [Aljunied apartment bought before the marriage in Husband's sole name, used as the family home for 2 years while awaiting the HDB keys — ordinarily used for shelter, category (ii); Wife's $20,000 renovation may also be substantial improvement]; [its later rental does not undo the transformation]; [Husband's pre-marital shop or company, in which Wife worked] — substantial improvement only if her work bears a direct causal link to a measurable improvement (higher turnover or profit), not mere indirect financial support (*Chen Siew Hwee*) or nominal roles; a small but real role still counts, with its size reflected in her share (*Koh Kim Lan*); [a holiday flat bought before marriage, mostly rented out] — not ordinarily used (*BJS v BJT*); [a flat Husband bought after the marriage broke down, which Wife refused to have anything to do with] — may be excluded although acquired during the marriage (*Ong Boon Huat* [25]); [an asset bought before marriage and never used by the family — only the portion paid during the marriage, category (iii)]. Second, note pre-marital CPF and assets that remain outside. Third, value the pool net of debts at the correct dates (1-31). **C** Therefore the pool comprises [list], including [the Aljunied apartment] at its full value.

*Source note: FL-SYL [57]–[58], [63]–[64] (non-core); WC-Text s 112(10); FL6 s9, s12–16 (s 112(10); USB v USA categories; TNC v TND; examples); eLit (Koh Kim Lan [26]–[45]; Chen Siew Hwee [51]; BJS v BJT [23]–[32]; Ong Boon Huat [2007] SGCA 19 [20]–[28]); Nic-Fam, Shang, Cel, Timo (secondary); PYP25 Q5 (Aljunied apartment); PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-30 — Gifts, inheritances, inter-spousal gifts and windfalls (non-core)
*Keywords: gift, inheritance, excluded from pool, matrimonial home exception, substantially improved, wedding gift, gift from parents, donor's intention, unwarranted windfall, CLC v CLB, donee spouse intention, re-gift, family estate, Chen Siew Hwee, inter-spousal gift, gift from husband, watches, handbags, jewellery, Wan Lai Cheng v Quek Seow Kee, Tan Hwee Lee v Tan Cheng Guan, Yeo Gim Tong Michael, de minimis, windfall, unexpected sale price, AYM v AYL, lottery, lottery winnings, TOTO, 4D, ticket bought for the family, presumption of equal contribution, BOI v BOJ, AAE v AAF, Ang Teng Siong*

**I** The issue is whether [the bungalow gifted by Wife's parents / Wife's inheritance / the jewellery Husband gave Wife / the lottery win] is part of the pool. **C** A gift or inheritance from a third party is excluded unless it is the matrimonial home or has been substantially improved during the marriage (s 112(10) WC); [the bungalow, used as the matrimonial home, is included; Wife's Hong Kong properties are excluded]; a gift from one spouse to the other is included unless de minimis (*Tan Hwee Lee v Tan Cheng Guan* [2012] SGCA 50 at [44], [49]); and lottery winnings during the marriage are a matrimonial asset, presumed to be contributed equally (*BOI v BOJ* [2019] SGCA 30 at [10], [29]).
**R** Assets acquired by gift or inheritance from a third party are not matrimonial assets unless they are the matrimonial home or have been substantially improved during the marriage by the other party or both (s 112(10) WC); the exclusion recognises the donor's intention and prevents an unwarranted windfall to the other spouse (*Chen Siew Hwee v Low Kee Guan* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 605 at [32]). The court may also give effect to the donee spouse's intention, either to re-gift the asset to the other spouse (excluding it) or to bring it into the family estate (including it) (*CLC v CLB* [2023] 1 SLR 1260). A pure inter-spousal gift is not a "gift" within s 112(10) and is a matrimonial asset without more, since it embodies the donor spouse's effort (*Wan Lai Cheng v Quek Seow Kee* [2012] 4 SLR 405 at [40]–[41]); it is always included unless de minimis, and any unfairness is addressed in the division (*Tan Hwee Lee* at [41], [44], [49]). Lottery winnings during the marriage are a matrimonial asset, and both spouses are presumed to have contributed equally unless the buyer shows the ticket was bought for himself or herself alone (*BOI v BOJ* at [10], [20], [29]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 112(10): gifts and inheritances excluded unless (a) the matrimonial home, or (b) substantially improved during the marriage by the other party or both.
> - *Chen Siew Hwee* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 605: the exclusion rests on "the recognition of the donor's intention as well as the concomitant need to prevent unwarranted windfalls" ([32]); it applies whether the gift was made before or during the marriage ([36]).
> - *CLC v CLB*: conversion of gifts and inheritances; the donee spouse's intention may convert them — a re-gift to the other spouse is excluded from the pool; an intention to bring the asset into the family estate includes it; ownership outside s 112(10) may be decided by general property law.
> - *VOD v VOC* [2022] SGHC(A) 6 (FL6 s11).
> - Inter-spousal gifts (non-core; FL-SYL [68]–[70]):
>   - *Wan Lai Cheng* [2012] SGCA 40 at [40]–[42]: a pure inter-spousal gift is a matrimonial asset "without the need to satisfy any further conditions"; it embodies "the initial effort expended by the donor spouse"; third-party gifts are excluded to "prevent an unmerited windfall"; a third-party gift or inheritance "re-gifted" by one spouse to the other stays outside the pool, since the donor spent no effort acquiring it ([55]), and the substantial-improvement exception does not apply to such re-gifts ([56]); the donee's non-matrimonial property may still be weighed under s 112(2)(h) read with s 114(1) ([57]);
>   - *Tan Hwee Lee* [2012] SGCA 50: an inter-spousal gift "should always be included in the pool" ([44]); the de minimis exception is retained ([49]); a "proprietary interests" exception is rejected ([50]–[52]); where it would be clearly inequitable for the donor to take a substantial share of the gift, that is dealt with in the division under s 112(1), not by exclusion ([41], [44]);
>   - *Yeo Gim Tong Michael v Tianzon Lolita* [1996] SGCA 14: property acquired during the marriage by the donor's effort is divisible "notwithstanding that it was a gift from one spouse to the other" ([12]); "minor items of gift such as dresses and even jewellery of no substantial value, are normally considered as de minimis", but gifts of substantial value (a car, land, investments) count ([13]); land in the Philippines the husband bought for the wife was included ([14]). (Pinpoints in the judgment's own numbering, as *Wan Lai Cheng* [55] cites [12]; eLitigation's running numbers are five higher.)
> - Windfalls (non-core; FL-SYL [71]–[72]):
>   - *AYM v AYL* [2014] SGCA 46: after a consent order, the matrimonial flat sold for $5.1m against a ceiling the parties had assumed of $3.75m — "an entirely unforeseen and unexpected windfall" ([27]); the agreed split stood ([28]), but the windfall was a material change in circumstances, so the wife's lump-sum maintenance was reduced by half the difference in what each spouse received from the windfall ([29]–[30], [44]);
>   - *BOI v BOJ* [2019] SGCA 30: lottery winnings are a matrimonial asset, not a gift or inheritance ([9]–[10], [18]); the intention with which the ticket was bought is "the more important point" ([20]); because of the "radical disproportionality" between ticket price and prize ([25]), "there is a presumption that both spouses contribute equally", rebutted if the buyer shows the ticket was bought only for himself or herself ([29]); winnings in 2002 were attributed equally, raising the wife's overall share from 42% to 49.1% ([36], [39]); winnings after separation were not ([37]).
> - Gifts and inheritance cases (non-core; FL-SYL [67]):
>   - *Chen Siew Hwee* (above);
>   - *AAE v AAF* [2009] SGHC 104: the matrimonial home "is irrefutably a matrimonial asset" even if bought with proceeds of pre-marital or inherited property; the link to the original inheritance was "too negligible to be counted"; registering the wife as joint tenant was "strong evidence" of an intention to integrate the assets into the pool ([10]); the family business and premises given to the husband before the marriage were excluded ([12]);
>   - *Ang Teng Siong v Lee Su Min* [2000] SGHC 76: a third-party gift received during the marriage is not divisible unless it is the matrimonial home or improved by the other party or both — "the matrimonial partnership stops short of extending to gifts received by either party"; whether a parent's gift goes to both spouses turns on the donor's manifested intention (a home put in joint names by the wife's father was a gift to both). The online text is unnumbered; the gift passage is cited as [11] in *Chen Siew Hwee* (as quoted in *AAE v AAF* [8]).

**P** In *BOI v BOJ* [2019] SGCA 30 at [29]–[39], the Court of Appeal attributed lottery winnings won during the marriage equally to both spouses, presuming that both had contributed equally since the ticket was not shown to have been bought for the husband alone, and the wife's share of the pool rose accordingly. In *CLC v CLB* the Court of Appeal added the donee's intention as a further way of converting a gift or inheritance into, or out of, the pool (FL6 s14).

**A** First, identify the source of each asset: [Grange Road bungalow — wedding gift from Wife's parents, registered jointly, used as the matrimonial home] → included as the matrimonial home; [Wife's two Hong Kong properties — gifts, never used by the family or improved] → excluded; [Wife's inheritance placed in the joint account and used for the family] → consider *CLC v CLB* (intention to bring it into the family estate). Second, gifts between the spouses: [watches, handbags and a car Husband gave Wife] → included (*Wan Lai Cheng*; *Tan Hwee Lee*), unless [small birthday gifts] de minimis; if it would be clearly inequitable for [Husband] to share in them, argue it at the division stage. Third, windfalls: [Husband's $1m 4D win during the marriage] → matrimonial asset; presumed bought for both and attributed equally unless he proves he bought it for himself alone (*BOI v BOJ*); [a win after separation] → not equal; [an unexpected sale price after a consent order] → may justify varying maintenance (*AYM v AYL*). **C** Therefore [asset] is [included / excluded], and [the lottery win is shared equally].

*Source note: FL-SYL [67]–[72] (non-core); FL6 s11, s14 (s 112(10) proviso; VOD v VOC; CLC v CLB; Chen Siew Hwee; Wan Lai Cheng); eLit (Chen Siew Hwee [32], [36]; Wan Lai Cheng [40]–[57]; Yeo Gim Tong [12]–[14]; AAE v AAF [8]–[12]; Ang Teng Siong; Tan Hwee Lee [41]–[52]; AYM v AYL [27]–[44]; BOI v BOJ [9]–[39]); PYP21-FAM (Grange Road bungalow; HK properties), PYP22-FAM (gifted watches and handbags).*

### pcp-w-1-31 — Dates for identifying and valuing the pool (ARY v ARX; BPC v BPB)
*Keywords: date of interim judgment, IJ, operative date, pool of assets date, valuation date, ancillary matters hearing, AM hearing, bank accounts, CPF accounts, ARY v ARX, BPC v BPB, date of separation, date of filing, departure from default*

**I** The issue is at what date [the parties'] assets are identified and valued. **C** By default the pool is identified as at the date of Interim Judgment and valued as at the ancillary matters hearing, except bank and CPF balances, which are taken at the Interim Judgment date (*ARY v ARX* [2016] SGCA 13; *BPC v BPB* [2019] SGCA 3).
**R** The default is that matrimonial assets are identified as at the date of Interim Judgment and valued as at the date of the ancillary matters hearing, save that monies in bank and CPF accounts are taken as at the Interim Judgment date; the court may depart from the default in particular circumstances (*ARY v ARX*; *BPC v BPB*).

> **Rule in full**
> - Four possible dates for identifying the pool: (a) separation; (b) filing of the divorce; (c) Interim Judgment; (d) ancillary matters hearing.
> - Default (*ARY v ARX*; *BPC v BPB*): identify at Interim Judgment; value at the AM hearing; bank and CPF monies at the Interim Judgment date; departure where the circumstances justify it.

**P** In *BPC v BPB* the Court of Appeal confirmed the separate dates for identification (Interim Judgment) and valuation (ancillary hearing) (FL-SYL [60]).

**A** First, identify the pool at [Interim Judgment, July 2025]: [assets acquired after that, e.g. Husband's new car, fall outside unless the default is departed from]. Second, value [the HDB flat and Aljunied apartment] at the AM hearing (current valuations), but [bank and CPF balances] at the Interim Judgment date. Third, consider whether there is reason to depart: [money withdrawn after separation to defeat the claim — argue for an earlier date or add it back]. **C** Therefore the pool is fixed at [date] and valued at [date].

*Source note: FL-SYL [59]–[60]; FL6 s10 (ARY v ARX; BPC v BPB; four dates; default).*

### pcp-w-1-32 — Global assessment and classification methodologies (NK v NL; UTQ v UTR)
*Keywords: global assessment methodology, classification methodology, NK v NL, UTQ v UTR, classes of assets, indirect contributions constant across classes, adverse inference one class, net value, which methodology*

**I** The issue is whether the court should assess contributions across the whole pool or class by class. **C** The global assessment methodology is the usual approach; classification is appropriate only where there are distinct classes of assets to which the parties contributed differently or some assets are not the product of the marital partnership (*UTQ v UTR* [2019] SGHCF 13), which [is / is not] the case here.
**R** The global assessment methodology calculates the net value of all matrimonial assets and divides it justly and equitably; the classification methodology separates the assets into classes with individual apportionments, keeping indirect contributions constant across classes (*NK v NL* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 743). *UTQ v UTR* considered which applies.

> **Rule in full**
> - Global assessment: compute the net value of all matrimonial assets; divide justly and equitably in light of all the circumstances.
> - Classification: separate the assets into classes; apportion each; indirect contributions remain constant across classes.
> - *UTQ v UTR*: classification only where there are multiple classes and some assets are not wholly the gains of the cooperative partnership; otherwise global (per Cyril-Exam §6 Step 2).
> - *NK v NL* applied classification because an adverse inference was drawn about the husband's cash assets (per Cyril).

**P** Section 112 is a broad directive; recent trends guide the likely proportions (FL6 s18).

**A** First, ask whether any class of assets was built by one party alone or is affected by an adverse inference: [Husband's investment portfolio funded solely from his pre-marital wealth and efforts; an adverse inference about his undisclosed accounts] → classification may be appropriate. Otherwise [a dual-income couple with jointly funded assets] → global assessment. **C** Therefore the court is likely to use the [global] methodology.

*Source note: FL-SYL [61]–[62]; FL6 s18 (NK v NL methodologies); Cyril-Exam §6 Step 2 (UTQ v UTR; NK v NL applications — secondary).*

### pcp-w-1-33 — The ANJ structured approach and the s 112(2) factors
*Keywords: ANJ v ANK, structured approach, direct contributions, indirect contributions, financial contributions, non-financial contributions, ratio, average ratio, weightage, adjustments, s 112(2) WC factors, homemaker, domestic helper, dual-income marriage, CPF contributions, mortgage, Twiss, UYP v UYQ, broad brush, gave up career, disabled child, conduct, gross misconduct, rent-free occupation*

**I** The issue is how the court will divide the pool between [Husband] and [Wife] in their [15-year dual-income] marriage. **C** The court will apply the ANJ structured approach (*ANJ v ANK* [2015] 4 SLR 1043): it will fix a ratio of direct financial contributions, a ratio of indirect contributions, average them (adjusting the weight for the circumstances), and then make adjustments under s 112(2) WC; on the facts [Wife] can expect about [X]%.
**R** The court (1) expresses as a ratio the parties' direct financial contributions to acquiring or improving the matrimonial assets; (2) expresses as a ratio their indirect contributions, financial and non-financial, to the family's welfare; (3) averages the two, giving more weight to one where the circumstances warrant; and (4) makes further adjustments to reach a just and equitable result, having regard to all the circumstances including the s 112(2) factors (*ANJ v ANK*). The approach is applied with a broad brush: "a rigid, mechanistic and overly-arithmetical application of the structured approach in ANJ v ANK must be assiduously avoided" (*UYQ v UYP* [2020] SGCA 3 at [3]); in a long breadwinner/homemaker marriage it tends to favour the breadwinner, whose financial contributions are credited at both stages (*UBM v UBN* [2017] SGHCF 13 at [23]), and it does not wipe out the value of earlier precedents ([61]).

> **Rule in full**
> - ANJ steps:
>   - 1: direct contributions ratio — financial contributions to acquisition or improvement; rough approximation where documents fall short;
>   - 2: indirect contributions ratio — financial (household expenses) and non-financial (homemaking, caregiving); a matter of impression;
>   - 3: average, keeping in mind that direct and indirect contributions may be given unequal weight;
>   - 4 (Cyril): further adjustments, including adverse inferences (uplift) and the s 112(2) factors.
> - Considerations at step 3 (FL6 s20): (i) indirect contributions feature more in long marriages; (ii) direct contributions weigh more where the pool is extraordinarily large and built by one party's exceptional efforts; (iii) courts lean towards homemakers who raised children at the expense of careers, as opposed to engaging a helper.
> - s 112(2): all the circumstances, including (a) contributions in money, property or work towards acquiring, improving or maintaining the assets; (b) debts or obligations for joint benefit or the children; (c) needs of minor children; (d) contributions to the family's welfare, including looking after the home or caring for the family or any aged or infirm relative; (e) any agreement on ownership and division made in contemplation of divorce; (f) rent-free occupation or other benefit in the matrimonial home; (g) assistance or support by one party to the other (material or not), including in the other's occupation or business; (h) the s 114(1) matters so far as relevant (conduct — only gross misconduct affects division).
> - Developments (FL6 s21): *ATE v ATD* [2016] SGCA 2 (short marriages); *TNL v TNK* [2017] 1 SLR 609 (not applied to long single-income marriages, 1-34); *DBA v DBB* [2024] SGHC(A) 12 (single vs dual income).
> - *UYQ v UYP* [2020] SGCA 3 ([2020] 1 SLR 551; FL-SYL [77] calls it "UYP v UYQ") — long dual-income marriages: division "must be made in the context of family law principles"; in long marriages it is "an impossible exercise" to account for every record ([2]); rigid, mechanistic, overly arithmetical application "must be assiduously avoided" ([3]); parties should show "reasonable accounting rigour" but not flood the court, on pain of costs ([4]); the CA restored 67:33 to the wife from the judge's 60:40, the judge's downward adjustment for an "inclination towards equal division" in long dual-income marriages being left open ([5]); High Court below, *UYP v UYQ* [2019] SGHCF 16: findings are "necessarily impressionistic" in long marriages ([62]), and the difficulties arise only when ANJ is applied "in a rigid and calculative way" ([65]).
> - *UBM v UBN* [2017] SGHCF 13 (FL-SYL [76]) — rationalising ANJ with earlier cases: a 37-year single-income marriage; direct 100:0 to the husband, indirect 65:35 to the wife ([17]–[20]); ANJ "favours the breadwinner" in such marriages because financial contributions get "credit at two stages" ([23]); unequal weighting "involves a measure of artificiality" ([25]–[26]); the average was adjusted to 60:40 by reference to marriage as an equal partnership, long-marriage factors and the trend of decided cases ([27]–[35]); ANJ's limitations "fall away if it is embraced as a useful guide ... within a broad brush approach" ([58]); "The introduction of the structured approach in ANJ v ANK did not wipe out the value of precedents pre-dating it" ([61]); records: parties in a functioning marriage may not keep records fastidiously ([15], [59]).
> - *Twiss v Twiss* [2015] SGCA 52 (FL-SYL [75], non-core) — applying ANJ: the judge's "uplift" method (75% direct plus 20%) was an error of law ([15]); giving all assets to one spouse only in the most exceptional or extreme circumstances ([16]); direct and indirect contributions "may not be accorded equal weight" ([17]); direct 70:30 and indirect 75:25 to the wife; more weight to indirect in a fairly long marriage with a modest pool and career sacrifice, so 75:25 adopted ([18]–[24]).
> - Example (Cyril): direct 60:40 (H:W), indirect 40:60, equal weight → 50:50; with 60:40 weight to direct → 52:48.

**P** In *ANJ v ANK* the Court of Appeal designed the structured approach so that financial and non-financial contributions receive equal emphasis in reaching a just and equitable division (FL6 s19). In *Twiss v Twiss* [2015] SGCA 52 at [15]–[24] the Court of Appeal replaced the judge's "uplift" method with the ANJ steps and, giving more weight to the wife's indirect contributions in a fairly long marriage with career sacrifice, awarded her 75%; in *UYQ v UYP* [2020] SGCA 3 at [3]–[5] it warned against mechanistic application and restored a 67:33 division to the wife.

**A** First, classify the marriage: [15 years; both worked until Wife stopped 3 years ago to care for the disabled younger child] — dual-income for most of it, so ANJ applies (not *TNL v TNK*). Second, direct contributions: [HDB flat — equal initial payments and CPF; Husband alone serviced the mortgage for 3 years; Aljunied apartment — Husband's pre-marital asset, Wife's $20,000 renovation; CPF and bank balances in each name] → roughly [65:35] to Husband. Third, indirect contributions: [Wife's care of the boys, especially the younger child with cerebral palsy, and sacrifice of her HOD career; Husband's support] → [35:65] in Wife's favour. Fourth, average with weight: in a 15-year marriage with young children, equal weight is reasonable → about [50:50]; the court leans towards a homemaker who sacrificed her career. Fifth, adjustments: [needs of the disabled child (s 112(2)(c)); the rental income used for the mortgage; any adverse inference]. **C** Therefore [Wife] can expect about [45–50]% of the pool, and [Husband] should be advised accordingly.

*Source note: FL-SYL [56], [73]–[77] ([75] non-core); eLit (UYQ v UYP [2020] SGCA 3 [2]–[5] and [2019] SGHCF 16 [62]–[65]; UBM v UBN [15]–[61]; Twiss [15]–[24]); FL6 s16, s19–21 (s 112(2); ANJ steps and considerations; developments); Cyril-Exam §6 Steps 3–4 (example; adjustments); PYP23-FAM Q6, PYP25 Q5.*

### pcp-w-1-34 — Long single-income marriages and trends in proportions (TNL v TNK)
*Keywords: long marriage, single-income marriage, homemaker, sole breadwinner, TNL v TNK, equal division, ANJ not applied, BOR v BOS, Yeo Chong Lin v Tay Ang Choo Nancy, exceptional contributions, $69 million, 65%, short marriage, childless, direct contributions dominate, UBM v UBN, DBA v DBB, appellate threshold 10%, trends*

**I** The issue is whether the ANJ approach applies to [the parties'] [20-year] marriage, in which [Wife] was a homemaker and [Husband] the sole earner, and what division is likely. **C** The ANJ approach is not applied to long single-income marriages, where the courts incline towards equal division (*TNL v TNK* [2017] 1 SLR 609); [Wife] can therefore expect close to [50]%.
**R** In long single-income marriages the ANJ approach tends to favour the earning spouse, so the courts instead look to precedent, which tends towards equal division (*TNL v TNK*). Indirect contributions weigh more in longer marriages, whereas in short, especially childless, marriages the division tends to track direct contributions. Direct contributions may weigh more where an extraordinarily large pool was built by one spouse's exceptional efforts (*Yeo Chong Lin v Tay Ang Choo Nancy* [2011] 2 SLR 1157, 65% to the husband).

> **Rule in full**
> - *TNL v TNK*: ANJ not applied to long single-income marriages; incline towards equal division (45-year marriage); appellate threshold high — adjustments under 10% not sympathetically received; costs risk.
> - Trends (FL6 s22–23): CA decisions on long marriages hover at equal division or within about 10% of it (*Lock Yeng Fun*; *BOR v BOS* [2018] SGCA 78); homemakers in long marriages often receive 40–50%; short marriages, especially without children, track direct contributions (*WGE v WGF* [2023] SGHCF 26; *WQR v WQS* [2023] SGHCF 41).
> - *Yeo Chong Lin* (FL6 s24): 49-year marriage, $69m pool, husband's "unusual drive and energy" — 65% to husband despite a long single-income marriage.
> - Features affecting proportions (FL6 s25): length of marriage; size of pool; total failure of one spouse in his or her role; total failure by one spouse in carrying out his or her role (*Chan Siew Fong v Chan Fook Kee* [2002] 1 SLR(R) 93); gross misconduct, e.g. poisoning a spouse (*Chan Tin Sun v Fong Quay Sim* [2015] SGCA 2).
> - Single vs dual income (*DBA v DBB* [2024] SGHC(A) 12); "single-income" includes some financial contribution by the homemaker (Cyril, citing *UBM v UBN* [2017] SGHCF 13).

**P** In *TNL v TNK* the Court of Appeal explained that the ANJ approach, applied mechanically to a long single-income marriage, could require artificially high indirect ratios for the homemaker, so precedent should guide instead (Cyril-Exam §6).

**A** First, classify: [married since 2002, about 20 years; Wife gave up her gym job in 2006 to raise the children; Husband the main earner] — long and substantially single-income → *TNL v TNK* applies, inclining to equal division. Second, check exceptions: [is the pool extraordinarily large and built by Husband's exceptional efforts? $2m apartment and investments — substantial but not Yeo Chong Lin]. Third, adjust for any gross misconduct or adverse inference ([Wife's undisclosed $240,000 POSB account — 1-37]). [For a short, childless marriage, expect division close to the direct contributions ratio.] **C** Therefore [Wife] can expect about [45–50]% of the pool.

*Source note: FL-SYL [74]–[78]; FL6 s21–26 (TNL v TNK; trends; BOR v BOS; Yeo Chong Lin; features; DBA v DBB; appellate threshold); Cyril-Exam §6 (TNL reasoning; UBM v UBN — secondary); PYP22-FAM, PYP25 Q5.*

### pcp-w-1-35 — Pre-nuptial and post-nuptial agreements; consent orders (TQ v TR) (non-core)
*Keywords: prenuptial agreement, prenup, pre-marital agreement, postnuptial agreement, marriage contract, s 112(2)(e), agreement in contemplation of divorce, TQ v TR, foreign prenup, Dutch law, proper law, validity, significant or even critical weight, highest significance, no blanket rule, not enforceable in itself, cannot oust the court's power, custody agreement presumed unenforceable, maintenance agreement, s 116, s 119, court's scrutiny, consent order, Surindar Singh, variation of consent order, AYM v AYL, setting aside consent order, AOO v AON, BMI v BMJ, settlement*

**I** The issue is what effect [the parties' pre-nuptial agreement] has on division [and on maintenance and the children]. **C** It is not void but cannot be enforced in and of itself; on division it is a factor under s 112(2)(e) WC that may carry significant, even critical, weight depending on the circumstances (*TQ v TR* [2009] SGCA 6 at [73], [77], [87]); [its terms on custody are presumed unenforceable, and its terms on maintenance are subject to the court's scrutiny ([67], [70])]; so [given the parties' legal advice and the agreement's clarity, it will weigh heavily / given the children and Wife's career sacrifice since, it will weigh less].
**R** The court has regard to any agreement between the parties on the ownership and division of matrimonial assets made in contemplation of divorce (s 112(2)(e) WC), which covers pre-nuptial agreements; such an agreement cannot detract from the court's ultimate power to divide under s 112 ([73]) and "cannot be enforced, in and of itself" ([77]), and there is no blanket rule that it must be enforced to the exclusion of all other circumstances ([80]), but depending on the circumstances it may carry "significant – indeed, even conclusive – weight" ([86]); for an agreement between foreign nationals, governed by and valid under foreign law, there is "no reason in principle why the court should not accord significant (even critical) weight" to it ([87]), and even a wholly local agreement may carry "significant (even pivotal) weight" ([91]) (*TQ v TR*). The validity of a foreign agreement is governed by its proper law ([32]). Agreements on the custody of children are presumed unenforceable unless clearly shown to be in the child's interests, and agreements on maintenance are subject to the court's overall scrutiny ([67], [70]).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 112(2)(e): any agreement with respect to ownership and division made in contemplation of divorce.
> - *TQ v TR* [2009] SGCA 6 ([2009] 2 SLR(R) 961) (CA):
>   - validity: "The validity of a contract, including marital property agreements, is governed by its proper law" ([32]); the Dutch agreement was valid under Dutch law ([37]); ancillary matters themselves are governed by Singapore law ([42]);
>   - scope: a prenup may cover maintenance, division and children ([45]); this one related only to the division of property ([49]);
>   - maintenance: prenups on maintenance of the wife or children are "subject to the overall scrutiny of the courts" ([67]); post-nuptial maintenance agreements are governed by ss 116 and 119 WC ([57]–[68]);
>   - custody: "a presumption that such agreements are unenforceable unless it is clearly demonstrated" that they are in the child's interests; the court is the final arbiter ([70]);
>   - division: s 112(2)(e) covers prenups "without any strained construction"; an agreement cannot detract from the court's ultimate power under s 112 ([73]); it "cannot be enforced, in and of itself" ([77]); "There is no blanket rule that a prenuptial agreement must be enforced to the exclusion of all the other relevant circumstances" ([80]);
>   - weight: "significant – indeed, even conclusive – weight"; "a factor of magnetic importance" ([86]); "significant (even critical) weight" for a foreign agreement valid under its foreign law, which also avoids forum shopping ([87]); applies mainly to division; "no blanket rule"; fraud or unconscionability may lead the court to disregard it ([88]); a local agreement may carry "significant (even pivotal) weight" ([91]); time and changed circumstances are "an important factor" ([92]); terms on maintenance and custody carry less weight ([93]); contract-law requirements apply except to a valid foreign agreement, subject to a residuary discretion ([94]–[100]); principles summarised at [101]–[105]; terms should be converted into a court order ([106]);
>   - result: the agreement (Dutch and Swedish nationals, notarised in the Netherlands, valid under Dutch law) was given "the highest significance" ([107]–[108]); "the length of time since the making of the Agreement cannot be, in and of itself, a reason for disregarding it" ([109]); no division ordered — each kept what he or she brought in ([5], [28]); no general principle to be drawn from the result ([110]);
>   - the agreement may be given effect by incorporating it into a consent order (FL6 s37).
> - Consent orders (FL6 s38): common, as settlement is encouraged (*Surindar Singh v Sita Jaswant Kaur* [2014] 3 SLR 1284); variation (*AYM v AYL* [2013] 1 SLR 924); setting aside (*AOO v AON* [2011] 4 SLR 1169; *BMI v BMJ* [2018] 1 SLR 43).

**P** In *TQ v TR* a couple signed a pre-nuptial agreement governed by Dutch law; the Court of Appeal held it valid under its proper law ([32], [37]), read it as dealing only with the division of property ([49]), held that it could not be enforced in itself but was a factor under s 112(2)(e) ([73], [77]), and gave it "the highest significance", so that no division was ordered ([5], [28], [107]–[108]).

**A** First, validity: [signed in [country] under its law] → valid under its proper law? [signed in Singapore] → ordinary contract principles. Second, scope: [division only] → s 112(2)(e) factor; [custody terms — "Husband to have the children"] → presumed unenforceable; welfare decides (1-17); [waiver of maintenance] → subject to the court's scrutiny. Third, weight on division: in favour — [both had independent legal advice, full disclosure, sophisticated parties, the agreement is clear, it was meant to govern exactly this situation]; against — [unforeseen change since: children born, Wife gave up her career, the family moved to Singapore; long marriage; homemaking contributions the agreement ignores]; time alone is not a reason to ignore it ([109]), though time and changed circumstances are an important factor ([92]); [fraud, unconscionability or a local agreement without advice] → less weight ([88], [94]–[100]). Fourth, post-nuptial agreement [signed after separation in contemplation of divorce] → squarely within s 112(2)(e); propose recording it in a consent order. **C** Therefore the agreement will be [a weighty / a modest] factor, not binding in itself.

*Source note: FL-SYL [65]–[66] (non-core); FL6 s36–38 (s 112(2)(e); TQ v TR; consent order cases); eLit (TQ v TR [5]–[110]).*

### pcp-w-1-36 — Third-party interests in alleged matrimonial assets (UDA v UDB) (non-core)
*Keywords: third party, property held for parents, house bought with parents, trust for third party, held on trust for the spouses, shadowy figure in the wings, UDA v UDB, jurisdiction of Family Justice Courts, s 112 limited to spouses, no application to any third party, intervene, intervener, stay of s 112 proceedings, separate proceedings, civil court, confirmation and undertaking, drop the claim, decide status without third party, both spouses agree, main or only substantial asset, Option 1, Option 2, Option 3 rejected, brother, company asset*

**I** The issue is how the court deals with [the terrace house registered in the names of Husband and his parents / Husband's claim that the flat in his name is held on trust for his brother]. **C** The Family Justice Courts can divide only as between the spouses: s 112 "has no application to any third party", so the court cannot determine [the parents'] rights or order their interest sold or transferred (*UDA v UDB* [2018] SGCA 20 at [31]–[34]); [their] interest must be resolved in separate proceedings, or, if both spouses agree and it is not the main asset, the court may decide only whether [the asset] is matrimonial, without binding [them] ([56]–[58]).
**R** Section 112 permits the Family Justice Courts to determine division between the divorcing spouses only; it does not confer jurisdiction to determine third parties' rights in alleged matrimonial assets or to affect their interests, and jurisdiction "can only be derived from statute" (*UDA v UDB* at [31]–[34], [48], [66]). A third party actively asserting rights should sue separately and may intervene only to seek a stay, which the court must grant ([54]–[55], [59]). Where the third party is not asserting rights, the spouses may obtain the third party's confirmation and undertaking, sue the third party separately (with a stay), drop the claim, or — only if both agree and the asset is not the main or only substantial asset — ask the court to decide whether it is matrimonial without involving the third party, which does not bind the third party ([56]–[58]).

> **Rule in full**
> - *UDA v UDB* [2018] SGCA 20 ([2018] 1 SLR 1015) (CA):
>   - s 112 "has no application to any third party"; an asset owned by a third party is not covered by s 112(1) ([31]–[32]); s 112 does not let a third party have its claim adjudicated ([34]); procedural rules cannot confer jurisdiction, which "can only be derived from statute" ([48], [66]);
>   - four situations ([51]–[53]): (a) the asset is accepted as matrimonial; (b) it is in a spouse's name and the question is whether its acquisition makes it matrimonial — no third party, s 112 applies fully; (c) it is in a spouse's name but claimed to be held on trust for a third party (the slides' "situation (b)", the "shadowy figure in the wings"); (d) it is in a third party's name but a spouse claims the third party holds it on trust for the spouses (the slides' "situation (a)");
>   - third party actively asserting rights: it should commence separate proceedings; intervention serves only to notify the court and seek a stay; the court "should utilise" a stay (Option 2), which is "not a matter of discretion" ([54]–[55], [59]);
>   - third party legally owns the asset and is not asserting rights, ownership contested ([56]): (a) obtain the third party's binding confirmation and an undertaking to abide by the court's order; (b) separate proceedings against the third party, with the s 112 proceedings stayed; (c) drop the claim that it is matrimonial; (d) ask the court to decide its status without involving the third party, adjusting the division of other assets (the judgment's "Option 1"; the slides' option (iv));
>   - limits on option (d) ([57]): only if both spouses agree; risk of prejudice if separate proceedings later show the third party owns it outright; "would not be viable if the disputed asset is the main or only substantial asset";
>   - shadowy third party not asserting a claim ([58]): option (d) is permissible because no order is sought against the third party, but the decision "will not bind the third party", so the owning spouse may later have to account to it as well; once an order against the third party is sought, separate proceedings are needed;
>   - the High Court's Option 3 (the Family Justice Court deciding the third party's rights itself) is not a lawful option ([65]–[66]); the CA upheld the stay of the s 112 proceedings pending separate proceedings ([18]–[19]).

**P** In *UDA v UDB* the Court of Appeal held that s 112 "has no application to any third party" ([32]), rejected the Family Justice Court deciding the third party's rights itself ([65]–[66]), and upheld a stay of the s 112 proceedings pending separate proceedings ([18]–[19]).

**A** First, identify the situation: [the Ang Mo Kio house in the names of Husband and his parents] — only the spouses' share can be a matrimonial asset; [Husband says the flat in his sole name is held for his brother] — situation (c), a "shadowy figure"; [Wife says the condominium in Husband's company's or mother's name is really theirs] — situation (d). Second, is the third party asserting rights? [the parents have written to claim their share / have sued] → they must sue separately; the s 112 proceedings will be stayed. [not asserting] → options: get [the parents'] written confirmation of their share and an undertaking to abide by the order; sue them separately (stay); drop the claim; or, if both spouses agree and [the house is not the main asset], ask the court to decide [Husband's] share only, warning [Client] that the decision will not bind [the parents]. Third, advise on cost and delay: separate civil proceedings delay the ancillary matters. **C** Therefore only [the spouses' share] can be divided in the s 112 proceedings, and [the parents'] claim must be dealt with by [confirmation and undertaking / separate proceedings].

*Source note: FL-SYL [79] (non-core); FL6 s32–35 (UDA v UDB options); eLit (UDA v UDB [18]–[66]); PYP22-FAM (house bought with Husband's parents).*

### pcp-w-1-37 — Full and frank disclosure and adverse inferences (UZN v UZM; BPC v BPB)
*Keywords: full and frank disclosure, duty to disclose, hidden assets, undisclosed bank account, POSB account, keep silent, conceal, adverse inference, BPC v BPB, UZN v UZM, substratum of evidence, particular access to information, quantification approach, uplift approach, NK v NL, Chan Tin Sun, dissipation, gambling, withdrawals, ethics, mislead the court*

**I** The issue is [whether the court will draw an adverse inference against [Husband], who earned a substantial income but disclosed negligible assets / whether [Client] may stay silent about her undisclosed account]. **C** [Both parties must make full and frank disclosure; the court may draw an adverse inference where there is a prima facie case of non-disclosure and the party had particular access to the information (*BPC v BPB* [2019] 1 SLR 608 at [60]), and will give effect to it by the quantification or uplift approach (*UZN v UZM* [2021] 1 SLR 426). / I must advise [Client] that she must disclose the account; I cannot help her conceal it.]
**R** Both parties are under a duty to make full and frank disclosure of their means and assets (*USB v USA* [2020] 2 SLR 588). An adverse inference may be drawn where there is a substratum of evidence establishing a prima facie case against the person, and that person had particular access to the information said to be hidden (*BPC v BPB* at [60]; *UZN v UZM*). The court may then quantify the undisclosed assets and add them to the pool, or award a higher proportion of the known assets to the other party (*UZN v UZM* at [27]–[39]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Duty of full and frank disclosure (FL6 s8, s27); failure → adverse inference.
> - Conditions (*BPC v BPB* [60]): (a) a substratum of evidence establishing a prima facie case against the person; (b) that person had some particular access to the information he or she is said to be hiding.
> - Caution: parties in a functioning marriage do not always keep fastidious records (*UBM v UBN* [2017] 4 SLR 921 at [15]).
> - *UZN v UZM*: a spouse with substantial income over years but negligible assets on divorce must explain the discrepancy; there was a basis to examine his cash flow.
> - Two approaches (*UZN v UZM*, citing *BPC* [64], *Chan Tin Sun* [64], *Yeo Chong Lin* [65], *NK v NL* [61]–[62]):
>   - quantification: find the value of undisclosed assets on the evidence and include it in the pool (unless shown unreasonable) — e.g. a specific undisclosed property or sum, even if value is imprecise;
>   - uplift: order a higher proportion of the known assets to the other party — to eliminate the effects of non-disclosure.
> - The court adopts the approach that best achieves a just and equitable division of the true gains of the marriage.
> - Ethics (PCR-Text): the practitioner must inform the client of the duty to be truthful to the court and comply with legal requirements (r 10(2)); must, so far as able, prevent the client from suppressing evidence and not assist in it (r 10(3)(a)); and must not knowingly assist or permit the client to mislead the court (r 10(6)(a)); if the client has given false evidence, cease to act or not perpetuate it (r 10(4)).

**P** In *NK v NL* the Court of Appeal applied an uplift because it could not tell how much of the $2.7 million in undisclosed cash had been spent rather than concealed (Cyril-Exam §6, Adverse Inferences).

**A** First, if [Husband] is the non-discloser: [he earned over $25,000 a month for years and withdrew over $1 million from the joint account without explanation, yet discloses little] — a prima facie case, and he alone knows what happened to the money (*BPC* [60]). Apply quantification where a specific sum can be identified ([the $1m withdrawn — add back]), otherwise an uplift. Seek disclosure orders (Part 9 FJ(G)R) first. Second, if [Client] wants to hide [a $240,000 POSB account]: advise that disclosure is mandatory; concealment risks an adverse inference that may cost her more than the account, undermines her credibility, and I cannot act on an instruction to conceal it from the court. **C** Therefore [the court is likely to draw an adverse inference against Husband by quantifying the $1m / Client must disclose the account].

*Source note: FL-SYL [80]; FL6 s8, s27–31 (USB v USA; UZN v UZM; BPC v BPB [60], [64]; UBM v UBN [15]; two approaches); Cyril-Exam §6 (NK v NL uplift example — secondary); PYP21-FAM (S$1m withdrawals), PCR-Text r 10(2)–(4), (6); PYP22-FAM (Cherry's POSB account; margin "Duty to disclose").*

## 1.VIII Protection against family violence

- **Can my client apply for a PPO? Against whom? For the child?**
  - Respondent must be a "family member" (spouse or ex-spouse, child, parent, in-law, sibling, other relative or incapacitated person) (s 58A); boyfriends and cohabitees are not → [1-38](#pcp-w-1-38)
  - Who applies: adult victim himself; for a child under 18, a family member, guardian or carer; protector (s 60) → [1-38](#pcp-w-1-38)
- **Will the PPO be granted?**
  - Balance of probabilities: family violence committed or likely, and order necessary (s 60A) → [1-39](#pcp-w-1-39)
  - Slap, thrown cutlery, a spoon that hit the child; vase thrown at the wall that scarred her; "accident" — physical abuse? emotional abuse? → [1-39](#pcp-w-1-39)
  - Self-defence; lawful correction of a child (*TCV v TCU*) → [1-39](#pcp-w-1-39)
  - One-off, out of character, parties now apart: necessity? → [1-39](#pcp-w-1-39)
- **Urgent or additional orders**
  - Expedited order (28 days); domestic exclusion; stay away; no contact; counselling; MTO; electronic monitoring; removal → [1-40](#pcp-w-1-40)
- **Breach; varying the order; procedure; effect on divorce and custody** → [1-41](#pcp-w-1-41)

### pcp-w-1-38 — Who may seek protection: Part 7 WC, "family member" (s 58A) and the applicant (s 60)
*Keywords: personal protection order, PPO, protection order, Part 7 WC, new Part 7, 2 January 2025, family member, s 58A WC, spouse, former spouse, child, step-child, parent, in-law, sibling, relative, incapacitated person, who may apply, s 60 WC, protector, child below 18, guardian, lack mental capacity, Muslims, boyfriend, cohabitee, POHA*

**I** The issue is whether [Ryu] may apply for a protection order against [Chun Li] for himself and [their child]. **C** Yes: [Chun Li] is a family member of both — [Ryu]'s spouse and the child's mother (s 58A WC); [Ryu] may apply for himself (s 60(2)(c)) and, as a family member of a child below 18, for the child (s 60(2)(a)).
**R** Part 7 of the Women's Charter (new Part 7 in force 2 January 2025) applies to all persons in Singapore, including Muslims. A family member of a person is a spouse or former spouse, a child (including adopted and step-children), a parent, a parent-in-law, a sibling, or any other relative or incapacitated person whom the court considers should be regarded as a family member (s 58A). An application may be made by a protector, or, for a person below 18, by a family member, guardian or carer; for a person lacking capacity, by a family member, donee or deputy, or carer; otherwise by the person himself (s 60).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 58A "family member": (a) spouse or former spouse; (b) child, including adopted child and step-child; (c) father or mother; (d) father-in-law or mother-in-law; (e) brother or sister; (f) any other relative, or an incapacitated person, who in the court's opinion should in the circumstances be regarded as a member of the family.
> - s 60:
>   - (1) a protector (appointed under s 65(2)(a));
>   - (2)(a) if Y is below 18: (i) a family member of Y, (ii) Y's guardian, (iii) a person responsible for caring for Y;
>   - (2)(b) if Y is assessed by a medical practitioner, psychiatrist or psychologist to lack capacity to apply: (i) a family member, (ii) donee or deputy, (iii) carer;
>   - (2)(c) in any other case, Y;
>   - (3) a person below 18 may not apply under (2) unless married or previously married.
> - Scope (FL7 s4): all persons including Muslims; complements the Penal Code, POHA, the Children and Young Persons Act and torts; purpose — protect the family while maintaining harmony.
> - Cyril: de facto partners (boyfriends, girlfriends, cohabitees) are not family members; alternatives are police reports, POHA, torts.

**P** Past papers before 2025 refer to old s 65; the current provisions are ss 58A, 58B, 60 and 60A (FL7 s2).

**A** First, relationship: [Chun Li is Ryu's wife and the child's mother] — family member under s 58A(a) and (c)/(b). Second, applicant: [Ryu, an adult, applies for himself (s 60(2)(c)); as the child's father, a family member of a child under 18, he may apply for the child (s 60(2)(a)(i))]. [If the abuser were a boyfriend, Part 7 would not apply — consider POHA.] **C** Therefore [Ryu] has standing to apply for a protection order for himself and the child.

*Source note: FL-SYL [86]–[88], [90]; FL7 s2–6 (new Part 7; s 58A; s 60); Cyril-Exam §7 Step 1 (de facto partners; alternatives — secondary); PYP25 Q4, PYP23-FAM Q4.2, PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-39 — The two-stage test for a protection order: family violence and necessity (ss 58B, 60A WC)
*Keywords: s 60A WC, balance of probabilities, family violence committed or likely, necessary for protection, s 58B, physical abuse, sexual abuse, emotional or psychological abuse, single instance, course of conduct, perceived by family member, child saw it, self-defence, lawful correction, TCV v TCU, accident, out of character, slap, threw cutlery, spoon hit child, vase, scar, still living together, remorse, likelihood of success*

**I** The issue is whether [Ryu]'s application for a protection order against [Chun Li] is likely to succeed. **C** The court must be satisfied on a balance of probabilities that family violence has been committed or is likely to be committed against [Ryu / the child], and that the order is necessary for their protection (s 60A WC); [the slaps are physical abuse of Ryu, but given that the incident was isolated, out of character and provoked, and the parties are separating, the court may find an order unnecessary / likely grant it because…].
**R** The court may make a protection order if satisfied on a balance of probabilities that family violence has been committed or is likely to be committed against the family member, and that the order is necessary for his protection (s 60A WC). Family violence is physical, sexual, or emotional or psychological abuse by a family member, which may be a single instance or a course of conduct, and need not be directed at the person if it can be perceived by him (s 58B). It excludes force lawfully used in self-defence or by way of correction of a child below 18 (s 58B(2)(c); *TCV v TCU* [2015] SGFC 3).

> **Rule in full**
> - s 60A: on a balance of probabilities, (1) family violence has been committed or is likely to be committed against the family member; and (2) it is necessary for the protection of the family member that the order be made.
> - s 58B(1): X commits family violence against Y if (a) X is Y's family member and (b) X commits physical, sexual, or emotional or psychological abuse against Y.
>   - physical abuse (Cyril, from s 58B(2)): conduct that causes or threatens personal injury or physical pain, or threatens death or injury; includes wrongful confinement or restraint;
>   - sexual abuse (s 58B(3)): coercing or attempting to coerce sexual activity;
>   - emotional or psychological abuse (s 58B(4)): conduct that torments, intimidates, harasses or distresses, or causes or may reasonably be expected to cause mental harm (including thoughts of suicide or self-harm);
>   - s 58B(5): a single instance or a course of conduct;
>   - s 58B(6): need not be directed at the family member but must be capable of being perceived by him.
> - s 58B(2)(c): excludes force lawfully used (i) in self-defence, or (ii) by way of correction of a child below 18; "lawful correction" — for discipline, with good sense, for the child's benefit (*TCV v TCU*).
> - Necessity factors (FL7 s10): whether the parties still live together or are likely to be in contact; the original cause of conflict and whether resolved; severity of the incident; the respondent's perspective on the violence.
> - Cyril adds: once family violence is proved, an order is prima facie necessary unless other factors show otherwise; a stand-alone incident weighs against; whether the respondent can still use violence; an objectively observable danger, though not necessarily immediate.

**P** The 2026 sample answer guide and past papers treat the question as two-stage: first characterise the conduct under s 58B, then weigh necessity on the parties' present circumstances (FL7 s7–10; PYP23-FAM Q4.2, PYP25 Q4).

**A** First, family violence: [Chun Li slapped Ryu twice — physical abuse (pain); she threw cutlery and a spoon ricocheted and bruised the child's cheek — physical injury to the child, and the quarrel was perceived by the child (s 58B(6))]. [John threw a vase at the wall in frustration; a shard scarred Jenny — he says it was an accident, but throwing the vase in a quarrel was conduct that caused injury and intimidated her; arguably physical and emotional abuse.] Second, exceptions: [not self-defence; not lawful correction]. Third, necessity: [a single incident, out of character, provoked by discovering the affair; she is remorseful; the parties are separating] → weighs against; [but they share the home until the divorce, conflict over the affair is unresolved, and there is contact through the child] → weighs for. Fourth, consequences for the divorce: a PPO is relevant to the children's welfare but not decisive on care and control (1-19). **C** Therefore the application [is likely / unlikely] to succeed for [Ryu] and [the child]; I would advise [Chun Li] to [offer an undertaking or attend counselling, and present her remorse and the isolated nature of the incident].

*Source note: FL-SYL [89], [91]–[92]; FL7 s7–11 (s 60A; s 58B(1), (5)–(6); s 58B(2)(c); TCV v TCU; necessity factors); Cyril-Exam §7 Steps 2.1–2.2 (abuse definitions; necessity gloss; TQY v TQX, LP v LQ, McCartney — secondary); PYP23-FAM Q4.2, PYP25 Q4, PYP21-FAM, PYP22-FAM.*

### pcp-w-1-40 — Other orders: expedited order, exclusion, stay away, no contact, counselling, MTO, monitoring, removal (ss 60B–61A WC)
*Keywords: expedited order, EO, s 61 WC, 28 days, danger pending application, domestic exclusion order, DEO, s 60B(2)(a), exclusive occupation, s 61A, stay away order, no contact order, s 60B, counselling order, s 60E, 36 months, mandatory treatment order, s 60F, psychiatric condition, electronic monitoring order, s 60C, removal order, s 60D, care order, supervision order, protector*

**I** The issue is what further or urgent orders [Client] can seek to protect herself [and the children]. **C** [Client] can seek an expedited order pending the hearing because there is a danger of further violence (s 61 WC), together with a domestic exclusion order excluding [Husband] from the home (ss 60B, 61A), a stay away or no contact order, and a counselling order (s 60E).
**R** An expedited order is a protection order made while the application is pending, where there is a danger of family violence in the meantime; it takes effect on service and expires after 28 days unless extended (s 61(2)). The court may also make a domestic exclusion order granting exclusive occupation of the home regardless of title (s 60B(2)(a)), stay away and no contact orders (s 60B), and counselling orders (s 60E); and, in prescribed cases, mandatory treatment orders (s 60F), electronic monitoring orders (s 60C) and removal orders (s 60D).

> **Rule in full**
> - Protection order (s 60A): restrains the respondent from committing family violence; may prohibit inciting or assisting others; indefinite or for a period.
> - Expedited order (s 61(2)): made while the application is pending, usually when the summons is issued; effect on service or later date; expires after 28 days, extendable; court must be satisfied of a danger of family violence while the application is pending.
> - Domestic exclusion order (s 60B(2)(a)): excludes X from Y's home (whole or part) and gives Y exclusive occupation, regardless of title (without otherwise affecting title); on the court's initiative or application (s 60B(3)); may accompany an expedited order (s 61A).
> - Stay away order (s 60B(b)) and no contact order (s 60B(c)); may accompany an expedited order (s 61A(2)).
> - Counselling order (s 60E): for X, Y or both, or their children; other programmes (parenting, caregiver training, family therapy); up to 36 months; survives revocation or expiry of the PPO.
> - Mandatory treatment order (s 60F): up to 36 months; reasonable grounds that X has a psychiatric condition likely contributing to the conduct; preliminary and formal assessment reports; made only if the report certifies the condition, contribution, susceptibility to treatment and suitability.
> - Electronic monitoring order (s 60C): only on a protector's application; necessary for Y's safety; high-risk, exceptional cases.
> - Removal order (s 60D): only on a protector's application; last resort; must be accompanied by a care order and supervision order; may prohibit Y returning home or contacting X.
> - Protector (FL7 s28): appointed by the Director-General of Social Welfare; assesses risk, enters premises, obtains information, makes emergency orders where danger is imminent, applies for protection orders.

**P** An expedited order requires only a danger of family violence while the main application is pending, so it suits cases where the victim still lives with the respondent (FL7 s12).

**A** First, urgency: [Husband assaulted Client on 29 June and lives in the flat] → apply for an expedited order with the PPO application, and a DEO to exclude him from the flat regardless of his ownership. Second, ongoing protection: stay away (from her workplace) and no contact orders. Third, therapeutic: a counselling order for both and [the children]; [if his violence is linked to a psychiatric condition, ask the court to consider an MTO assessment]. **C** Therefore [Client] should seek [an expedited order, DEO and counselling order] at filing.

*Source note: FL-SYL [93]–[95]; FL7 s11–18, s28 (ss 60A, 61(2), 60B(2)(a)–(c), (3), 61A, 60E, 60F, 60C, 60D; protector).*

### pcp-w-1-41 — Breach, variation and procedure for protection orders (s 63C WC; FJ(G)R)
*Keywords: breach of PPO, family violence offence, s 63C WC, fine $10,000, 12 months, 18 months aggravated, arrestable offence, s 63D, beyond reasonable doubt, counselling order breach $2,000, vary, suspend, revoke, s 60A(4), s 60A(5), change of circumstances, procedure, quasi-criminal, s 179A(4), iFAMS, Family Protection Centre, Court Family Specialist, duty judge, summons, mention, trial in camera, Teng Cheng Sin, child evidence*

**I** The issue is [what happens if [Respondent] breaches the order / how [Client] applies / whether the order can be varied]. **C** [Knowingly breaching a protection order is a family violence offence punishable by a fine of up to $10,000 or imprisonment of up to 12 months (18 if aggravated) (s 63C WC), and is arrestable / The application is filed as a complaint in the prescribed form, confirmed before a duty judge, and heard as a quasi-criminal proceeding / The court may vary, suspend or revoke the order on a change of circumstances (s 60A(4)–(5)).]
**R** A person who knowingly contravenes a protection order, expedited order, exclusion, stay away, no contact or electronic monitoring order commits a family violence offence punishable under s 63C WC, proved beyond reasonable doubt; it is an arrestable offence (s 63D). Unreasonable breach of a counselling order or MTO is an offence with a fine of up to $2,000 (ss 60E(8), (10), 60F(30)). The court may vary, suspend or revoke orders, considering any change of circumstances and any counsellor's or psychiatrist's report (s 60A(4)–(5)). Applications are quasi-criminal, made like a summons under the CPC (s 179A(4) WC; FJ(G)R).

> **Rule in full**
> - Breach: s 60A(6), s 60B(9), s 60C(9), s 61(11), s 61A(10) read with s 63C — knowing contravention is a "family violence offence".
>   - s 63C(1): aggravated — fine up to $10,000, imprisonment up to 18 months, or both; otherwise — fine up to $10,000, imprisonment up to 12 months, or both.
>   - Criminal standard (beyond reasonable doubt); arrestable (s 63D); new enforcement powers (Division 7).
> - Counselling order breach without reasonable excuse (s 60E(8)): fine up to $2,000 (s 60E(10)); MTO breach (s 60F(30)): fine up to $2,000.
> - Variation, suspension, revocation: s 60A(4), s 60B(7), s 60C(7), s 60D(10), s 60E(6), s 60F(24), s 61(7), s 61A(8); MTO only on the psychiatrist's application with report (s 60F(27)); s 60A(5): consider change of circumstances and any counsellor's or psychiatrist's report; necessity.
> - Procedure (FL7 s23–27):
>   - quasi-criminal; application in the same manner as a summons under the CPC (s 179A(4) WC; FJ(G)R; FJC PD);
>   - written complaint in the prescribed form online (iFAMS), then at the Family Protection Centre, a Protection Specialist Centre or PAVE, or in person;
>   - form: particulars of parties; particulars of family violence (latest and past incidents);
>   - meeting with a Court Family Specialist; duty judge confirms the truth of the contents; may dismiss, issue a summons, or make an expedited order;
>   - service (FJ(G)R P 3 r 9(2)); both attend mentions — if the complainant is absent, struck off; if the respondent is absent, heard in absence or warrant of arrest; withdrawal; admission;
>   - mentions: affidavits, striking out, witnesses, discovery, experts, related proceedings (including Syariah Court), trial dates;
>   - trial: in camera; complainant and witnesses, then respondent and witnesses; incidents after filing not considered (*Teng Cheng Sin v Law Fay Yuen* [2003] 3 SLR(R) 356); children's evidence with care (*QU v QV* [2008] SGDC 364, applying *Lee Kwang Peng v PP* [1997] 2 SLR(R) 569).

**P** In *Teng Cheng Sin* the High Court held that disputed incidents occurring after the application was filed are not considered at the trial of that application (FL7 s27).

**A** First, for breach: [Husband called and turned up at her workplace despite a no contact order] → report to the police; a family violence offence, arrestable, punishable under s 63C. Second, for the application: file in the prescribed form with all incidents, attend the CFS meeting and the duty judge, seek an expedited order. Third, at trial: confine evidence to incidents before filing; prepare [the child]'s evidence carefully if needed. Fourth, for variation: [the parties have reconciled and completed counselling] → apply to vary or revoke under s 60A(4)–(5). **C** Therefore [Client] should [report the breach / file the complaint / apply to vary] as set out.

*Source note: FL7 s19–28 (breach provisions; s 63C(1); s 63D; ss 60E(8), (10), 60F(30); variation provisions; s 60A(5); procedure; Teng Cheng Sin; QU v QV; Lee Kwang Peng).*
